--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page is the formal dedication of Sebastiano Serlio’s Fourth Book on Architecture (1540 edition). It follows the title page and addresses Alfonso d’Avalos, a powerful military commander and diplomat under Emperor Charles V. Serlio is using this space to justify the reprinting of his work by praising the system of artistic patronage that allowed great Renaissance masters to flourish.</meta>

# TO THE MOST ILLUSTRIOUS AND EXCELLENT LORD, LORD ALFONSO D’AVALOS, GREAT MARQUIS OF VASTO, AND LIEUTENANT GENERAL OF HIS IMPERIAL MAESTY IN ITALY,
## SEBASTIANO SERLIO OF BOLOGNA+

If all Princes and great personages possessed that beauty of soul which your Excellency has, one could hope that our century—endowed as it is with the most beautiful talents in every laudable profession, though poorly rewarded—might return to that height of greatness found in the good times of the ancient Romans. Indeed, it would be possible that in some respects ancient things might be surpassed by our modern ones; for it is easier to add to an invention than to discover it anew, as those ancient inventors did in many things. These things (as I have said) could be increased—if not in scale, at least in understanding—with better art. This "good art" began to fail and decline little by little with the fall of the Roman Empire. Now, it seems that the benevolence of the Heavens is once again granting it to us, provided that the greedy audacity of our times does not also lock away the treasures of liberality with so many hard keys; for when rewards are lacking, the works of ingenious men also fail. <margin>The accursed hunger for gold <note>original: "auri sacra fames." A famous phrase from Virgil’s *Aeneid* (3.57) referring to how the pursuit of wealth can lead to the abandonment of virtue and art.</note></margin> And to see the truth of this, let us consider for a moment, since we are speaking of our own times: if **Bramante** <note>Donato Bramante (1444–1514), the architect who initiated the High Renaissance style in Rome and designed the initial plans for St. Peter's Basilica.</note>, the reviver of good Architecture which had been buried from antiquity until his time, had not found **Pope Julius II**, whose greatness of soul matched Bramante's own desires, he would not have been able to create the works he did in Rome. And if the great **Michelangelo Buonarroti** had not been supported by the noble **House of Medici**, and later well-rewarded by the aforementioned Pope and others as well, perhaps he would not have produced as many stupendous works of painting and sculpture as he has. And if the virtuous **Duchess Isabella of Urbino** <note>Likely referring to Elisabetta Gonzaga, Duchess of Urbino, though Serlio may be conflating names with Isabella d'Este; both were significant patrons of the arts.</note> had not first raised up and promoted the divine **Raphael** in his youth, followed by Julius II (who was a great rewarder of his) and finally **Leo X**, the father and protector of all the fine arts and all good practitioners, certainly he would not have been able to raise painting to that splendor where he led it, nor would he have left behind so many admirable works of Painting and Architecture as are seen today. And if **Giulio Romano**, the true pupil and certain heir of this same divine Raphael of Urbino, had not found a place with the most liberal **Duke of Mantua** <note>Federico II Gonzaga, for whom Giulio Romano built the famous Palazzo Te.</note>, who is such a friend to Painting and Architecture, how could he have created the infinite and beautiful things of Architecture and Painting that are seen in many places within and outside the noble city of Mantua? If **Girolamo Genga** did not have as his master the Lord **Duke of Urbino, Francesco Maria**, who is as knowledgeable in Architecture and Painting as he is in military matters and many other noble arts, he would never have created the masterful works of Architecture he has made to please his Lord. In short, if the great **Titian**—the example, father, and master of painting in our times—had not first had as a great rewarder **Alfonso d'Este, Duke of Ferrara** <margin>+</margin> (who made him a Knight with most ample gifts), and then the very liberal **Federico of Mantua** (for whom he has made and makes every day many works), besides many other Lords and Cardinals, and above all **Emperor Charles V** (who was portrayed by him and recognized his great virtue with large and honored gifts and a new title of knighthood); and finally your **Excellency**, who holds him in protection with such kindness: I cannot believe he would have ever reached such heights in his work as is seen in him. But to return to my original purpose, I wish to say this: that since your Excellency has come here to **Venice** representing the person of His Imperial Majesty, and having not only negotiated most prudently with this illustrious Senate for the common benefit of all Christendom, but having also performed many other pious works truly worthy of great merit before God and the highest praise among men, and having shown great courtesy to various people who labor in the fine arts (of which I am in large part a faithful witness, being the least among many); to whom—because I presented this current volume, already published to the world but not yet dedicated to your Excellency—you have shown such liberal courtesy, accompanied by a most gracious face and kind, loving words. This courtesy was not one of mere promises or vain hope, but of deeds and a good sum of *scudi* <note>A significant unit of currency (gold or silver coins) in 16th-century Italy.</note>. In this way, you bound me to a strict obligation toward you: for although (moved by just cause) I had decided within myself not to reprint these *Rules* of mine anymore, even though no more copies were to be found for sale because they were all sold out and desired by many; through the good fortune they have had with your Excel-

<summary>In this dedication, Serlio argues that great art requires great patrons, citing masters like Michelangelo, Raphael, and Titian as evidence. He thanks Alfonso d’Avalos for the financial support that convinced him to reprint this architectural treatise.</summary>
<keywords>patronage, Renaissance artists, Bramante, Michelangelo, Raphael, Titian, Alfonso d’Avalos, Venice, architecture, reprint, scudi, Charles V</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to Alfonso d’Avalos. Serlio is explaining his decision to reissue the work in a high-quality format to protect the integrity of his architectural drawings against inferior, unauthorized copies.</meta>

...cellency, I have wished—to honor you and for the common benefit, and with your help—to reprint them once more. I have done so regardless of the fact that some, moved by a greed for profit, have sought to reprint them in a smaller format without any respect for either the proportions or the measurements of my illustrations. These figures require this size, or even larger, so that they may be understood by those who must put them into practice; and because of this importance, the size of the figures in the other books will be larger. 

With this new printing, besides many corrections, I have included several additions in many places, such as in the **Doric** frieze on page 21; in the **Doric** door according to **Vitruvius** <note>Marcus Vitruvius Pollio (c. 80–15 BC), the Roman author of the only major ancient Greek/Roman treatise on architecture to survive, which became the "bible" for Renaissance architects.</note> on page 24; in the treatise on the **Ionic** base on page 18; and in the volute of the **Ionic** capital on page 37, which is of great importance. I have also made additions to the **Corinthian** base on page 47 and the **Corinthian** capital in the same place. Furthermore, in the **Composite** order, the **fluting** <term>Strie: the vertical grooves or channels carved into the shaft of a column or, in this case, a decorative element.</term> has been added beneath the capital featuring the **Horse** monsters <note>Serlio is likely referring to "hippocampus" or "centaur" motifs used in the highly decorative Composite style.</note>, which were previously missing. 

Therefore, those who henceforth benefit from these labors of mine—whatever they may be—will be indebted to your **Excellency**, and not to me. Thus the **World** shall know where noble courtesy holds its primary seat—with due honor to all other princes. May your **Excellency** deign to accept this good will of mine; to whom I humbly offer my reverence, praying to the most high **God** that He may always preserve you in long and prosperous happiness.

<summary>Serlio justifies this new edition by criticizing low-quality pirated versions that ruin architectural proportions and lists specific technical updates made to the five orders of architecture. He concludes the dedication by attributing the work's success to his patron, Alfonso d’Avalos.</summary>
<keywords>architectural printing, copyright, scale and proportion, Doric, Ionic, Corinthian, Composite order, Vitruvius, Alfonso d’Avalos, architectural ornaments</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the formal dedication to Alfonso d’Avalos into the author’s direct address to his readers. Serlio explains the pedagogical structure of his entire seven-book treatise, noting that although this is the fourth book, he is publishing it first because of its practical importance regarding the Five Orders of architecture.</meta>

->### THE AUTHOR TO THE READERS.<-

Kind reader, having prepared several rules on Architecture, I presuppose that not only elevated geniuses will understand them, but that every person of average ability may also be capable of doing so, according to whether they are more or less inclined toward such an art. These rules are divided into seven books, as will be noted below. Since the subject allows for it, I have chosen to begin by publishing this **Fourth Book**, as it is more relevant and more necessary than the others for the knowledge of the different styles of buildings and their ornaments. For everything you find in this book that pleases you, do not give praise to me, but rather to my teacher **Baldassarre Peruzzi of Siena** <note>Baldassarre Peruzzi (1481–1536) was a leading Sienese architect and painter who influenced the High Renaissance style; Serlio was his pupil and inherited many of his drawings.</note>. He was not only most learned in this art through both theory and practice, but he was also quite courteous and generous, teaching it to anyone who took delight in it—and especially to me. Whatever I know, I acknowledge it all to his kindness, and following his example, I intend to use it as well with those who do not disdain to learn it from me. I do this so that everyone may have some knowledge of this art, which is no less delightful to the soul when planning what is to be done than it is to the eyes when it is finished. This art, through the virtue of the famous and excellent minds I have mentioned, flourishes in this century of ours just as the Latin language did in the time of **Julius Caesar** and **Cicero**. Take up then, with a cheerful and noble spirit, if not the finished effect, then at least the will—which I have certainly had in great measure—to satisfy you in this labor. And where you recognize that my weak strength could not support such a weight, pray that those more robust than I may sustain it for me and supply that which I have lacked.

**I**n the **First Book**, I will treat the principles of **Geometry** and the various intersections of lines, so that the Architect may give a good account of everything he does.

**I**n the **Second**, I will demonstrate in drawings and in words so much of **Perspective** that, should he wish, he may reveal his concept in a visible drawing.

**I**n the **Third**, one will see **Ichnography** <term>Ichnography: from the Greek *ichnos* (track/footprint), meaning a ground plan.</term>, which is the plan; **Orthography** <term>Orthography: from the Greek *orthos* (straight), meaning a vertical elevation or upright drawing.</term>, which is the upright; and **Sciography** <term>Sciography: technically the study of shadows, but here Serlio defines it as "lo Scórcio" or foreshortening/perspective views.</term>, which means the foreshortening of the majority of the buildings that are in **Rome**, in **Italy**, and abroad, diligently measured, with their locations and names written down.

**I**n the **Fourth**, which is this one, we will treat the five manners of building and their ornaments: **Tuscan**, **Doric**, **Ionic**, **Corinthian**, and **Composite**; and with these, almost the entire art is embraced through the knowledge of these diverse things.

**I**n the **Fifth**, I will speak of the many ways of **Temples** <note>In the Renaissance, "temples" (tempij) frequently referred to churches or sacred buildings modeled on classical forms.</note> designed in various forms: round, square, six-sided, eight-sided, oval, and in the shape of a cross, with their plans, elevations, and foreshortened views diligently measured.

**I**n the **Sixth**, we will speak of all the **Habitations** that can be used today: beginning from the humblest little hut or cabin, and following by degrees up to the most decorated princely palace, for the countryside as well as for the city.

**I**n the **Seventh** and last, we will finish with the many accidents that can occur to the Architect in different places and strange shapes of sites; and in the restorations or restitutions of houses; and how we must act to make use of other buildings and similar things that are, or have been in use at another time.

**N**ow, to better proceed in a reasonable manner, I will begin with the most solid and least ornamented order: that is, the **Tuscan**, which is the most rustic and the strongest, and of the least subtlety and slenderness.

**T**he ancients dedicated buildings to the Gods, adapting them according to their nature, whether robust or delicate: such as the **Doric** work to **Jove** <note>Jupiter/Zeus</note>, to **Mars**, and to **Hercules**—taking these Doric forms from the male figure. They dedicated the **Ionic** to **Diana**, to **Apollo**, and to **Bacchus**, taking the work from the matronly form, which shares in both the robust and the delicate. **Diana**, by her feminine nature, is tender; but through the exercise of the hunt, she is robust. Likewise, **Apollo** for his beauty is soft, yet he is robust because he is a man; I say the same of **Bacchus**. But the **Corinthian** manner, taken from the virginal form, they wished to be dedicated to the Goddess **Vesta**, the patroness of Virgins. But in these modern times, it seems to me we should proceed in another way, without deviating from the ancients: I mean that, following our Christian customs, I will dedicate (as far as I am able) sacred buildings according to their species to **God** and his **Saints**; and profane buildings, both public and private, I will give to men according to their state and their professions. They say, then, that the work...

<summary>Sebastiano Serlio introduces his seven-book architectural treatise, crediting his mentor Baldassarre Peruzzi and outlining the contents of each book. He explains his pedagogical approach to the Five Orders, choosing to publish Book IV first due to its practical necessity.</summary>
<keywords>Sebastiano Serlio, Baldassarre Peruzzi, Five Orders of Architecture, Tuscan, Doric, Ionic, Corinthian, Composite, Ichnography, Orthography, Sciography, architectural theory, Renaissance architecture</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues Serlio’s introduction to his "Fourth Book" on the Five Orders of architecture. He transitions from his dedication to a technical discussion on the appropriate use of the Tuscan order and explains the pedagogical layout of the chapters to follow.</meta>

The **Tuscan** order, in my opinion, is best suited for fortresses: such as city gates, strongholds, castles, and places for storing treasures, or where munitions and artillery are kept; it is also appropriate for prisons, seaports, and other similar structures used in war. It is true that **rustic work** <term>opera rustica: masonry made of large, roughly textured blocks with deep joints, intended to look "natural" or "unrefined"</term>—that is, various joints of roughly sketched stones—has sometimes been mixed by the ancients into the Doric, and occasionally even into the Ionic and Corinthian orders (some of which were even made with a certain delicacy to please the sculptors). Nevertheless, because the Tuscan order is truly the coarsest and least adorned of them all, it seems to me that rustic work is more appropriate and more in keeping with the Tuscan than with any other style. 

This principle was clearly observed by the **Tuscans** themselves, both within their greatest and principal city, **Florence** <note>original: "Fiorenza"</note>, and outside it in country villas. These contain as many beautiful buildings and rich structures made of rustic work as can be seen in the rest of **Christendom**, yet they are mixed with that balance of ruggedness and delicacy that pleased the architects. For this reason, I say such works are more suited to the Tuscan order than to any other species. Therefore, by gathering some examples from the ancients and others from our own time, I will demonstrate various ways these works can be used to make gates for cities and fortresses, as well as for public and private places, facades, loggias, porticos, windows, niches, bridges, aqueducts, and other various ornaments that a good architect might need to create. One could also—without departing from what the ancients did—mix and combine that rustic work with the Doric, and even with the Ionic, and sometimes with the Corinthian, according to the whim of someone who wants to satisfy a personal caprice. This, however, should be called a matter of **license** rather than **reason** <note>In Renaissance theory, "reason" refers to the strict mathematical rules of the ancients, while "license" refers to creative departures from those rules.</note>; for the architect must proceed with great modesty and restraint, especially in public and serious works, where it is praiseworthy to maintain proper decorum.

I wanted to imitate the ancient Comedians at the beginning of this book: some of whom, when wanting to perform a comedy, sent a messenger ahead to give the spectators a brief summary of everything the comedy would treat. Because I must treat the five styles of buildings in this volume—namely the **Tuscan**, **Doric**, **Ionic**, **Corinthian**, and **Composite**—it seemed right to me that the figures of every species be seen at the beginning. And although not all proportions and measurements are noted on the columns and their ornaments, but only the principal ones as a general rule, the details will not be missing in their proper places, where everything will be noted more minutely. This is only (as I have said) to demonstrate a general rule at a single glance. To be better understood by everyone, I will use the vocabulary of **Vitruvius** <note>Vitruvius (c. 80–15 BC) was the author of "De architectura," the only surviving major architectural treatise from antiquity.</note> at the start of each order, accompanied by the modern terms used commonly throughout Italy.

First, regarding the Tuscan **pedestal**, I say the **die** <term>il netto: the central, flat block of a pedestal between the base and the cap</term> shall be a perfect square. The Doric pedestal shall be taller than a square by the length of a line drawn from corner to corner of a perfect square and stood upright <note>This describes a proportion of 1 to the square root of 2 (roughly 1:1.41).</note>. The Ionic pedestal shall be a square and a half. The Corinthian pedestal shall be one square and two-thirds of that square. The Composite pedestal shall be made of two perfect squares; all of this refers to the "die" without its bases and caps. Do not be surprised if the following chapter is labeled as the **fifth**, even though others might expect it to be the first: this is because the first book on **Geometry** will occupy one chapter; the second on **Perspective** will occupy two; and the third on **Antiquities** will occupy one—making four in total. Therefore, for this reason, the following chapter will be the fifth.

Tuscan, Doric, Ionic, Corinthian, Composite, Vitruvius, Florence, Geometry, Perspective, Antiquities, pedestal, Architect, rustic work

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the five orders of architecture introduced on page 111, moving from the general overview of proportions to the specific rules for the Tuscan Order.</meta>

# ON THE TUSCAN ORDER, AND ITS ORNAMENTS: CHAPTER V

It is found in the writings of Vitruvius <note>Marcus Vitruvius Pollio, the Roman author of 'De Architectura,' whose work was the foundation for Renaissance building theory.</note> in Book 4, Chapter 7, that the Tuscan column should be seven parts high, including its base and capital, taking this measure from its thickness at the bottom. The height of the base should be half the <term>diameter<note>original: "groſſezza"; the text says "half the column," but contextually refers to the thickness or diameter of the shaft.</note></term> of the column; and divided into two parts, one shall be for the <term>plinth<note>original: "zocco"; the square or circular block forming the lowest part of the base.</note></term>, and the other part shall be divided into thirds, where two parts are given to the <term>torus<note>original: "baſtone"; a large, rounded molding.</note></term> and the remaining part shall be for the <term>fillet<note>original: "cinta"; a small, flat band.</note></term>. Its <term>projection<note>original: "ſporto"; how far the molding extends outward from the shaft.</note></term> shall be determined in this way: first, let a circle be made as large as the column is thick at the bottom, and place that within a square; then, draw a circle outside the square passing through its four corners, and this shall be its projection. And because all other bases have square plinths, nonetheless this Tuscan one must be round according to the text of Vitruvius.

The height of the capital should be the same as the base. If this capital is divided into three parts, one shall be the <term>abacus<note>original: "abaco"; the flat slab at the very top of a capital.</note></term>. The second part shall be divided into four, and three of these shall be given to the <term>echinus<note>original: "Vuouolo"; the rounded, cushion-like molding.</note></term> while the other shall be its listel. The remaining third part shall be for the <term>necking<note>original: "fregio"; literally 'frieze,' but here referring to the neck of the capital.</note></term>. The <term>astragal<note>original: "tondino"; a small bead-like molding.</note></term> with its <term>collarino<note>original: "collarino"; a small ring or necking band.</note></term> should be half the height of the necking; if this is divided into three parts, two shall be for the astragal and the other for the collarino. Its projection should be as much as its height; and although it is joined with the capital, it is nonetheless a member of the column shaft.

The upper part of this column must be <term>diminished<note>original: "minuita"; the tapering of the column as it rises.</note></term> by a fourth part, so that the capital at the top will not be larger than the column at its base. The method for tapering the column shall be this: let the trunk of the column be divided into three parts, and the bottom third shall be perpendicular—that is, plumb. The remaining two-thirds shall be divided into as many equal parts as one desires. Then, at the one-third mark of the column, draw a semicircle. From the lines that hang from the outer edges of the capital, draw inward by one-eighth on each side, which totals one-fourth of the width. Below the collarino, one will draw two plumb lines falling onto the semicircle. That part of the circle remaining between this line and the outer edge of the column shall be divided into as many equal parts as those in the upper two-thirds of the column. Once this is done on both the right and left sides, let horizontal lines be drawn across from the two sides of the semicircle; each line should be numbered in order as they go down. Similarly, number the lines dividing the column in the same order. It is certain that the first line of the circle will align with the line below the collarino; then the second line of the circle is transferred to the second line of the column, the third line of the circle to the third line of the column, and the fourth line of the circle to the fourth line of the column. Once this is finished, a line is drawn from the base of the semicircle to the fourth line, from the fourth to the third, from the third to the second, and from the second to the first, doing so on both sides of the column. Even though these lines are straight in themselves, they nonetheless create a curved line, which the diligent craftsman then moderates by hand to smooth all the angles where the lines meet. Although this rule is made for the Tuscan column which is diminished by a fourth part, it can nonetheless serve every kind of column; the greater the number of parts used for the column and the semicircle, the more accurate the tapering will be.

<summary>This page details the specific geometric proportions and moldings of the Tuscan Order according to Vitruvius, including a step-by-step technical guide for creating 'entasis' or the graceful tapering of the column shaft.</summary>
<keywords>Tuscan Order, Vitruvius, architectural proportions, column tapering, entasis, moldings, base, capital, Renaissance architecture</keywords>

<meta>This page follows the introductory discussion of Vitruvian proportions for the Tuscan Order. While the previous page established the mathematical rules for height and width, this page provides a visual key and specific terminology for the component parts of the column's capital and base.</meta>

VII
->FOURTH BOOK<-
B

<image-desc>On the left is a vertical elevation of a column's capital and its base. The capital is labeled with letters A through F. The top of the shaft is marked G and the bottom H. The base consists of sections I, K, and L. Below this elevation is a plan view diagram showing concentric circles labeled M, N, and O, illustrating the different diameters and projections of the column components.</image-desc>

**A, Plinth, called the abacus, or cymatium.** <note>original: "Plintho, detto abaco, o cimasa." These terms refer to the flat, square slab forming the topmost part of the capital.</note>
**B, Echinus, called the ovolo.** <note>original: "Echino, detto Vuouolo." A rounded, convex molding; the name 'ovolo' comes from its egg-like shape.</note> +
**C, Annulet, called the small square, or regula.** <note>original: "Anulo, detto quadretto, o regolo." A small, flat decorative band.</note> +
**D, Hypotrachilium, called the neck-frieze.** <note>original: "Hipotrachelio, detto fregio." The part of the column that transitions between the shaft and the capital.</note> +
**E, Astragal, called the bead-molding.** <note>original: "Astragalo, detto Tondino." A small, semi-circular molding.</note> +
**F, Small square, called the collarino.** <note>original: "Quadretto, detto Collarino." The necking or 'little collar' of the column.</note> +
**G, Upper Shaft, that is, the thickness of the column at the top part.**

---

**H, Lower Shaft, that is, the thickness of the column at the bottom part.**
**I, Small square, called the small step; others call it a listel, others a band.** <note>original: "Quadretto, detto Gradetto, altri lo dicono Listello, altri cinta."</note>
**K, Torus, called the staff; others call it by various names.** <note>original: "Toro detto bastone." A large, semi-circular convex molding at the base of the column.</note>
**L, Plinth, called the block.** <note>original: "Plintho, detto Zocco." The square or circular lowest part of the base.</note>

---

**M, Projection of the base, called the overhang.** <note>original: "Proiettura de la base detta sporto." This indicates how far the base moldings extend beyond the column shaft.</note>
**N, Lower shaft of the column, that is, its thickness at the bottom.**
**O, Upper shaft of the column, that is, its thickness at the top.**

At the bottom of the shaft, a cross-section or profile of the base is shown with four horizontal subdivisions also numbered 1 through 4.</image-desc>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical breakdown of the Tuscan order begun on the previous page. Having defined the column's capital and base, the author now moves upward to describe the entablature—the horizontal structure consisting of the architrave, frieze, and cornice—providing both mathematical proportions and practical advice for the architect.</meta>

# BOOK

Once the column is completed with its base and capital, the architrave, frieze, and cornice are to be placed above it. The architrave should be as high as the capital, and a sixth part of it shall be the fascia <note>original: "fascia," a flat, horizontal band or strip</note>. The frieze should be of the same height, and similarly the cornice with its members. After dividing the cornice into four parts: one shall be for its ovolo <note>original: "Vuouolo," a rounded, egg-shaped molding</note>, two for the corona <note>original: "corona," the flat, projecting member of a cornice that sheds rain</note>, and the remaining part shall be given to the fascia beneath it. The projection of the whole should be at least as much as its height; and in the underside of the corona, some small channels should be made—larger or smaller according to the work—at the discretion of the Architect. 

But because this work is very solid and simple in its members, the Architect may, in my opinion, take the liberty of adding some members which seem naturally suited to such a species. This would be appropriate when one wishes to make the work more delicate, as can be seen in the separate part shown below. I also praise those coronas that have a projection greater than their square, provided the stones are capable of supporting themselves in the buildings; such projections provide both utility and decorum. Utility, because if walkways occur above them, they will be more spacious, and they will also preserve the work from rainwater. They provide decorum because, at the proper distance, the work will appear larger; and where the stone might lack thickness, the greater projection will compensate for it in appearance.

<image-desc>A detailed architectural diagram of a classical entablature, showing the profile of the cornice, frieze, and architrave. A central vertical section reveals a core structure, likely representing the internal masonry or support. Labels identify the specific architectural elements: 'Cymatium, called the ovolo' for the top molding; 'Corona, called the drip edge,' for the projecting drip edge; 'Cymatium, called the band,' for the molding above the frieze; 'Zophorus, called the frieze,' for the central decorative or plain band; 'Taenia, called the band or listel,' for the narrow band above the architrave; and 'Epistyle, called the architrave,' for the lowest structural beam.</image-desc>

<summary>This page details the proportions and components of the entablature (architrave, frieze, and cornice) that sits atop a column. The text emphasizes the architect's ability to adjust projections for both aesthetic grandeur and practical water management.</summary>

<keywords>entablature, architrave, frieze, cornice, architectural proportions, ovolo, corona, drip edge, Zophorus, Epistyle, Taenia, architectural utility</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Tuscan order. While the previous page focused on the crowning elements of the entablature, the author now addresses the fundamental proportions of the column shaft itself and introduces the pedestal, a feature not detailed by Vitruvius but necessary for practical architecture.</meta>

# BOOK FOUR
B ii

<image-desc>The page features two large architectural woodcuts flanking the central text block. On the left is a full-length depiction of a Tuscan column, showing a simple base, a plain shaft, and a simple capital supporting an entablature with a plain frieze. On the right, the lower portion of a similar column is shown resting on a substantial, square-proportioned pedestal ("piedestalo"), illustrating the geometric principles discussed in the text. The decorative initial 'B' at the start of the text contains a small cherub or putto figure.</image-desc>

Although I have stated above that the **Tuscan** column, according to the text of **Vitruvius**, should be seven parts high including its base and capital—a proportion and form that is certainly good and approved—nonetheless, because the first columns were made of six parts (taking this measure from the human foot, which is the sixth part of a man's height), and because the **Doric** columns will also be seven parts high (the ancients having added a part to those to give them more height), it seems to me, by such authorities and because this column is of a more robust style, that it should be made shorter than the **Doric**. Therefore, in my opinion, it shall be made of six parts including its base and its capital. Let this be a general rule, while observing the rest of the measurements we mentioned for the previous column and its ornaments.

And because neither **Vitruvius** nor any other architect, as far as I have seen, has ever given any rule for stylobates <note>original: "stilobati," the continuous masonry platform or individual pedestals supporting columns</note>, called pedestals—because in antiquity, as it appears, these things were made by architects according to their circumstances and needs, either to elevate the columns, or to reach porticos with steps, or for other arrangements—I would judge that, while we are not constrained by necessity, each style of column should be given its own appropriate pedestal, based on some probable reasoning.

It is a manifest thing that the pedestal should be at least square—I mean the die <note>original: "netto," the central, plain block of the pedestal without its top or bottom moldings</note>, excluding the base and the cap. Since the **Tuscan** column is the sturdiest of all others, its pedestal shall be a perfect square, the front of which should be as wide as the plinth <note>original: "zocco," the square block at the very bottom of the column's base</note> of the column’s base. Its height [of the die] shall be divided into four parts: one part shall be added for the lower plinth, and just as much shall be given to the cap. These members should be without any carving. Thus, since the column consists of six parts, the pedestal will itself be made of six parts <note>referring to the ratio of the four parts of the die plus two for the base and cap</note>, proportioned to the column.

<summary>The author argues for a sturdier, six-part Tuscan column based on human proportions and introduces a standardized design for pedestals, which were omitted by Vitruvius.</summary>
<keywords>Tuscan order, Vitruvius, column proportions, pedestals, stylobates, Doric order, architectural geometry</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the general rules of the Tuscan order to the practical application of architectural principles in the design of city and fortress gates. While the previous page focused on the specific proportions of the Tuscan column and pedestal, the author now begins to discuss the ornamentation of civic and military structures, preparing the reader for the more decorative Doric order.</meta>

# BOOK FOUR

I have promised in this present volume to treat only of the ornaments and the various styles of buildings. For this reason, I will not speak now of how city and fortress gates should be positioned—with their flanks, <term>embrasures<note>original: "cannoniere"; openings in a wall for firing cannons</note></term>, and other defensive requirements—leaving such a task to the military architect according to the sites and circumstances that may arise. However, once the gate of the city or fortress has been positioned, I will certainly describe the manner in which it should be adorned, in my opinion, by showing some figures. Every city gate requires a <term>postern gate<note>original: "porta del soccorso"; literally "gate of relief," a secondary entrance used for reinforcements or safe exit</note></term>, which others call a <term>wicket<note>original: "ponticella"; literally "little bridge"</note></term>. But to maintain **symmetry**—which means a proportionate correspondence—it is necessary to create another false one on the opposite side.

The measurements of the gate are to be made so that the height is equal to the width of the opening plus half of that width <note>i.e., a 1:1.5 ratio</note>. The height should be divided into six parts: one part shall be the width of the pilaster-strip on the right and left sides. The face of the pilasters should be one-third the width of the gate, and their height should be five parts, including the bases and capitals. The height of the bases should be one-third of the pilaster’s width, as should the capitals, always observing the rules given in the first column. The height of the **architrave**, **frieze**, and **cornice** should be equal to the face of the pilaster, following the rules given for the first order. Between one pilaster and the other shall be the postern gate; its width should be equal to the face of the pilaster, and its height should be double its width. Its pilaster-strips must be one-third of the width of that gate. The elevation above the gate shall be at the discretion of the Architect, but we will demonstrate the proportions of the <term>pediment<note>original: "fastigio" or "frontespicio"</note></term> in two ways within the **Doric order**.

---

This central arch is flanked by two smaller, rectangular side entrances. The entire facade is composed of heavy, textured stone blocks (rustication). Above the openings is a complete entablature—including an architrave, a plain frieze, and a projecting cornice—topped by a central triangular pediment and horizontal attic sections.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of city and fortress gates within Book Four. While the previous page established the primary proportions for a single-opening gate, this section introduces a more complex design involving a central arch flanked by two side entrances. It focuses on the importance of an architect's "invention" and the specific mathematical ratios required to maintain harmony when multiple openings are combined.</meta>

-># FOURTH<-

And because the Architect must be rich in **invention** to satisfy himself and others, one may also adorn the gate of a city or fortress in this alternative manner. One must observe this rule: that however wide the opening of said gate shall be, its height shall be a <term>sesquialter<note>original: "sesquialtera"; a ratio of 1:1.5, or 2 parts width to 3 parts height</note></term>—that is, two parts in width to three in height. Its pilaster-strip should be one-eighth part of the width of the gate, and the column should be one-fourth part of that same gate. But because the column is one-third part embedded within the wall—bound with the other stones and placed more for ornament than for supporting weight—it shall be made seven parts in height; it could even allow for eight in such a case, should the Architect wish to make the gate more slender. 

The opening of the side gates shall be half that of the main gate, and their pilaster-strips shall be like those of the large one. Its height shall be such that the band supporting the arch serves as its <term>lintel<note>original: "supercilio o architraue"; the horizontal beam or "eyebrow" spanning the top of the door</note></term>, or architrave, whichever we wish to call it. And if a stone of a single piece cannot be found for such a need, let the <term>voussoirs<note>original: "cunei"; the wedge-shaped stones that form an arch or lintel</note></term> be made as illustrated. Thus, the proportion of these gates will be <term>five-to-three<note>original Latin: "superbipartiens tertias"; a ratio of 1 and 2/3, which is 5:3</note></term>, that is, three parts in width and five in height. The voussoirs of the arch shall number fifteen. In the bases, capitals, architrave, frieze, and cornice, the rule given for the first column should be observed; likewise, the central elevation shall be at the Architect's discretion, as has been said of the others. The more roughly such works are hewn <note>referring to the "rusticated" or unfinished look of the stone</note>, the better they will serve the <term>decorum<note>original: "decoro"; the principle that a building's style should suit its function</note></term> of the fortress.

<summary>This page describes the design of a triple-entry city gate, emphasizing the specific mathematical ratios (3:2 for the main arch and 5:3 for side doors) and the use of rusticated stone to convey the strength appropriate for a fortress.</summary>
<keywords>fortress gate, sesquialter ratio, superbipartiens tertias, voussoirs, rustication, architectural decorum, proportions</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of city and fortress gates, moving from a single-arched entrance to a more robust, tripartite (three-part) design. It builds on the previous page's focus on the "sesquialter" ratio—a height-to-width proportion of 1.5 to 1—while offering the architect more flexibility to adapt to specific site conditions.</meta>

-># BOOK<-

In another way, both simpler and also stronger, one may construct the gate of a city or a castle by observing the design noted here below. Its proportion should be such that the width of the **opening** of said gate matches its height measured up to the <term>band<note>original: "fascia"; a flat, horizontal molding or impost from which the arch begins to spring</note></term> that supports the <term>vault<note>original: "uolto"; here referring to the curved ceiling of the archway</note></term>. Its height from the band upward shall be as much more as is required for the **semicircle**. However, it is always at the discretion of the **Architect** to provide more or less according to necessity, and especially when he is constrained by some accidental circumstance. 

And thus on its sides—on the right and left parts—two smaller gates are to be made, as I have said of the others. Their width should be half that of the central gate, and an equal amount of **solid wall** should be left between the large gate and the two smaller ones. The height of these side gates should be double their width; thus the band that supports the arch of the main gate will also serve as a support for the <term>voussoirs<note>original: "cunei"; the wedge-shaped stones that form an arch or lintel</note></term> of these side doors. Furthermore, one could make this band the <term>lintel<note>original: "supercilio"; the horizontal beam forming the upper part of a door frame</note></term> itself—that is, the architrave for said gate. This gate, as has been stated, may be made both smaller and larger as it shall seem fit to the Architect, provided he does not deviate much from the forms given here.

It is flanked by two smaller rectangular doorways. Above each of these side doors is a rectangular recessed panel or niche. The entire structure is rendered with horizontal bands of rustication, common in Renaissance military or civic architecture.</image-desc>

<meta>This page transitions from the specific measurements of city gates to the broader applications of vaulted structures. ->### FOURTH BOOK ###<-

The diversity of inventions sometimes leads the **Architect** to decide on things that perhaps never even crossed his mind. For this reason, the figure shown below will provide much convenience and utility to buildings according to the circumstances that might befall the Architect. For instance, in the wall of a **fortress**, even if the walls are of a good thickness, by constructing this work on the interior side, it would first serve as a <term>loggia<note>a covered exterior gallery or corridor</note></term> to remain under cover. It would also make the walkway above wider and more convenient for defense; and in the event of a <term>battery<note>original: "batteria"; an artillery assault or bombardment</note></term>, for greater safety, all the <term>openings<note>original: "uani"; the recessed spaces or voids within the structure</note></term> could be filled in and made solid.

It might also happen that the **Architect** must build near a mountain. There, to protect the site from said mountain—because of the waters that continually run down from the rains and carry soil to the lower parts—it is necessary to buttress against the mountain with a similar structure. Through this, one would not only be secured against such risks but would also add great ornament to the building. **Raphael of Urbino** made use of such an invention at **Monte Mario** just above **Rome**, at the <term>Vigna<note>literally "vineyard," but used in Renaissance Rome to describe a grand suburban villa and its gardens</note></term> of Clement VII, which Raphael began while the Pope was still a Cardinal. **Girolamo Genga**, at the Imperial Hill outside of **Pesaro**, used this same invention near a beautiful building for the convenience of his patron; he used it to support a water cistern, also leaning against a mountain, but executed in very delicate <term>brickwork<note>original: "opera lateritia"; masonry made of fired bricks</note></term>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of architectural "inventions" from the previous page, moving from the large-scale application of arches for fortifications and mountain buttressing to the specific technical construction of rusticated masonry openings. ->### FOURTH BOOK ###<-

*The ancients used various ways of* <term>bonding<note>original: "ligamenti"; the structural interlocking of masonry units</note></term> *this* <term>rusticated work<note>original: "opera rustica"; masonry featuring rough, textured surfaces and deep-set joints, often used for the ground floors of palaces or for fortifications</note></term>*, as is shown demonstrated below; from which invention the* **Architect** *may adapt to various things, according to the circumstances that may occur. Its measurement shall be such that the opening is a perfect square. The solid pier between one opening and the other should be one-quarter smaller than that opening. The lintel, called the* **Architrave**, *should be one-fourth the height of the opening, and it should be made of* <term>wedges<note>original: "cunei"; these are wedge-shaped stones, also known as voussoirs, which distribute weight outward</note></term> *that converge at the center, in an odd number. And thus, above it, a semicircle shall be drawn, divided into nine equal parts, and all lines shall be drawn to its center, and its wedges formed, with the three pieces with the band above placed between them: and in this way the work will be very strong and perpetual. But so that the wedges of the architrave stay firmer, it will be necessary to fill the semicircle with* <term>brickwork<note>original: "opera lateritia"; literally "baked stone" (pietra cotta)</note></term>*, and for greater ornament, it can be made of* <term>reticulated work<note>original: "opera reticulata"; a Roman masonry technique using diamond-shaped tuff blocks that create a net-like appearance</note></term>*, as the ancients used. And in* **Rome**, *at the church of* **Saints Cosmas and Damian**, *one can still see similar bonds that remain very strong, even though they are extremely ancient.*

<summary>This page describes the precise geometric proportions and structural techniques for constructing rusticated openings, emphasizing the use of wedge-shaped stones and relieving arches for durability. The author cites the ancient Roman technique of "opera reticulata" and the church of Saints Cosmas and Damian as historical precedents for these methods.</summary>

<keywords>rustication, architrave, voussoirs, brickwork, opera reticulata, Roman architecture, structural bonding, Saints Cosmas and Damian</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for constructing rusticated portals. It moves from the general principles of masonry bonding discussed on the previous page to specific mathematical proportions for a semi-circular arched gateway, noting its suitability for civil rather than military architecture.</meta>

<sig>C</sig>

In this gateway, the ingenious **Architect** may make use [of this design] in various places, as was stated at the beginning; however, not for fortress gates, because the entrance or <term>vestibule<note>original: "vestibolo"; a foyer or hallway leading from the door into the interior</note></term> (as we might call it) would not be suitable for handling <term>artillery<note>original: "artigliarie"; large cannons or heavy mounted weaponry</note></term> or large weapons for defense. Nevertheless, the exterior portion can serve for any doorway. Its proportions shall be such that the height of the opening is twice its width. The <term>wedges<note>original: "cunei"; also known as voussoirs, these are the tapered stones that form the arch and distribute weight</note></term> of the semi-circle shall be nine in number, and let all their lines be drawn toward the center of the circle. The <term>flat band<note>original: "fascia piana"; here referring to the impost, the block upon which the arch rests</note></term> that supports the arch should be one-seventh the width of the doorway. From this band down to the floor, the space shall be divided into seven and a half parts, and six courses of stones shall be made: three of these shall be one and a half parts each, and the other three shall be one part each, and in this way the seven and a half parts shall be distributed. The height of the <term>middle wedge<note>original: "cuneo di mezo"; the keystone, which locks the arch together at its highest point</note></term> should be half the width of the doorway. The <term>molding<note>original: "fascia"; a decorative strip or band running along the outer edge of the arch</note></term> that runs above the wedges should be as high as the "foot" (the inner edge) of the wedges; however, the middle wedge shall be one-fourth larger than the others.

The masonry courses on the facade vary in height according to the proportions described in the text.*

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for constructing rusticated portals. It moves from general masonry principles to specific mathematical proportions for a grand gateway, emphasizing its use in domestic villa architecture rather than military fortifications.</meta>

The following door may serve for all the buildings mentioned at the beginning: and since it is **rusticated work**, it would be very appropriate for the **villa**, as is seen in many places in Italy along the roads opposite some noble house, as an enclosure for the **courtyard**; for such structures give a grand presence to these locations. 

The proportions of this gate shall be such that the height of the opening is double its width up to the underside of the arch. The <term>facing of the opening<note>original: "pilastrata"; the vertical inner frame or "jamb" of the doorway</note></term> shall be one-fifth the width of the <term>clearance<note>original: "lume"; literally 'the light', referring to the open span of the doorway</note></term>, and the arch shall be the same. The <term>pilaster<note>original: "pilastro"; a rectangular column integrated into the wall</note></term> should be double the width of the facing, and its height should be six parts of its width. 

The height of the base shall be one-fourth the width of the pilaster. The <term>capital<note>original: "capitello"; the decorative head of the pilaster</note></term> shall be one-third the height of the pilaster. The <term>flat band<note>original: "fascia piana"</note></term>, which takes the place of the <term>architrave<note>original: "architrave"; the primary horizontal beam resting atop the capitals</note></term>, should be as tall as the capital; the **frieze** and the **cornice** should likewise be the same height. However, for the specific components—such as the bases, capitals, and cornice—the rules provided for the first order should be followed. 

The <term>impost<note>original: "imposta"; the structural block from which the arch begins to curve</note></term> that supports the arch shall be the same height as the capital, divided according to the same rules mentioned earlier. As for the other components shown—namely the <term>wedges<note>original: "cunei"; the tapered stones, or voussoirs, that form the arch</note></term> and other stones—their proportions can easily be determined using a compass. And if, for even greater ornament, one wishes to add a <term>pediment<note>original: "frontespicio"; a decorative triangular or curved gable above the door</note></term>—truly a feature of great beauty—the method for this is demonstrated in two ways within the **Doric order**.

# B O O K

Although the portal illustrated below differs greatly from other styles, I have chosen to include it because, in my view, it is well-suited to the **Tuscan** <term>Tuscan: the simplest and sturdiest of the five classical orders, characterized by unadorned columns and a rustic appearance</term> order and is an authentic ancient design. For a long time, this was located at the head of the **Trajan** <note>original: "militia Traiana"—likely referring to the area of the Markets of Trajan or the medieval "Torre delle Milizie" built over Roman ruins</note> barracks in **Rome**, though due to various ruins, it can no longer be seen there today. The two niches shown alongside are not in their original positions; however, to demonstrate the variety of niches that can be adapted to such a work, I have placed them here so that the discerning architect might make use of them, later situating them appropriately in his own designs. Their proportions will be easy for the **Architect** to determine using the rules provided at the beginning of this book. As for the portal itself, I will provide no specific measurements, for they can be easily found by using a compass <note>Architects of this period used proportional dividers or a compass to scale measurements directly from the printed plates</note> on the drawing.

<summary>The author describes a rustic ancient portal formerly located in Rome near Trajan's complex, providing it as a model for the Tuscan order alongside two decorative niche designs.</summary>
<keywords>Tuscan order, Trajan's Markets, Rome, architectural proportions, portal design, niches, rustication</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding the "Tuscan" or rustic style of Roman architecture, specifically focusing on the construction and placement of a portal and decorative niches.</meta>

# B O O K F O U R

*This style of **segmental-arched portal** <note>original: "porta a remenato"—a shallow arch forming a segment of a circle rather than a full semi-circle</note>, which comprises the sixth part of a circle, is a very sturdy structure; nevertheless, the **voussoirs** <term>voussoirs: the wedge-shaped stones, called "cunei" in the original, that make up the arch</term> cannot be integrated with other stone bindings, but must remain separate without being joined to other continuous courses of masonry. For this reason, if one wishes to build such a work, it would be well suited to a wall made of **brickwork** <note>original: "opera lateritia"—the ancient Roman style of building with thin, flat bricks</note>. Regarding its proportions, I shall not elaborate further, as it will be a simple task to find its measurements using a **compass** <note>the author assumes the reader will use a divider or compass to take measurements directly from the printed illustration to scale them to a real building</note>. However, as for the **niches** <note>original: "nicchi"</note> placed at the sides to fill the empty space, the **Architect** may position them at his own discretion in the places that best suit his purpose. They may serve not only as niches but also as windows. If they are intended to hold **statues**, it is always advisable for the height to exceed the **double proportion** <note>meaning the height should be more than twice the width to provide enough vertical space</note>, so that a standing statue placed within corresponds more harmoniously; such matters are always left to the judgment of the Architect.*

<summary>This page describes the technical construction of a segmental arch (remantato) and the aesthetic placement of decorative niches, suggesting they be paired with brickwork and sized harmoniously for statues.</summary>
<keywords>segmental arch, voussoirs, brickwork, architectural proportions, niches, statues, Tuscan order, compass measurement</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of portal design, transitioning from the simple segmental arch to a more complex "mixed" style that combines rustic stonework with the refined classical orders. This follows the author's previous advice on using judgment to place niches and statues.</meta>

-># B O O K<-

It was the opinion of the ancient **Romans** to mix with the rustic style not only the **Doric**; but the **Ionic**, and the **Corinthian** as well: therefore, it will not be an error if a mixture is made within a single style, representing in this work part the work of nature, and part the work of the artisan. For the columns banded by rustic stones, as well as the architrave and frieze interrupted by **voussoirs** <term>voussoirs: the wedge-shaped stones, called "cunei" in the original, that form the arch</term>, demonstrate the work of nature; however, the capitals and parts of the columns, along with the cornice and the pediment, represent the work of the human hand. This mixture, in my view, is very pleasing to the eye and represents great strength. Therefore, I would judge this style more suitable for a fortress than any other; nonetheless, in whatever place it is positioned within a rustic building, it will always turn out well. 

**Giulio Romano** took more delight in such a mixture than any other, as **Rome** bears witness in several places, and also **Mantua** in the most beautiful palace called the **Te**, located just a short distance outside the city—a truly magnificent example of **Architecture** and painting in our times. The proportion of this portal is to be made such that the opening is of **double proportion** <note>meaning the height is exactly twice the width</note>: that is, two widths in height up to the underside of the arch. If the width is divided into seven parts, one part shall be for the **jamb** <note>original: "pilastrata"</note>; thus the column will be twice as thick, and its height including the capital shall be eight parts. The capital, the architrave, the frieze, and the cornice should be as described at the beginning of this book, and the pediment shall appear as described in the **Doric** section. 

The semi-circle of the arch is to be divided into 11 parts, and these shall be the voussoirs; however, the one in the middle <note>the keystone</note> shall be somewhat larger, and it is also at the liberty of the **Architect** to make it hang below the arch somewhat further than the others. The **impost** <term>impost: the horizontal masonry weighted by the arch, referred to here as the "fascia" that supports the arch</term> should be half the thickness of the column, and from that point downward, nine parts shall be made. Of these, two shall be for the lowest part of the column, and the remaining seven parts, divided equally, shall be for the stones that bind and cross the column. The more roughly this work is sketched out—provided it is done with artistry—the more it will preserve the **decorum** <note>the sense of appropriateness and dignity in classical design</note> of such an order, especially regarding the stones that encircle the columns and also the voussoirs.

<summary>This page explains the aesthetic and technical principles of mixing "rustic" (natural-looking) stonework with refined classical orders, citing Giulio Romano's Palazzo del Te as a prime example. It provides specific proportional rules for constructing a banded column portal with a semi-circular arch.</summary>

<keywords>Giulio Romano, Palazzo del Te, rustic style, Mannerism, proportions, voussoirs, Doric order, Roman architecture, architectural theory</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
as for the cornice, and for the corbels, which are above the door, and of which part of its height...

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues the architectural discourse from the previous discussion on cornices and corbels, moving from decorative elements above a door to the structural and aesthetic integrity of stone arches.</meta>

It is not enough for a work to be exceptionally strong; it must also be pleasing to the eye and ingeniously crafted. For this reason, this method of <term>stone binding</term> <note>Italian: *legature di pietre*; the technique of interlocking stones to ensure stability and beauty</note> is not only incredibly strong, but also clever and pleasing to those who view it. An architect may make use of this invention for many different purposes.

Its proportions shall be such that the height is one and a half times the width of the opening. The number of <term>voussoirs</term> <note>Italian: *cunei*; the wedge-shaped stones that form the curve of an arch</note> shall be arranged so that the semicircle is divided into nine and a quarter parts; this is because the central wedge shall be one-quarter larger than the others, and each of the other stones shall occupy one part. The height of the central <term>keystone</term> <note>the wedge at the very top of the arch</note> should be half the width of the opening. The flat band that supports the arch should be one-seventh of the opening’s width; likewise, from that point downward, the <term>pier</term> <note>Italian: *pilastrone*; a heavy rectangular pillar supporting the arch</note> should be divided into seven parts. The band above the wedges should be as high as the width of the bottom of the wedge. This wedge can be made to hang lower by one-eighth of its width. Regarding the interlocking of the other stones with the wedges, the illustration demonstrates it clearly.

<summary>This page explains the geometric and aesthetic principles for constructing a reinforced stone arch, emphasizing that architectural works must balance structural strength with visual appeal.</summary>
<keywords>architecture, masonry, stone binding, arch proportions, voussoirs, keystone, Renaissance engineering</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of architectural proportions, moving from the specific construction of stone joints to the overall design of a monumental arched gateway that balances utility with decorative splendor.</meta>

D

Since conveniences have been transformed into ornament, and at times ornament exceeds what is strictly necessary—to showcase both the skill of the artist and the wealth of the patron—this design was created for <term>utility</term> <note>Italian: *commodità*; functional convenience</note>, for <term>strength</term> <note>Italian: *fortezza*</note>, and for <term>decorum</term> <note>Italian: *decoro*; the principle of aesthetic and social appropriateness</note>. It serves utility through its <term>openings</term> <note>Italian: *aperture*; the windows or doorways</note>; it provides strength because there is a wide, solid, and well-bonded section of masonry between each opening; and it maintains decorum because it is richly ornamented. A discerning <term>Architect</term> will know how to adapt this invention to various needs.

The proportions shall be such that the solid wall section is equal in width to the opening, and the opening itself shall be twice as high as it is wide. The <term>pilasters</term> <note>flat, rectangular columns attached to the wall</note> shall be one-eighth of the opening’s width, and the <term>columns</term> shall be one-fourth. The space between one column and the next should be equal to the thickness of a single column, and the column's total height, including the <term>base</term> and the <term>capital</term>, should consist of eight parts. For the <term>architrave</term> <note>the primary beam resting across the tops of the columns</note>, the <term>frieze</term> <note>the decorative horizontal band above the architrave</note>, and the <term>cornice</term> <note>the projecting top molding that crowns the structure</note>, as well as the bases and capitals, the established rules of the orders should be observed. 

The <term>voussoirs</term> <note>Italian: *cunei*; the wedge-shaped stones that form the arch</note> and other structural bindings are visible in the illustration. Furthermore, although these columns exceed the standard measurement by two thicknesses, this is because they are placed close together and are partially recessed into the wall, serving more for ornament than for the actual support of the work. In such cases, this variation is permitted, supported by the authority of many <term>ancient works</term> <note>original: *antique*; referring to the surviving structures of classical Rome</note>.

<summary>This page describes the design of a classical arched entryway, detailing how to balance structural strength with decorative elements like recessed columns and entablatures according to ancient Roman precedents.</summary>
<keywords>classical architecture, proportions, columns, voussoirs, decorum, Renaissance engineering, architrave</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of architectural design, moving from the general principles of gateways to a specific solution for using undersized columns in a loggia through the clever use of masonry wedge-stones.</meta>

It is a wonderful thing for an **Architect** to be rich in **inventions** <note>Italian: *inuentioni*; creative problem-solving or design ingenuity</note>, because of the variety of accidents and circumstances that occur during building. For example, one may occasionally find a good number of columns available, but of such a low height that they cannot fulfill the needs of the structure—unless the Architect knows how to find a clever way to employ them. For this reason, if a column is not of sufficient height to reach and support the **fascia** <note>a flat horizontal band in architecture</note> which sits at the level of the ceiling of this **loggia** <note>an open-sided gallery or porch</note>, one may use this method of **wedge-stones** <note>Italian: *cunei*; also known as voussoirs, these are tapered stones that lock together to form an arch or a lintel</note> to rise to the necessary height. However, this is provided that there are very strong **buttresses** <note>Italian: *contraforti*; structural supports built against a wall</note> on both the right and left sides of such an **order** <note>the specific style of columns and their decoration</note>, so that it will remain very strong despite the weight from above. 

It is indeed true that the floor above would not be secure in an arched form without very good **keys** <note>Italian: *chiaui*; metal clamps or tie-rods used to hold masonry together</note> made of either bronze or iron. Yet, it will be safer if the loggia is not so wide that the floor cannot be made of a single piece of stone, or else from good beams of **larch**, **oak**, or **pine**. All these timbers, while perhaps not eternal, will last at least a great number of years if they are put to use with diligence—either by charring the surfaces of the beam-ends that are placed into the walls, or by wrapping them in lead sheets, or by coating them in pitch. 

The **proportion** of this work shall be as follows: the width of the arched opening shall be four column-thicknesses, and its height shall be twice that measure. The smaller spaces shall be three column-thicknesses in width, and their height shall be six column-thicknesses. Thus, all the openings will be **two squares** <note>a 2:1 ratio of height to width</note>, and the columns, in order to support a great weight, shall follow the first measurement. The arrangement of the **wedge-stones** and the other bindings is seen very clearly in the figure, but regarding the **bases** and the **capitals**, everything that needs to be said was covered in the discussion of the first column.

<summary>This page describes a creative architectural solution for using columns that are too short for a design by extending their height with decorative and structural masonry wedges. It also provides practical advice on preserving wooden floor beams using lead or pitch.</summary>
<keywords>architectural invention, loggia, wedge-stones, cunei, masonry, timber preservation, larch, oak, proportions, columns</keywords>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of architectural design, moving from the use of short columns to a specific method of constructing rusticated arches with interlocking masonry for structural strength and aesthetic appeal.</meta>

This method of arches, besides being very strong, is also ingenious due to the harmony of its **interlocking joints** <note>Italian: *legature*; the way the stones are bound or "keyed" together for structural stability</note>, and is pleasing to the eye. This **invention** <note>original: *inuentione*; a creative technical design or solution</note> can serve not only for **loggias** and **porticos** <note>covered walkways supported by columns or arches</note> of such works, but it would also be very convenient for bridges over rivers and torrents, and for conducting water from one mountain to another; it would even serve for an **aqueduct** spanning a long road. 

Its proportion is such that the distance from one **pillar** <note>Italian: *pilastro*</note> to another should be the same as the height measured up to the **fascia** <note>a flat horizontal band or trim</note> that supports the **vault** <note>Italian: *uolto*; the arched structure</note>. The fascia should be one-seventh the width of the opening, and from that point downwards, the pillar should be divided into six parts. The **semi-circle** <note>the arch itself</note> is to be divided into nine and a quarter parts, because the middle **wedge-stone** <note>Italian: *cuneo*; the central keystone</note> is larger than the others by one-quarter. For the rest of the interlocking joints, the measurement of the whole will be found with a **compass** in hand.

# BOOK <unclear>...</unclear>

**P**ecause sometimes it might happen that a continuous structure is needed, in which there would be a need for many openings for the convenience of the buildings; this work would be very strong and suited to support any great weight. And if there is no need for so many openings, one may well observe this order of closing some of them with **brickwork** <term>lateritia</term> <note>original: *opera lateritia*; construction made of bricks or tiles, a hallmark of Roman engineering</note>, and this arrangement will serve the purpose. 

Its **proportion** <note>the mathematical relationship between different parts of the structure</note> shall be that the **solid pier** <note>Italian: *sodo*; the solid part of the wall between two openings</note> is as wide as the **opening** <note>Italian: *apertura*</note>, and let the height be twice the width, although such things can be done more or less according to the needs and the judgment of the modest **Architect**. Similar works can be seen at present in **Rome**, things not only very ancient, but even to this day very strong, which are located at **Saints Cosmas and Damian** <note>The Basilica of Santi Cosma e Damiano, which incorporated the ancient Roman Temple of Romulus and the Forum of Peace</note>.

# FOURTH BOOK

Witnessing various designs often inspires one to create things that might never have been attempted without seeing them realized in practice. For sometimes one may find the facade of a building without any openings—perhaps in a garden, a courtyard, or another location—that requires rich ornamentation in this **rusticated style** <term>opera rustica</term> <note>original: *opera rustica*; a style of masonry using large, rough-hewn or textured stone blocks to create a sense of strength, weight, and natural beauty</note>. In such instances, the prudent **Architect** may make use of this design; these spaces are also well-suited for the placement of statues and other relics of antiquity. Regarding its **proportions** <note>the mathematical relationship between the height, width, and depth of the architectural elements</note> and measurements, I shall not dwell on them at length, as it remains at the discretion of the **Architect** to widen or heighten the structure according to the specific circumstances of the site.

The central niche is larger and topped with a decorative shell-like semi-dome made of radiating rusticated masonry. The two flanking niches are smaller and topped with heavy rusticated arches. The entire facade is composed of large, textured stone blocks (opera rustica). Light geometric construction lines are visible in the background above the structure, indicating perspective or proportional planning. At the bottom, a horizontal section or plan view shows a series of square piers or openings.</image-desc>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of architectural masonry, moving from the aesthetic use of rusticated stone facades to specific structural solutions for wide door and shop openings.</meta>

-> BOOK <-

Because the majority of **lintels** <term>supercilij</term> <note>original: *supercilij*; from the Latin for "eyebrow," referring to the horizontal beam or molding over an opening</note>, or **architraves** as we may call them, which are placed over certain doors or even shops, cannot resist the weight due to the width of the opening if the stone is not of a very great thickness; and for this reason, in the course of time, they come to break, as can be seen in many, many places; it will be possible—no matter how great the span—provided that the side supports <term>spalle</term> <note>literally "shoulders"; the vertical piers or jambs that receive the weight of the arch</note> are strong, to construct such a thing out of multiple pieces, in the two ways illustrated here below; undoubtedly such a work will be very strong, and the greater the load from above, the more the work will move toward greater durability.

<image-desc>
Two architectural woodcut diagrams illustrating methods for constructing lintels or arches over wide openings to prevent structural failure. 
The top diagram shows a flat arch (platband) integrated into a brick wall, composed of several wedge-shaped stone blocks (voussoirs) that distribute weight to the side supports.
The bottom diagram shows a segmental arch constructed of stone blocks set into a brick wall, designed to support the load above a horizontal opening.
Both diagrams use hatching and line work to distinguish between stone blocks and brickwork.
</image-desc>
