--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page follows the technical discussion of masonry and structural lintels for wide openings. The author now transitions from the exterior structural elements of doors and shops to the interior architectural features of noble rooms, specifically focusing on the functional and ornamental design of fireplaces.</meta>

Even though in the writings and drawings of **Vitruvius** <note>Marcus Vitruvius Pollio, the Roman architect whose 1st-century BCE treatise *De Architectura* served as the foundational text for Renaissance architecture</note> there is no record of how the ancients used fires to warm themselves in noble rooms, nor is any trace of **fireplaces** <term>camini</term> <note>original: *camini*; used here to refer to the entire chimney and hearth system</note> found in ancient buildings to allow smoke to escape, nor have I ever been able to learn the truth of this matter from any **Architect**, no matter how expert; nevertheless, because for many, many years now it has been the custom not only to build fires in halls and chambers for human comfort, but also to decorate such places with various ornaments: as I am treating in this volume all the ornaments that may be required of the Architect and for buildings, I shall not fail to show some forms of fireplaces, as they are commonly called throughout Italy. These forms and methods are adapted to the **Tuscan order**, should such things be needed in similar buildings; one is made in a refined Tuscan style protruding from the wall, and the other is of **rusticated work** <term>opera rustica</term> <note>original: *opera rustica*; a style of masonry featuring large, rough-hewn stone blocks that suggest a rugged, natural strength</note> contained entirely within the thickness of the wall.

<image-desc>Two side-by-side woodcut illustrations of fireplace designs. On the left, a fireplace in the "refined" Tuscan style features a prominent, tapered hood projecting outward from the wall, supported by slender pilasters. On the right, a "rusticated" fireplace is shown flush with the wall surface, its opening framed by heavy, textured stone blocks arranged in a sturdy arch. Both designs show a domed interior structure within the hearth to facilitate the drawing of smoke.</image-desc>

<summary>In this passage, the author acknowledges that ancient Roman architecture provides no precedents for fireplaces, yet he provides two contemporary designs in the Tuscan style to meet the modern demand for indoor heating and ornamentation.</summary>
<keywords>Vitruvius, fireplaces, Tuscan order, rustication, interior design, smoke exhaustion, Renaissance architecture</keywords>

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Tuscan and Rusticated orders, shifting from the specific application of fireplaces (seen on the previous page) back to the general decorative techniques of masonry walls. The author explores the evolution of rusticated stone from its rough, primitive origins to the refined "diamond point" styles popular in the Renaissance.</meta>

The first **Rusticated** <term>Rustiche</term> <note>original: *Rustiche*; refers to *bugnato*, a style of masonry where the stones are left with a rough, protruding surface to suggest strength and antiquity</note> works were made in this way: that is, with pieces of stone sketched out very roughly; but their joints <term>commissure</term> <note>original: *commissure*; the seams or gaps where two stones meet</note> are made with the greatest care.

<image-desc>A horizontal rectangular illustration showing a wall of "opera rustica" masonry. The stone blocks have a rough, textured, irregular surface (rough-hewn), but the joints (commissure) between them are straight and tightly fitted.</image-desc>

**A**fterward, with somewhat more delicacy, they divided the square blocks with this flat plane, which separates them, making them worked with more precision; and then they added these **crossed edges** <term>Spigoli incrociati</term> <note>original: *Spigoli incrociati*; beveling or chamfering the edges of the stone where they meet to create a recessed border</note> for further ornament.

<column-break/>

**O**ther **Architects**, wishing to imitate cut diamonds, worked in this way, crafting them with more refinement.

<image-desc>An illustration of two types of refined rusticated masonry. On the left half, rectangular blocks have smooth beveled edges, with the lower row showing "spigoli incrociati" (crossed edges forming a low pyramid). On the right half, the blocks are carved with facets to resemble polished cut diamonds.</image-desc>

**A**nd so, from age to age, this work has come to be varied: sometimes in imitation of a diamond on a flat surface <term>tauola piana</term>, and sometimes with greater relief, as is seen drawn here below.

<image-desc>Masonry examples showing further variations in relief. The top row features rectangular blocks with wide, flat-faced bevels. The bottom row shows high-relief "diamond point" rustication, where each block is a sharp, four-sided pyramid. Ghostly outlines from the reverse side (a circle and arch) are visible behind the engraving.</image-desc>

**S**ome other **Architects** have wanted to use even greater delicacy and a more orderly arrangement; nonetheless, all such work had its origin in **Rusticated** work, even though it is commonly called "diamond point."

<image-desc>A detailed decorative masonry panel showcasing a complex architectural pattern. It features alternating sizes of blocks: large rectangular blocks with recessed panels alternate with smaller square blocks that have raised diamond facets. The whole forms a rhythmic, ornamental grid.</image-desc>

->THE DORIC BEGINS.<-

<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of Rusticated stone masonry to the first of the formal architectural orders: the Doric. It moves from general wall textures to the specific proportions and symbolic meanings of the columns and their bases.</meta>

# ON THE DORIC ORDER CH. VI.

The ancients dedicated this **Doric** work to **Jupiter**, **Mars**, **Hercules**, and certain other robust **gods**. But after the incarnation of human salvation, we **Christians** must proceed with another order: for when building a temple consecrated to **Jesus Christ our Redeemer**, or to **Saint Paul**, **Saint Peter**, **Saint George**, or other similar saints—whose profession was not only that of a soldier, but who possessed the manliness and strength to lay down their lives for the faith of **Christ**—for all such as these, this **Doric** style <term>generation</term> <note>original: *generation*; here used to mean a specific class or species of architectural style</note> is appropriate. And it is suitable not only for **gods**, but if an edifice—whether public or private—is built for men-at-arms, robust men, great personages, or those of middle or low station, this **Doric** work is fitting; and the more robust the personage, the more solid the work should be for them. And if the man, though a soldier, should also possess something of the delicate, the works may likewise be made with some delicacy, as we shall speak of in its proper place; now let us come to the specific works and their proportions.

**Vitruvius** <note>Marcus Vitruvius Pollio, a Roman architect whose 1st-century BCE treatise *De Architectura* was the primary authority for Renaissance architects</note> treats this **Doric** work in the third chapter of his fourth book, though he treated the base for the column in the third: although it is the opinion of some that this is the **Corinthian** base, because it is put into use and placed on **Corinthian** and **Ionic** columns. It is also the view of some that **Doric** columns had no bases, considering many ancient buildings, such as the Theater of **Marcellus** in **Rome**—a most beautiful work that is **Doric** from the middle downward—the columns of which theater have no bases, but the shaft <term>tronco</term> of them is seen resting upon a step without any other member. There are also at the **Tullian** Prison <note>The Mamertine Prison in Rome</note> the remains of a **Doric** temple, the columns of which are without bases. A triumphal arch of **Doric** work is seen in **Verona**, where the columns are deprived of bases; nevertheless, because the ancient **Romans** made **Corinthian** bases in another way (as I will show in its proper place), I say that the **Attic** base <term>Atticurga</term> described by **Vitruvius** in the third book is the **Doric** one. This can be seen observed by **Bramante the Architect** <note>Donato Bramante, the leading architect of the High Renaissance who designed the Tempietto and the initial plans for St. Peter's Basilica</note> in the buildings he constructed in **Rome**. To this **Bramante**—he being the discoverer and light of good and true **Architecture**, which from the ancients until his time under **Pope Julius II** <term>Iulio. II. Pontifice massimo</term> had been buried—full faith should be given.

**The Doric base**, then, shall be as high as half the thickness of the column, and the **Plinth** <term>Plintho</term>, called the **Footing** <term>Zocco</term>, shall be a third part of its height. From the remainder, let four parts be made: one shall be for the superior **Torus** <term>Toro</term> <note>A large, convex, rounded molding</note> called the **Roundlet** <term>Tondino</term>; the three remaining shall be divided into two equal parts: one shall be for the inferior **Torus** called the **Baton** <term>Bastone</term>, and the other shall be given to the **Trochilus** or **Scotia**, which others call the **Hollow** <term>Cauetto</term> <note>A concave molding that creates a deep shadow</note>. But then, having made seven parts of this [the Scotia], one shall be for the superior small square <term>quadretto</term> and another for the inferior. The projection <term>Sporto</term> of the base shall be half its height, and thus the **Plinth** will be one and a half thicknesses of the column on each face. If the base is viewed from above <term>superata da l'occhio nostro</term>, the small square under the superior **Torus**, being partially obscured by it, must be somewhat larger than the other; but if it is higher than our eyes, the small square above the inferior **Torus** (which is obscured by it) shall be made larger than the other, and also the **Scotia** obscured by the **Torus** in such a case shall be made larger than the given measurements. In these such circumstances <term>accidenti</term>, the **Architect** must be very shrewd and diligent, because **Vitruvius** presupposes that students of his writings are instructed in the **Mathematical** sciences, which make a man astute in many situations.

---

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Doric order. While the previous page introduced the "robust" character of the Doric style and its suitability for churches dedicated to soldier-saints or buildings for men-at-arms, this page moves into the technical debate regarding the proportions of the column base.</meta>

# THE ORDER OF THE BOOK

->**THE PROPORTIONS OF THE BASES**<-

The most knowledgeable masters of the art, such as **Vitruvius** and **Alberti**, say that the orders are five: following their lead, and so as not to interrupt the instruction on bases, I provide here a more exact method of arranging their proportions, to give clear knowledge to those who might need it. One of these shall be the **Doric** base—alternatively called **Tuscan** or **Latin**—which is set here to show how the ancients used its proportions. One of these bases shall have a height equal to half its diameter; for this reason, there is a strong opinion that **Vitruvius** <note>Marcus Vitruvius Pollio, the Roman authority on architecture</note> was either in error or that his books have been corrupted <note>original: *guasti*; Renaissance scholars often blamed "corrupted" medieval manuscripts for confusing passages in Vitruvius</note>. This is because he says that this **Base** should be as high as its entire diameter, which is highly improbable, especially since the buildings of the ancients do not show such a thing; nor does it seem right to a man of good judgment, because the column would sit too high upon it.

But **Vitruvius** seems to speak the truth in one place where he says that the capitals and the bases of columns ought to be half a <term>thickness</term> <note>original: *grossezza*; referring here to the diameter of the column</note>. In another book, in the fourth chapter, **Vitruvius** speaks of a base for the **Doric** column, but one would not want to follow his opinion there, because that same chapter is read in another way: that the **Corinthian** column has its base, and the **Ionic** has its own likewise, and that the **Doric** does not have its own proper base, but that **Ionic** bases are used for them. On this point, the moderns of Architecture have reached an agreement: for having looked at many ancient buildings, such as the **Theater of Marcellus** in **Rome** <note>An ancient open-air theatre in Rome, built in the closing years of the Roman Republic</note> and other buildings—including the theater in **Verona**, and the theater of **Pola** in **Istria** <note>The Roman arena/theater in Pula, Croatia</note>—one sees that these columns have bases. Although many say the **Doric** order should not have bases, they do indeed have them, because they are visible in the **Theater of Marcellus** and the theater in **Verona**. The Romans "based" the **Doric** order, and that base is the true one, as is observed there. I shall give its measurements and how it is to be made, because it seems a very reasonable thing to me, even if it exceeds the opinion of **Vitruvius**, because **Doric** columns in **Italy** do have bases. I will provide an account of these on the following page so they may be seen more clearly.

---

*The diagram shows the profile of a column base. It consists of a square bottom block (the plinth), a large rounded molding (the lower torus), a concave groove (the scotia or "gola"), and a smaller rounded molding (the upper torus) at the top.*

---

This base is half its diameter in height, and is considered by experts to be one of the best for the **Doric** order. Others set the height at one-quarter of the column's diameter, such as the **Attic** base, which is used in the **Ionic** and **Corinthian** orders, as seen elsewhere.

But for the experts, this one is half a diameter in height. To make its parts equal, the diameter of the column is divided into three equal parts; one of these parts forms the height of the base including its <term>plinth</term> <note>original: *zocco*; the square slab at the very bottom of a base</note>. Then, the entire base without the plinth is divided into three equal parts: one of these parts is the **Upper Torus** <note>original: *Toro superiore*; a large convex, semicircular molding</note>. The remaining two parts are divided into six equal parts; from these, the top two parts form the <term>fillet</term> <note>original: *listello*; a small, flat architectural band</note> and the <term>scotia</term> <note>original: *gola*; a concave molding</note>, and from the other four parts, one makes the **Lower Torus**. Its plinth is half the height of the entire base.

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the Doric order. The author continues to compare the "corrupted" classical texts of Vitruvius with the physical evidence of ancient Roman ruins.</meta>

# DORIC

Because **Vitruvius** <note>Marcus Vitruvius Pollio, the 1st-century BC Roman architect whose rediscovered writings became the "bible" of Renaissance architecture</note> has organized this **Doric** order into <term>modules</term> <note>a "module" is a unit of measurement based on the thickness of the column, used to ensure all parts of a building are in proportion</note>, making the column two modules in thickness, and its height—including the base and the capital—thirteen modules. Since the base is one module high, the shaft of the column will be twelve modules, and its capital only one module, which makes thirteen in total. Let the height of the capital be divided into three parts: the first shall be for the **Plinth**, also called the **Abacus**, and this is understood to include the **Cymatium** <note>a decorative molding</note>. The second part is for the **Echinus** <note>the rounded, "egg-shaped" part of the capital</note> along with its **Rings**. The third part is given to the **Necking** <note>original: *hipotrachelio*; the area between the shaft and the capital moldings</note>. The thickness of this necking should be one-sixth smaller than the bottom of the column. The width of the capital at the top should be two modules and one-sixth of a module on each face. This is according to the text of **Vitruvius**.

However, I am led to believe that the text is corrupted regarding the <term>projection</term> <note>original: *Sporto*; how far the moldings stick out</note>, because in effect it appears very meager compared to the ancient ruins that one sees. Therefore, alongside this capital, I will form another according to my own opinion, described more minutely with its own particular measurements. I do this because **Vitruvius** does not provide the specific measurements for the individual members, but passes over them with brevity. I say, then, that having made the three parts of the capital as stated above, the **Plinth** must be divided into three parts: one shall be for the **Cymatium** with its <term>fillet</term> <note>original: *regolo*; a small, flat architectural band</note>. If that part is divided into three again, one will be the fillet and two will be given to the **Cymatium**. The **Echinus** should likewise be divided into thirds, and two-thirds shall be for the **Echinus** itself. The remainder, for the **Rings** (also called fillets), will be divided into three parts, giving one part per ring. The **Necking** shall be as stated above. The projection of every member should be equal to its height; by doing this, the design will have a probable logic and will also be pleasing to the eyes of the beholders.

<meta>This page continues the technical description of the Doric order, moving from the column and capital described on the previous page up to the entablature (the horizontal structure supported by the columns), specifically the architrave, frieze, and cornice.</meta>

-># ORDER<-

Above the capital, the <term>epistyle</term> <note>from the Greek for "above the column"</note>, also called the **architrave**, is to be placed. Its height shall be one **module**, divided into seven parts. One part shall be the **taenia** <note>original: *tenia*; a narrow, flat band or fillet</note>. Beneath the taenia, for the space of one-sixth of a module, let it be divided into four parts: three shall be for the **guttae** <note>meaning "drops"; these are the small, peg-like ornaments</note> and one for the **regula** <note>original: *regolo*; the small block from which the drops hang</note>. There should be six of these drops hanging beneath the **triglyphs** <note>the vertical blocks with three grooves that characterize the Doric frieze</note>. The height of the triglyphs shall be one and a half modules, and their width shall be one module. This width is divided into twelve parts: one part is left on each side for the half-channels, and of the ten remaining parts, six are given to the flat surfaces of the triglyph, and four are for the two central channels.

Between one triglyph and the next, there should be a space of one and a half modules, creating a perfect square. These spaces are named **metopes** by **Vitruvius**. When one wishes to decorate these spaces for greater elegance, they may be carved with various objects, as seen in the square marked **B**, or even with **ox heads**, as shown below. These things are not without meaning: because the ancients sacrificed bulls—at which sacrifices a shallow dish <note>original: *piatto*; referring to a *patera*, a ceremonial libation bowl</note> was used—they placed such things in these locations as ornaments around sacred temples.

Above the triglyphs, their own capitals must be made, the height of which should be one-sixth of a module. Above the triglyphs, the **corona** <note>the "crown" or projecting part of the cornice</note> is placed with two **cymatia** <note>decorative moldings</note>, one above and the other below. If the whole is divided into five parts, three shall be for the corona and two for the cymatia; the total height should be half a module. Above the corona, the **sima** <note>original: *scima*; the gutter or uppermost molding</note> is placed; its height shall be half a module, adding an eighth part of it for its small square block at the top. 

The projection of the corona should be two-thirds of a module. On its underside, above the triglyphs, the **guttae** [drops] should be carved in low relief, as shown in the figure hanging from it. Between one triglyph and the next, the spaces may be left flat or carved with **thunderbolts** <note>a traditional symbol of Jupiter/Zeus</note>. The projection of the sima should be equal to its height, as with all the members except the coronas. Their projection is always made equal to their height, but when coronas have a greater projection (provided the stone can support it), they represent greater **gravity** <note>a sense of weight, dignity, and importance</note>. This is what the ancient Romans observed, and I will provide information on this in its proper place with drawings and measurements.

***

<meta>This page continues the technical specifications of the Doric order. While the previous page focused on the upper entablature (architrave and frieze), this page details the geometry of the column fluting and the proportions of the pedestal (stilobate), including a defense of a specific capital design that differs from Vitruvius.</meta>

-># DORIC<-

<image-desc>On the left side of the page is a vertical architectural drawing of a fluted Doric column. It displays a detailed capital with an abacus and echinus, a shaft featuring twenty vertical flutes (channels), and a base consisting of several moldings resting on a square plinth.</image-desc>

If the column is to be **fluted** <note>original: *striata* or *canellata*</note>, the flutes shall be twenty in number. They are carved in such a way that a straight line is drawn from one side of the flute's space to the other. This line forms the side of a square. By placing the point of the compass at the center of that square and the other point touching either end of the line, the arc drawn will create the proper depth of the flute, which will be the fourth part of a circle. An example of this is shown below at the foot of the column.

And if, for the sake of elevating the column or for some other reason, a **pedestal** <term>Stilobate: the masonry base that supports a row of columns</term> is required—and there is no fixed height it must obey—the width of this pedestal should be equal to the **plinth** <note>the square slab at the bottom of the column base</note>. Its height—that is, the **die** <note>original: *netto*; the central, plain block of the pedestal</note>—is determined by making a perfect square of this width and drawing a diagonal line from corner to corner. The length of this diagonal shall be the height of the die. If this height is divided into five parts, one part shall be added for its **cymatium** <note>original: *cimatio*; the crowning molding of the pedestal</note> and other members, and another part shall be given to its base. Thus, this pedestal will consist of seven parts, proportioned to that height like the column itself. And although the current projection of the **capital** deviates significantly from the writings of **Vitruvius** <note>Marcus Vitruvius Pollio, the Roman architect whose 1st-century BCE treatise "De Architectura" was the foundation of Renaissance architectural theory</note>—by being vertically aligned with the plinth of the base—nevertheless, because I have seen ancient examples of this sort and have even put them into practice myself, I thought it right to include it in the drawing for the satisfaction of those who wish to use it. While those who study only Vitruvius without having otherwise handled ancient remains will deny this opinion, if they consider the **abaci** <note>the flat slabs on top of capitals</note> of **Corinthian** capitals, whose projection aligns vertically with the plinths of their bases, they will not so easily condemn such a design.

<image-desc>On the right side is an illustration of a pedestal (Stilobate) designed to elevate the column. The drawing includes construction lines: a square is drawn at the base of the pedestal's central die, with a diagonal line (diagono) reaching the top corner, illustrating the geometric method used to determine the height of the die as described in the text. Above the pedestal, the base of a smooth-shafted column is shown.</image-desc>

<image-desc>At the bottom center is a diagram of a circular cross-section of a column shaft. The circumference is divided into twenty small semicircular flutes. A small square is superimposed on one of the flutes to illustrate the geometric construction described in the first paragraph for determining the arc of the flute using a compass and a square.</image-desc>

The author also defends his design for capital projections by citing ancient Roman ruins, even where they contradict the strict rules of Vitruvius.</summary>

-># ORDER<-

Because I find a great difference between the <unclear>structures</unclear> <note>original: "cose", literally "things"</note> of **Rome** and other places in **Italy** compared to the writings of **Vitruvius**, I wanted to demonstrate some of them, some of which can still be seen in standing works to the great satisfaction of **Architects**. And although they are small in form and without numbers or measurements, they are nonetheless proportionate to the large ones and have been transferred from large to small with great diligence. The capital, **R**, was found outside **Rome** at a bridge over the river called the **Teverone** <note>The Aniene River, known historically as the Teverone</note>. The capital, **V**, is in **Verona** above a triumphal arch. The capital, **T**, is at a **Doric** temple at the **Tullianum prison** <note>The Mamertine Prison in Rome</note> in **Rome**. The capital, **P**, was found at **Pesaro** along with many other ancient things worthy of praise; its projection <note>the distance the molding extends outward</note>, though large, is nonetheless very pleasing to the viewers. The pedestal, the base, and the capital, **A**, are at the **Forum Boarium** in **Rome**. The cornice, the capital, and the **impost** <term>Impost: the structural member on which an arch rests</term> of an arch, **B**, are at the **Theater of Marcellus**. The cornice, frieze, and architrave, **A**, are at the **Forum Boarium** in **Rome**: all of which things I wanted to demonstrate so that the Architect may choose what pleases him most in this **Doric** order. Now I shall continue with certain specific measurements necessary for the Architect.

---

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the Doric order from page XX, focusing on the mathematical proportions and the placement of decorative elements like triglyphs and metopes. # ORDER

Since the distribution of **triglyphs** <note>Rectangular blocks with three vertical grooves, a hallmark of the Doric frieze.</note> and **metopes** <note>The square spaces, often decorated with carvings, found between triglyphs.</note> is very necessary and also difficult in this Doric order, I will strive to provide as much clarity as possible. I say first that even though in a **hexastyle** work—that is, one with six columns in a portico—the text of **Vitruvius** <note>Marcus Vitruvius Pollio, the ancient Roman architect whose writings were the primary authority for Renaissance builders.</note> says that the **modules** <note>A standard unit of measurement in classical architecture, usually equal to the radius or diameter of a column at its base.</note> for such a distribution should be thirty-five, I do not find that the arrangement can work this way. This is because if one wants to give the middle space four metopes and the other spaces three, the aforementioned number is insufficient. 

But as far as I can determine, there should be forty-two [modules], as can be seen and calculated in the following figure. And likewise in a **tetrastyle** work—that is, one with four columns—the text says the front of the whole work must be divided into twenty-four parts. This cannot be so if one wants to give the middle space four metopes and the two side spaces three metopes each. In my opinion, there must be twenty-seven [modules], as can likewise be seen in the following figure. 

Therefore, having divided the front of the temple into twenty-seven modules:
* Two modules will be the thickness of the column.
* The **intercolumniation** <note>The clear space between two adjacent columns.</note> in the center will be eight modules.
* The spaces at the sides will be five and a half modules each.

Thus the twenty-seven modules will be distributed. Once his triglyph is placed above every column, and the triglyphs and metopes are arranged in the manner given at the beginning of this order, the middle space will have four metopes and those on the sides will have three each. 

Regarding the height of the columns, bases, capitals, and other members, let the rule given at the beginning be observed. However, the height of the **pediment** <note>The triangular gable forming the roof line.</note> should be one-ninth of the length of the **cyma** <note>A molding with an S-shaped curve.</note> which is above the **corona** <note>The flat, projecting part of a cornice that "crowns" the wall.</note>, taking this measurement from the base of "A" <note>Referring to a labeled point in the accompanying diagram.</note> up to just below the cyma of the corona. I mean that measurement below the corona itself. 

The **acroteria** <note>Decorative pedestals for statues or ornaments placed at the apex and corners of a pediment.</note>, called "little pillars," above the pediment should be half the height of the pediment's clear space. Their front should be as wide as the top part of the column, and the middle one should be one-eighth higher than the others. 

Because this Doric door is very difficult to understand, I will describe it in writing and demonstrate it in a drawing as much as I understand of it. The text of Vitruvius says that from the pavement to the **lacunaria** <note>Sunken ceiling panels, also known as coffers.</note>—that is, from the floor of the portico to the ceiling at the base of "A"—the space should be divided into three and a half parts, and two parts should be for the height of the opening, as the text says in my opinion. But because the specific measurements cannot be well demonstrated in a small figure, on the following page I will speak more minutely and demonstrate them in a larger figure.

<summary>The author critiques Vitruvius's mathematical proportions for Doric porticos, arguing that more "modules" are required for proper metope distribution in four- and six-column designs. He also provides specific measurements for pediments and doorways.</summary>

The provided text for translation is unusual. While the prompt states "Translate the following Italian text to English" and the previous page is clearly Italian, the `<lang>` tag indicates "Latin," and the main content of the current page, `qñ dñ qñ dñ...`, is not coherent Italian or Latin text.

`qñ` and `dñ` are common abbreviations in Latin manuscripts. `qñ` typically stands for `quando` (when) or `quoniam` (since). `dñ` typically stands for `domino` (to the lord, by the lord) or `domine` (O Lord). However, a repetition of "when to the lord" or similar phrases does not form a meaningful architectural text in either Latin or Italian.

It is highly probable that the sequence `qñ dñ qñ dñ...` is either:
1.  A transcription error.
2.  A placeholder for text that was unreadable or missing in the original document.
3.  A non-textual element that was literally transcribed.

Therefore, a meaningful translation of the main body text is not possible. I will translate the header and provide an explanation for the main content.

---

Having then made, as I have said, three parts and a half of the pavement up to the <term>coffered ceilings<note>original: 'Lacunarij'—recessed panels in a ceiling or soffit</note></term>; two parts
shall be for the height of the opening, which divided into 12 parts, one shall be for the face of the 
<term>doorframes<note>original: 'Antipagmenti'</note></term> called pilasters, and five and a half parts shall be the width of the opening: and if it is 
16 feet at the bottom, in the upper part let the pilaster be diminished by a third part, and that pilaster
in the upper part be diminished by a fourteenth part ✠ the <term>lintel<note>original: 'supercilio'</note></term> called the architrave shall be of the same 
height: in which one must make the <term>Lesbian cymatium<note>A molding with a S-shaped profile, often decorated with leaf patterns</note></term> with the <term>astragal<note>A small, semi-circular molding, often called a bead</note></term>, which cymatium shall be the sixth part of
that pilaster—I mean the Lesbian astragal in the manner shown in figure A. And the text, although it 
suggests that the cymatium should be made only above the lintel: yet from what I have seen in the ancient ruins, I 
have wanted to make it around the pilaster. Above the lintel, one is to place the <term>hyperthyrum<note>original: 'Hiperthiro'—the frieze or cornice assembly above a door</note></term> in place of a frieze, of the same 
height, in which the text says to carve the Doric cymatium and the Lesbian astragal in the <term>simatous sculpture<note>original: 'sima sculptura'</note></term>, 
which passage is very confused; but in this, I am led to believe the text is corrupted, and where it says *sima* 
*sculptura*, it means *fine* [finished] sculpture—that is, the Doric cymatium and the Lesbian astragal without carving: 
the form and proportion of which are in figures D and A. The text seems to say that the cymatium of the 
flat <term>corona<note>The projecting part of a cornice that sheds rain</note></term> should be at the level of the top capitals: and if so, the corona becomes very large, to which I 
have given as much projection as the height of the lintel, as the text says: though I would never make such a corona 
in any actual work; nevertheless, to treat the ornaments, I wanted to state my own opinion in this and 
demonstrate it with an apparent figure.

## CORRECTION OF THE AFOREMENTIONED PART. ✠

<image-desc>A historiated woodcut initial 'IO' featuring two cherubs or putti standing within a rectangular frame.</image-desc>
I, with more mature counsel, have considered 그at passage of Vitruvius where he says that one should carve 
the Doric cymatium and the Lesbian astragal in the *scima sculptura*: and I have also conferred with some 
Greeks, where it is concluded that *scima sculptura* means <term>low relief<note>original: 'basso rilieuo'</note></term> carving—that is, that the 
works do not have as much projection as others, and thus the sculptures cannot have as much relief. 
And I, having seen similar things in antiquity—where the astragals, leaves, <term>egg-and-dart<note>original: 'vuouoli'</note></term>, and other works 
are in low relief—affirm that this is what it means: that is, carving in low relief.

<image-desc>A faint, large architectural line drawing at the bottom of the page showing the schematic elevation of a portal, including the base, jamb outlines, and the floor line.</image-desc>

-> # BOOK <-
-> ## ORDER <-

Because in truth, in our times, it is not customary to narrow doors at the upper part as the ancients used to do—a practice I do not find fault with for several reasons—nevertheless, some intelligent Architects have made some in that style, which did not please the majority of people. Therefore, if the Architect wishes to make a simple Doric door with little ornamentation, he may observe the order and proportion of the following figure: the opening of which shall be twice as high as it is wide. The <term>architrave</term> <note>original: "pilastrata"; referring here to the jamb or the decorative frame surrounding the door opening.</note> shall be the sixth part of the width of the opening, around which an <term>ovolo</term> <note>original: "Vuouolo"; a convex, rounded molding.</note> will be fashioned with its fillets, which shall be one-fifth the width of the architrave. Although the ovolo on the previous door was a sixth part, I have seen it as a fifth part in an ancient work for a door of medium size, and so I have chosen to make it in this manner. As I have said above, this ovolo should not be made from a quarter-circle, but half as low; a member which <term>Vitruvius</term> <note>The Roman author of "De architectura," the primary source for Renaissance classical theory.</note> calls the <term>Lesbian cymatium</term> <note>original: "Cimatio Lesbio"; an S-shaped molding profile.</note> when comparing his text with ancient remains. The remainder of the architrave is to be divided into 9 parts: five shall be given to the larger fascia, and four to the smaller. Above this architrave, the cornice shall be placed, having a height equal to the width of the architrave, and it should be divided into three equal parts. The first part shall be for the <term>cymatium</term> called the <term>inverted throat</term> <note>original: "Gola rouersa"; also known as a cyma reversa.</note>, with its <term>bead</term> <note>original: "Tondino"; a small, semi-circular molding.</note> and fillets. The second part is for the corona, called the <term>dripstone</term> <note>original: "Gocciolatoio"; the part of the cornice designed to deflect rainwater.</note>, with its smaller cymatium. The third part shall be for the <term>cyma</term> called the <term>upright throat</term> <note>original: "Gola dritta"; also known as a cyma recta.</note>, but an additional eighth part should be added to it. In its <term>projection</term> <note>original: "Sporto"; the distance the molding extends outward from the wall.</note>, the rule given at the beginning of this order should be followed.

### FOURTH BOOK: DORIC

AA IIII

Much has been discussed in the present Book regarding the method of applying the Doric <term>order</term> <note>In classical architecture, an 'order' is a specific style of columns and decoration (like Doric, Ionic, or Corinthian) that follows set rules of proportion.</note>: and indeed, as I have demonstrated, it is such an appropriate thing that it should not only be displayed, but used to enrich a building, house, or palace; which can be done many times, when one desires a certain <unclear>portal</unclear> <note>original: "portamento"; likely referring here to the carriage, design, or presence of a doorway entrance.</note>, and this which is shown in this illustration <note>original: "facciata"; literally 'facade', but here referring to the architectural plate or drawing on the page.</note> shall be according to its order with its <term>cornice</term> <note>The decorative, projecting molding that crowns the top of the door frame or building.</note>.

<meta>Continuing from the previous discussion on the basic proportions of a Doric doorway, where the height was established as double the width. This page expands on that foundation, offering alternative designs and specific geometric instructions for constructing pediments that deviate from strict Vitruvian temple proportions to better suit residential or civic architecture.</meta>

# BOOK ON THE ORDERS

Although Vitruvius <note>Marcus Vitruvius Pollio, the 1st-century BC Roman architect whose "Ten Books on Architecture" served as the foundational textbook for Renaissance builders.</note> in his writings mentions only one type of portal in the Doric <term>order</term> <note>A specific style of classical architecture; the Doric order is characterized by its sturdy proportions and decorative triglyphs.</note>—and even that, as I have demonstrated, quite confusedly in my opinion—it seems appropriate to me that we should not be limited to a single style. Instead, we should employ various manners to enrich a building and satisfy diverse tastes. Therefore, when one must design a portal of specific character and gravity, it may be done in the following manner, observing this rule: the height of the door opening should be double its width. The **pilaster** <note>The vertical frame or jamb of the door opening.</note> should be one-eighth of the opening’s width, and the column one-fourth. This column will appear somewhat tall, yet even if it exceeds the standard proportions, it is not faulty, as it is partially set into the wall; furthermore, looking at ancient examples, some in similar contexts are even more slender. Above the columns, the **architrave** <note>The horizontal beam resting directly on the columns.</note> should be placed, and its height should be equal to the width of the pilaster. The **frieze** <note>The middle section of the entablature, located above the architrave and below the cornice.</note> should be three-quarters of the column's thickness in height. Accordingly, above each column, a **triglyph** <note>A block with three vertical grooves, typical of the Doric style.</note> is to be placed, and between them, four triglyphs and five spaces <note>These spaces between triglyphs are called metopes.</note> should be distributed. For the other specific members, such as the bases, capitals, frieze, triglyphs, and **cornice**, the rules provided at the beginning should be followed.

Because some **pediments**—called *frontespici*—are taller than those Vitruvius described for the fronts of temples, his rule for them shall be as follows: once the cornice is drawn on both sides, divide the upper line in the middle from point **A** to **B**. The midpoint of this line must fall vertically to the center point, which will be **C**. By placing one point of the compass at point **C** and the other at the side of the cornice at **A**, and swinging it round to the side at **B**, the peak of the circular line will provide the proper height for the **pediment** <note>The triangular or curved gable forming the top of the doorway.</note>. This same rule may also be used to create a **segmented** <note>original: "remenato"; referring to a pediment that follows a curved arc rather than forming a triangle.</note> pediment.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram illustrating the construction of a pediment (frontespicio). A horizontal line represents the base of the pediment, with endpoints labeled A and B. A curved arc spans the distance between these two points. A vertical line drops from the peak of the arc down to a point labeled C, which is used as the center point for the compass construction described in the text.</image-desc>

<summary>This page provides instructions for a more decorative Doric portal than those described by Vitruvius, detailing the proportions for integrated columns and a geometric method for determining the height of a pediment using a compass.</summary>
<keywords>Doric order, portal design, Vitruvius, architectural proportions, pediment construction, triglyphs, geometry, Renaissance architecture</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the Doric order from page XXIV, shifting from general measurements of the frieze and architrave to a specific invention for a portal or doorway attributed to Baldassarre Peruzzi.</meta>

# BOOK ON THE ORDERS

Sometimes a mixture, so to speak, is more pleasing to the observer because of its variety than a pure simplicity of its own nature. Hence, it is all the more praiseworthy if a well-proportioned body is formed from different members of the same nature, as can be seen in the following figure. In this figure, <term>triglyphs<note>Rectangular blocks with three vertical grooves, a hallmark of the Doric frieze.</note></term> and <term>mutules<note>Projecting decorative blocks found under a Doric cornice, usually positioned above triglyphs.</note></term> are used in one and the same order—something I have neither seen in ancient ruins nor found written about. However, **Baldassarre of Siena** <note>Baldassarre Peruzzi (1481–1536), a famous Sienese architect and painter known for his study of Roman antiquity.</note>, who was most deeply learned in antiquities, perhaps saw some vestige of it, or else by his own excellent judgment was the inventor of this variety. He placed the triglyphs over the opening because they bear less weight, and the mutules over the solid part of the <term>pilasters<note>Rectangular columns built into a wall.</note></term>, which support the entire weight of the <term>pediment<note>The triangular upper part or gable of a classical building.</note></term>. This arrangement, in my opinion, maintains <term>decorum<note>The classical principle that a building's style should suit its function and status.</note></term> and is pleasing to the eye; it was much praised by **Clement VII** <note>Giulio di Giuliano de' Medici (1478–1534), who reigned as Pope from 1523 until his death.</note>, who was certainly most discerning in all the noble arts.

The proportions of this door shall be as follows: the opening should have a double proportion <note>A 2:1 ratio of height to width.</note>. The face of the pilaster should be one-seventh of the height. The lintel <gloss>supercilio</gloss> should be half of that width. The width of the mutules and triglyphs should be half the width of the lintel, and their height should be double their width. By placing two mutules over each pilaster and four triglyphs over the opening, with the spaces shared equally, these spaces will become perfect squares. Above the mutules and triglyphs, the capitals—or abaci, if we prefer to call them that—are to be placed. Sometimes in a **Doric** cornice these are called mutules. Its height should be a quarter less than the face of the triglyph, but the **cymatium** <note>A molding with a wave-like profile.</note> should be one-third of it. The height of the <term>corona<note>The flat, vertical face of a cornice that acts as a drip edge.</note></term> with its **cymatium** should be equal to the face of the triglyph, and the **sima** <note>The gutter or topmost molding of a cornice.</note>, called the **straight throat** <gloss>gola diritta</gloss>, should be the same. The projection of the corona at the front should be such that the spaces between one triglyph and another at its base are perfect squares; but the projection of this corona from the right and left sides should be half of that at the front. The projection of the **sima** and the **cymatium** should each be equal to their height. The <term>pediment<gloss>fastigio</gloss></term> will be high enough at its topmost part so that if a straight line is drawn from either side of the sima and divided into five parts, one of those parts will be its height. The **Architect** can use this invention not only for a door, but also for various ornaments as the occasion requires.

<image-desc>A technical architectural drawing in the lower half of the page showing the profile and frontal view of a pilaster or column base resting on a tiered pedestal.</image-desc>

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Doric order from the previous page, moving from general proportions to a specific variation involving "rusticated" masonry. It elaborates on the concept of "mixed" styles where classical forms are combined with rougher, more textured elements.</meta>

-># ORDER<-

Although many kinds of doors could be made in the **Doric** order; nonetheless, because the majority of men have always liked—and even today still like—novelty and things not too commonly used: and especially those designs give the most satisfaction which, even though they are **mixed** <note>The author refers to the "mixing" of refined classical orders with "rustic" textures.</note>, remain within their proper boundaries, like this door shown here: in which, even though the columns, the frieze, and its other members are interrupted and covered with **rusticated** <term>opera Rustica: a style of masonry using large, roughly textured blocks with deep joints to create a sense of weight and strength</term> work; nevertheless, one sees in it the entire completed form, with all its proportions: which are these, that the opening of the door be of **double proportion** <note>A 2:1 ratio, where the height is twice the width.</note>, and its width be divided into six and a half parts, and one part shall be given to the pilaster-frame around the door. The column shall be double that: which column with its capital will come to be 14 **modules** <term>module: a standard unit of measurement in classical architecture, usually based on the radius or diameter of a column at its base</term> according to the measure given at the beginning, and this column from the capital downward shall be divided into 13 and a half parts, and each of the bands that cinch the column of **rusticated** work shall be one and a half parts, except those in the lower part that cover the bases, which should be two parts, but the five parts of the column that show the **living stone** <note>original: "viuo d'essa"; referring to the smooth, underlying shaft of the column visible between the rustic bands.</note> shall be one part each, and thus the 13 and a half parts will be distributed. Above the columns shall be placed the architrave, the frieze, and the cornice with the **pediment** <note>original: "frontispiccio"</note>, observing the rule given at the beginning. The **wedges** <term>cunei: the V-shaped stones (voussoirs) forming an arch</term> that occupy the said members shall be seven in number, drawn to their center, which it will be at the discretion of the **Architect** to omit, instead following the order of the **triglyphs** <term>triglyph: a tablet with three vertical grooves in a Doric frieze</term> and the **metopes** <term>metope: the square space between triglyphs, often decorated</term> in the manner given in the door on page 6. And although I have said that **rusticated** work is suited to **fortresses**; nonetheless, inside a fortress I would praise such work for the sake of variety, but not on the outside, as it is work that would easily receive the hits of **artillery** because of its great **relief** <note>The protruding stones of rusticated work would catch cannonballs rather than letting them deflect off a smooth surface.</note>. I say also that this door would be well-suited, in my opinion, for the palace of a soldier, whether in the city or at a villa.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Doric order from page XXVI, transitioning from individual components to the composition of entire facades and temple fronts.</meta>

# BOOK / THE ORDER

[Decorative woodcut initial letter 'M' featuring a landscape background with a classical building and trees.]

My initial thought, in the fourth book, was to treat only the ornaments of the five manners of buildings—namely columns, pedestals, architraves, friezes, and cornices—along with various doors, windows, niches, and other such separate members, intending then to treat whole buildings and their orders in their proper places in the other books. But afterwards, I decided to enrich this volume more by demonstrating various facades of buildings, both of temples as well as houses and palaces, so that greater benefit might be drawn from this work. **And** because (as I said at the beginning) whenever columns have their placement on the floor level of the building, they will be more praiseworthy than if they were upon a pedestal; yet it is possible that sometimes the Architect may not have columns of such thickness as are sufficient for his needs; wherefore it will be necessary to place their pedestals beneath them. Because of this, I have created the following order, which the Architect may use for various ornaments.

The proportion shall be that the opening is double in height compared to its width. The pilastered section, and likewise the arch, shall be a twelfth part of the width of said opening, and the column shall be a sixth part of that same width. The space between one column and the other shall be half of that opening. The width of the <term>niche</term> <note>a decorative recess in a wall for a statue</note> shall be two column thicknesses, and its height shall be double its width. The height of the pedestal shall be three column thicknesses. Its width and the other members shall be as stated at the beginning of this order. The column will be nine parts in height, including the base and the capital, observing the rule already given. The architrave shall be half the thickness of the column, and likewise the face of the <term>triglyph</term> <note>original: "triglipho"; a tablet in a Doric frieze with three vertical grooves</note> shall be the same, but its height including the capital will be double its width. 

**And** so, by placing the triglyphs perpendicular to the columns, with two triglyphs between one column and the other, and five triglyphs in the middle space divided equally, all the spaces will result in perfect squares. The <term>corona</term> <note>the projecting part of a cornice that sheds water</note> and other members shall be as stated at the beginning. The height of the <term>frontispiece</term> <note>original: "fronteſpicio"; the triangular pediment at the top of a facade</note> departs somewhat from the precepts of <term>Vitruvius</term> <note>an ancient Roman architect whose writings were the foundation of Renaissance theory</note>; because I have seen such, and even higher ones, in ancient ruins. This height shall be determined by a straight line from one side of the cornice to the other at its top; divide this into six parts, and one of these shall be the height of the frontispiece—that is, from the <term>cymatium</term> <note>the crowning molding</note> of the corona to its peak. The <term>acroteria</term> <note>original: "Acroteree"; decorative pedestals for statues at the corners or apex of a pediment</note>, or little pilasters as we may call them, which go atop the frontispiece, should be as wide in front as the column is thick at the top, and their height should be the same, excluding their cornice; but the middle one should be higher by a sixth part. And it is not a flaw if the columns are nine parts high, as they are part of the building and bound with the other stones.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Doric order, specifically focusing on the practical application of architectural designs for altars, portals, and windows, transitioning from general rules to specific decorative examples.</meta>

# BOOK
# ORDER

*In this following figure, the discerning <term>Architect|A designer skilled in the theory and practice of building</term> will be able to adapt to various things and transform it according to the circumstances that may occur, and most especially to ornament a painting above an altar, as is customary today in many places in Italy. It could also serve for a <term>triumphal arch|A monumental structure in the shape of an archway, usually built to celebrate a victory</term>, by removing the middle pedestal; similarly, one could ornament a door without the wings on the sides, or even with them; and occasionally for the ornament of a window, or a <term>niche|A decorative recess in a wall</term>, or <term>tabernacle|A small decorative structure or canopy</term>, and similar things. Its proportion shall be thus: let the opening's width be divided into five parts, and one of these shall be the thickness of the column. The band or surround at the sides, above, and below, should be half the thickness of the column. The height of the opening should be seven parts of the column <note>The author uses the "thickness of the column" as the basic unit of measurement, often called a module.</note>; and thus, the base and the capital being one column thickness in total height, it will come to be eight parts in height. The height of the <term>pedestal|The support or foot of a column</term> should be three parts of the column, and its front width as much as the <term>plinth|The lowest square block of a column base</term> of that column. The <term>intercolumniation|The distance between two columns</term> at the side should be as thick as a column, and at the corner of that side there should be a quarter part of a column.*

*The wing on the sides where the niches go should be one and a half columns wide; but the niche itself will be as wide as the column is thick, and let its height be triple its width. The height of the <term>architrave|The main beam resting across the tops of the columns</term> should be half a column; likewise, the <term>triglyph|A block with three vertical grooves in a Doric frieze</term> should be the same in its front width, but its height without the capital should be one and two-thirds squares. Because by placing the triglyphs on the right and left sides perpendicular to the column, and placing five <term>metopes|The square spaces between triglyphs</term> and four triglyphs between them, the distribution will be correct—that is, the spaces will come out as perfect squares. The <term>corona|The projecting part of a cornice</term> and the <term>pediment|The triangular gable topping the structure</term>, and all the other members both below and above, are to be made as stated at the beginning. Although the triglyphs on the sides depart from the writings of <term>Vitruvius|An ancient Roman architect whose books became the foundation for Renaissance architecture</term>, I have seen them on the corners among the remains of the ancients; this, however, is at the discretion of the Architects, whether to include them or leave them out. And although in this fourth book it was not my intention to make any <term>plan|A drawing showing the floor layout from above</term>, as I have to treat them in the other books; nonetheless, for those facades that will be difficult to understand, I will make their plan for greater clarity and the satisfaction of all.*

The author justifies deviating from Vitruvius's strict rules by citing ancient Roman examples and provides a ground plan for clarity.</summary>

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Doric order from the previous page, shifting focus from general rules to a specific application: designing multi-story facades using straight beams (architraves) rather than arches.</meta>

-># BOOK OF THE ORDERS<-

Although the ancients, as far as can be seen, having placed architraves <term>architrave: the main horizontal beam resting directly on the columns</term> upon columns, did not place any other order above them except the pediment <note>original: "frontespicio"</note>, using such an order only for temples and not for other buildings; I do not wish to refrain from creating an order for houses without arches. For if we wish to make arches with their square pillars <term>pilasters: square-shaped columns built into a wall</term>, and also add round columns for further ornament, and desire a bright portico, the arches and pillars will block much of the light. But if we wish to place arches directly upon round columns alone, it would be a most false thing; because the four corners of the arch above a round column would protrude beyond the edge of the shaft <note>original: "passaranno fuori del viuo"</note>. Therefore, not only in this order, but in others as well, I intend to design certain houses or other buildings without arches.

This, then, shall be how it is: let the larger intercolumniation <term>intercolumniation: the space between two columns</term> be four column thicknesses, and the smaller one be one and a half. The height of the columns shall be nine parts, including the bases and capitals. The architrave, frieze, and cornice, and other members shall be made as stated at the beginning. The opening of the windows should be two column thicknesses wide, and their height should be one square and two-thirds. Their pilaster-frames should be one-sixth of the opening, and the cornice above should be aligned with the capitals. The central door should be three parts of a column in width, and the height should be seven parts; in this way, the tops of the windows and the door will all be at the same level. The triglyphs and the metopes <term>triglyphs and metopes: the alternating decorative blocks and spaces in a Doric frieze</term> shall be divided as seen, and this will achieve their proper distribution.

The second order should be one-fourth smaller than the first, and likewise the architrave, frieze, and cornice should be one-fourth smaller than the first, reduced proportionally. The windows of this order should be as wide as those below, including all their pilaster-frames. The ornaments of the niches should be set vertically above the columns, and the openings of these niches should be as wide as the spaces between those columns, and their height shall be two and a half squares.

The third and final order should be one-fourth smaller than the second, and similarly the architrave, the frieze, and the cornice shall be reduced proportionally; but divided by thirds: one part will be given to the architrave, one to the frieze with its modillions <term>modillions: ornamental brackets placed under a cornice</term>, and the other for the cornice; however, the specific measurements for these will be found in the section on the Composite Order. The windows will be as wide as those below, but the niches should be one-fourth smaller than those in the middle, and their height shall be two and a half squares. For the rest of the ornaments, it will be easy to find them with a compass in hand.

<meta>This page continues the technical application of the Doric order. While the previous page discussed the rationale for using architraves instead of arches to allow more light into buildings, this page demonstrates how to stack these orders to create a grand, multi-story facade suitable for a major civic building.</meta>

### BOOK FOUR. THE DORIC ORDER

->H<-

<note>The text at the top of the page is extremely faint, appearing as bleed-through from the back of the page or very light printing.</note>

With this arrangement, we create this facade in the guise of a building for supreme civil justice <note>likely a courthouse or a magistrate’s palace</note>, for such is the nature of this work. It is constructed with the profile of the architrave <term>architrave: the lowest part of the entablature that rests directly on the columns</term> below; however, because we cannot give it its full body in this location—as it belongs to a different order—we must imagine that this section is the cornice <term>cornice: the projecting decorative molding at the top of a wall or building</term> of an order that must stand upon the ground. Above this are the columns of the Doric order, with a height of seven and a half of their diameters. These are used to create the pilasters <term>pilaster: a rectangular column projecting slightly from a wall</term> and half-columns to produce the arrangement of a theater in three levels, one above the other. This design provides such stability that every great mass can be built using these pilasters, as seen in many places in Rome and elsewhere, such as the twenty-six arches <note>this may refer to a specific Roman monument or a contemporary Italian building known for its lead and copper work</note>, the Italian work of lead and copper with these profiles, all of which shall be shown separately by these drawings.

The second story is in proportion to the one below it, standing on its own, with an intercolumniation <term>intercolumniation: the distance between the centers of two adjacent columns</term> of one-half more than its three squares. The columns are two units shorter in height than the columns below, and with their capitals, they shall follow the canon <note>original: "canone"</note> of <unclear>...</unclear>

A detailed architectural woodcut illustrating a three-story facade designed according to the Doric order. The bottom level features a central arched portal with rusticated (rough-hewn) masonry. It is flanked by pairs of engaged Doric columns on pedestals, between which are rectangular windows with molded frames and projecting sills. Above the first story is a complete entablature featuring a frieze with characteristic Doric triglyphs (vertical channeled blocks) and plain metopes (the spaces between them). The second story consists of an arcade of five arches. The central and outermost arches appear as open loggias or windows, while the intermediate two contain recessed arched niches. These are separated by engaged columns. The third story features five bays separated by pilasters: three rectangular windows alternating with two arched niches. The entire composition demonstrates the application of classical proportions and the Doric order to a large civic or residential building, inspired by Roman structures like the Colosseum.

# BOOK FOUR
## THE ORDER

Although in the **Tuscan** order <note>The Tuscan order is the simplest of the five classical orders of architecture, characterized by plain columns and sturdy proportions.</note> on page 13, on the second side, I demonstrated a similar invention, but in **Rustic** work <note>original: "opera Ruftica"; masonry featuring rough-hewn or textured stones</note>; this one is, however, quite different from that. This is because this loggia or portico, as we may call it, is meant to be barrel-vaulted <term>barrel vault: a continuous arched ceiling shaped like a half-cylinder</term>. But where the arches are located, it will be necessary to create cross-vaults <term>cross-vault: also known as a groin vault, formed by the intersection of two barrel vaults</term>, as appears in the plan below. Because the columns could not support the sides of both the barrel and cross vaults—which always push outward—it will be necessary above every column, in the sides of the barrel vault, to place iron tie-rods <note>original: "chiaue di ferro", literally "iron keys"; these are metal bars used to hold walls together and counteract the outward pressure of a vault</note>. However, bronze ones would be more permanent. If they are indeed made of iron, they can be heat-varnished to protect them from rust, baking the finish over the metal. Also, wrapping the part that will be placed inside the wall with sheets of lead or copper will greatly aid its durability.

The proportions of this facade should be made such that the larger **Intercolumniation** <term>intercolumniation: the clear space between two adjacent columns</term> is four column-thicknesses wide, and the smaller one is two. The height of the columns, including the bases and capitals, shall be seven parts. The architrave <term>architrave: the main beam resting across the tops of the columns</term> should be three-quarters of the thickness of a column. Above this, a semi-circle shall be drawn, the face of which should be half a column-thickness at the upper part. Above the arches, the cornice <term>cornice: the decorative projecting molding at the top of the wall</term> should be placed at the same height as the architrave. Between one arch and the other, a window shall be made; its width will be the same as the **Intercolumniation** beneath it, and its frame shall be as wide as the face of the arch. The **Cavetto** and the **Ovolo** <note>original: "Cauetto & il V ouuolo"; the cavetto is a concave molding, and the ovolo is a convex, egg-shaped molding</note> above this window, which are members of the cornice, will project outward slightly over the windows for their ornamentation. 

The width of the door will be two columns plus an additional quarter-thickness. Its pilaster-frame will be one-sixth the width of the opening. For the height of the opening, once the lintel is placed above the pilasters, it should reach just below the necking <note>original: "tondino", the small circular molding</note> of the capitals. Thus, their form will serve as a cornice for the door, and also for the windows at the sides. These windows will have an opening of two column-thicknesses, taking the measurement from the top and not from the bottom in this case. The height of the opening will be one-and-a-half squares, and the niches will be the same height. 

The upper order shall be one-fourth smaller, divided in this manner: the parapet above the cornice shall be one-and-a-half column-thicknesses high. The remainder shall be divided into five parts: one of these will be for the architrave, frieze, and cornice. The niches with their ornaments shall be perfectly aligned above the windows between the arches. If the width is divided into five parts, the columns will take two parts, and the remainder will be for the niche and its pilasters. The cornice above these niches should be as thick as one of these columns, and their bases half a column-thickness. The windows between the niches will have an opening one-fourth smaller than the door and will be of double proportion <note>meaning the height is twice the width</note>. As for the rest of the ornaments, since such work is somewhat mixed, they can be found more clearly in the **Ionic** and **Corinthian** orders. In this composition, the spaces between the **Triglyphs** <term>triglyph: a tablet in a Doric frieze with three vertical grooves</term> will not be perfect squares, because I intend to place three triglyphs above every window and every niche, as can be seen in the accompanying drawing. If any other measurement of the members remains, I always refer back to the rules given at the beginning.

Solid diagonal lines crossing the arch represent iron tie-rods or "keys," labeled with the word "Chiaue" in the center. Small circles at the ends of these lines indicate the anchoring points on the exterior of the structure.</image-desc>

This page continues the discussion of architectural orders, specifically focusing on the construction of a three-story facade with a portico using square columns and the Tuscan or Doric style of proportions.</meta>

-># BOOK<-
->## ORDER<-

And because sometimes there will be someone who will want to build a <term>portico</term><note>A porch or walkway with a roof supported by columns</note>, or <term>loggia</term><note>An architectural feature which is a covered exterior gallery or corridor</note>: and will not want to be deprived of the light that the building receives under said arches: and also because, as we have said in this chapter on page 29, it is a flawed practice to place arches upon <term>round columns</term>: one may instead make a <term>square column</term><note>A pier or rectangular pillar</note> with its <term>base</term> and <term>capital</term> in the manner demonstrated here before: although such a figure shows an entire house in these three arches, a thing that in truth seems to be small and hardly sufficient for a house; this is nonetheless done for one who might have little land: but when the land is greater, the facade may be divided into five arches, and sometimes into seven, which in this same subject will always look well. Which division will be made so that the thickness of four columns fits into one opening. The height of the column shall be six parts of itself including the base and capital: and having placed the arch above them, the face of which shall be half a column; the opening will result in a double proportion. Above the arches one is to place the <term>architrave</term>, the <term>frieze</term>, and the <term>cornice</term>. Let the height of the whole be two thicknesses of a column: and divided into three and a half parts, one shall be given to the architrave, one and a half to the frieze, and one to the cornice; for the other members, the given rule shall be observed. The door should be as wide as two columns, and its <term>pilaster-frame</term><note>The vertical structural element framing the door</note> the sixth part of it: but its cornice should be level with the capitals made of the same members, and thus it will accompany the <term>windows</term> as well: the width of which should be one and a half columns, and its height shall be of diagonal proportion. The corner columns shall be of the same thickness as the others, but their height shall be eight and a half parts.

The second order, which goes above this, should be smaller by a fourth part. The corner columns, and the architrave, the frieze, and the cornice should be diminished by the proportional part: but the windows above the arches should be of the same width as the others below, but their height should be of two squares, and their frames, as was said of the others. The frieze above them should be as wide as the frame, and the cornice just as much. The small windows above them are made for two respects: one is that if the room is of the full height shown from the outside, the ceiling of said room, and also the room itself, will be more luminous: the other respect is that wishing to halve any room <note>creating a mezzanine</note> for more convenience, those would serve for its light.

The third order should be smaller than the second by a fourth part, and then having made five parts of it; one shall be for the architrave, frieze, and cornice; divided by thirds, giving one part to the architrave, one to the frieze, and the third to the cornice, and in the frieze let the <term>modillions</term><note>Ornamental brackets under the cornice</note> be distributed, as can be seen. The light of the windows shall be like the others, but their height shall be a twelfth part more, because they are more distant from the sight <note>An adjustment for perspective foreshortening</note>. The frame shall be like the others, and so the frieze, and the cornice; the <term>frontispieces</term><note>Pediments</note>, and the <term>Remenati</term><note>Curved or segmental pediments</note> shall be as was said further back regarding the Doric doors. And to decorate, and to follow an order at the summit of the facade, these small pilasters can be made, just as this facade is laid out: and in those places where it will be most convenient, the chimneys can be made for the exit of smoke. The spaces between the windows, which remain white, are reserved for paintings at the discretion of the Architect, and at the will of the master of the house. And for greater security of the structure it will be good to place <term>keys</term><note>Structural tie-rods or anchors</note> there, at least across the portico, in the sides of the cross-vaults, in the manner that has been described.

<keywords>portico, square columns, architectural proportions, orders of architecture, window design, tie-rods, mezzanine lighting, pediments</keywords>

, the first is that if the room will be of the full height that it shows from outside, its ceiling should be [so], and also it will be more luminous. The other reason is that, wanting to partition any room for more convenience, those [partitions] would serve for its light. The third order should be smaller than the second by one fourth. And then, making five parts of it, one will be for the architrave, frieze, and cornice; this part then divided into three, giving one part to the architrave, one to the frieze, and the third to the cornice. And in the frieze the modillions should be spaced, as can be seen. The light from the windows will be like the others, but their height will be one twelfth part more, to be more distant from view. The pilastrade should be like the others, and so the frieze, and the cornice. The frontispieces and the R emenati will be made as said further back in the Doric doors. And to adorn, and to follow an order at the summit of the facade, these small pilasters can be made, as this facade is arranged. And in those places that will be more convenient, chimneys for the smoke's exit can be made. And furthermore, the blank spaces that remain white are reserved for paintings at the discretion of the Architect, and at the will of the master of the house. And for more security of the building, it will be good to insert tie-rods, at least across the portico, in the sides of the cross-vaults, in the manner that has been said.
