--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of architectural orders and the placement of chimneys, specifically addressing the unique urban constraints of Venice compared to the rest of Italy.</meta>

-> # BOOK <-
-> ## THE ORDER <-

In this most noble city of Venice, it is customary to build in a manner very different from all the other cities of Italy. Because it is so densely populated, the ground must be tightly utilized and partitioned with great discretion; it is not enough, nor is there room in the buildings, for large courtyards or many gardens. I speak in general terms, though one does see both in some particular palaces. If these spaces could be accommodated, the lighting for the dwellings would be greater and come from various sides; however, lighting is restricted almost entirely to the facades, or to the squares—which they call *campi* <note>Venetian squares are called "campi" (fields) because they were originally unpaved open spaces or grazing lands.</note>—or over the canals, or onto the streets, most of which are narrow and cramped. 

Despite all this, I say that these facades can still be made abundant with light by observing the ancient way of building in the manner shown here. This method involves dividing the opening of an arch into two and a half parts: one of these parts shall be for the front of the pillar, the thickness of which shall be one half, and the round column shall be the same. The height of the arch should be two-thirds of its width added to its height, which will result in a proportion of one square and two-thirds. Furthermore, it could be made of two squares by making the column a little thinner and raising the arch until it is just below the architrave <term>architrave: the main horizontal beam resting on top of the columns</term>. 

The bases and capitals should be made as described at the beginning. The impost <term>impost: the structural member on which an arch rests</term> of the arches should be half a column, using the members of the one demonstrated earlier from the Theater of Marcellus <note>original: "theatro di Marcello" — A famous ancient Roman theater used by Renaissance architects as a standard model for the Doric and Ionic orders.</note>. The door under the portico shall be three column-thicknesses in width, and its height shall be one square and two-thirds, proportioned to the arch. Its pilaster <term>pilastrata: the rectangular pillar-like support around the door</term> shall be one-eighth of the light <note>The "light" refers to the width of the opening.</note>. Its corona <term>corona: the projecting part of a cornice</term> will be level with the capitals; but by adding the straight cyma <term>gola dritta: an S-shaped molding</term>, the frontispiece <note>frontespicio: the ornamental pediment above the door.</note> shall be made in the manner already described, with more or less light above it as the building requires. 

If the building is on a square or another frequented place, shops can be made in the manner shown, corresponding to the rest of the structure. Above the columns, the architrave shall be placed, and its height shall be half a column. The front of the triglyphs <term>triglyph: a tablet in a Doric frieze with three vertical grooves</term> shall be the same, but their height shall be such that, when partitioned in the manner shown, their spaces <note>The "metopes" between the triglyphs.</note> result in a perfect square. Above these, the cornice shall be placed, one-sixth part larger than the architrave. For the specific members, the given rule should be observed. 

The order above shall be smaller by one-fourth; however, a plinth <term>zocco: a block or pedestal</term> is placed under the columns of such a height as the projection of the cornice occupies. Let the remainder be divided into five parts: one of these shall be for the architrave, frieze, and cornice. This is then divided by thirds: one part for the architrave, one for the modillions <term>modiglioni: ornamental brackets under a cornice</term>, and the other for the cornice. 

The columns that support the architrave shall be nine parts in height. The smaller columns that support the middle arches are one-third smaller in thickness than the others; thus, by placing the smaller half-columns leaning against the larger ones, the middle space where the arch is located will be double those at the sides. Once the cornice that supports the arches is placed above the columns, and the semi-circle is made up to the architrave above, with those "eyes" <note>Round windows or oculi.</note> at the sides of the arches, the facade will be extremely luminous and will maintain its dignity (*decoro*). 

Continuing this order in those places where the rooms will be, one can close the middle openings, and the two at the sides will serve as windows. Nevertheless, the order will not be broken from the outside, nor will it break the order inside; for that closed space will be for the chimneys <note>camini</note>. It is said that these should always be made between two windows, representing the face of a man: the windows are the eyes for light, and the chimney represents the nose, which always receives the smokiness.

<summary>This page explains how to adapt classical architectural proportions to the unique, cramped urban environment of Venice, emphasizing how to maximize light in facades. It concludes with a famous Renaissance metaphor comparing the layout of a building's facade to the features of a human face.</summary>
<keywords>Venice, urban density, campi, architectural proportions, Theater of Marcellus, Doric order, triglyphs, architrave, facade lighting, anthropomorphic architecture, chimneys</keywords>

<header>FOURTH.
DORIC</header>
<page-num>XXXII</page-num>

--- Page 68 ---
<meta>Following the description of the Doric order on the previous page, this section moves from individual columns to the practical application of these rules in designing a complete building facade.</meta>

# BOOK ON THE ORDERS

To partition the following facade, its width shall be divided into 14 parts, and one of these shall be for one column. The middle part between one column and the other shall be six column-thicknesses <term>diameter: the width of the column at its base, used as the primary unit of measurement</term>. The other spaces will each be three columns wide. The window shall be one and a half columns wide, and its height shall be two and a half squares. The pilasters <term>pilaster: a rectangular column projecting slightly from a wall</term> should be one-sixth of the "light" <note>the width of the window opening</note>. The windows of the first order should be of the same width. Those below for the ground-floor rooms shall be a perfect square; but those in the middle for the mezzanine rooms <note>original: "stantie ammezade", low-ceilinged rooms located between main floors</note>, as they are called, shall be a square and a half high. The door shall be five column-thicknesses in width, so that the columns have a solid foundation. Its height shall be one square and two-thirds. Regarding the wedge-stones <note>original: "cunei", the V-shaped stones or voussoirs forming an arch</note> and the other stone bindings, one can see and measure them on the drawing. From the bottom under the arch of the door to above the band <term>fascia: a long, flat horizontal strip</term> of the first order, there should be two column-thicknesses.

All the other orders stacked upon orders should decrease by one-fourth in height; but in this case, in my opinion, since the partition of the columns begins above this solid base, it should be of the same height as the first. This is because if the **Rustic** <term>Rustication: a style of masonry using large, rough-hewn blocks with deep joints</term> were a fourth part larger than the middle **Doric**, and the third order a fourth part smaller than the second, this third order would be too diminished, and the first would be too tall.

Therefore, having completed the first order with its band, a **Podium** called a **Parapet** shall be made, of a height equal to one and a half column-thicknesses, upon which the columns shall be placed with the order described above. Their height shall be determined such that, since the second order is as high as the first, once the **Podium** portion is removed, the remainder is divided into 5 parts: four of these shall be for the column, and the other shall be for the architrave, frieze, and cornice. These parts will be partitioned as shown in the drawing, observing the first rule, and thus the columns will have the correct proportion.

The middle space should be divided so that the small columns are half the size of the large ones, and the middle space should be twice as wide as those on the sides. These spaces will be level with the other windows, above which windows "eyes" <note>original: "occhi", round or oval windows</note> shall be made for greater light, as can be seen. Above the two smaller middle spaces, one should either do what is seen in the work, or for better harmony, the same "eyes" at that level. If any other specific members remain, one must always refer back to the first rule.

The third order should be reduced from the second by one-fourth—that is, all the members in proportion—but the windows should all be as wide as those below, and likewise their height. The other members can be diligently found with the compass. The central elevation, without the pediment <note>original: "frontespicio"</note>, shall be half the height of the third order. In the remaining members, as I have said, the ingenious **Architect** will always be able to adapt, and increase or decrease according to his pleasure. And this facade is made according to the custom of **Venice**.

said above: their height will be such that, the second order being of the same height as the first, and having removed the part of the Podium; the remainder shall be divided into five parts, of which four shall be for the column, and the other shall be for the architrave, frieze, and cornice: which parts shall be divided as shown in the design, observing the first rule, and thus the columns will come out in just proportion. The middle space shall be divided so that the small columns are half the size of the large ones, and the middle space shall be twice as wide as those on the sides: which spaces shall be level with the other windows, above which windows, for greater light, eyes (oculi) shall be made as seen: and above the two smaller middle spaces, or if there is what is seen in the work, or for more accompaniment, the same eyes at that level: and if other particular members remain, one must always resort to the first rule. The third order shall be diminished from the second by a fourth part, that is, all members proportionally: but all the windows go as wide as those below, and thus their height, and the other members can be diligently found with the compass. The central elevation, without the frontispiece, shall be half the height of the third order. In the remaining members, as I have said, the ingenious Architect will always be able to adapt, and increase, and decrease at his pleasure. And this facade is made in the Venetian style.

--- Page 70 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Venetian-style facades from the previous section (page XXXIII/XXI), shifting focus from general proportions to the specific structural challenge of balconies (pergoli) and the arrangement of the upper stories.</meta>

# BOOK ON THE ORDERS

*I have demonstrated just before in two ways how the facades of houses can be made in the* **Venetian** *style: but because in such facades the Venetians delight in certain small balconies that project out from the windows, which in that city they call* **pergoli** <note>original: "pergoli"</note>*, and they do this to be able to more comfortably enjoy the waters of the canals and the cool breeze that is continually felt there: because for the most part their houses have their facades facing the said canals. Also, for the triumphs and naval festivals that are often held in that most happy city, these balconies provide great convenience for viewing and represent a grand ornament in those buildings. Nevertheless, they are technically flawed things* <note>The author considers them "vicious" or structurally unsound by classical standards because they lack vertical support.</note> *beyond the utility of the buildings and beyond ornament: because by placing them outside almost as if in the air, they have no other support than the* <term>modiglioni</term> <note>Modillions: ornamental brackets or consoles used to support a weight, such as a cornice or balcony.</note>*, and because also that thing which does not have its stable foundation harms the walls, as the ancients foresaw, who never projected any overhang from them, except for cornices supported by their members or by modillions. I say that if in such buildings we wish to make such things with some logic, it will be necessary that the first wall* <note>The ground floor wall.</note> *be of such thickness that in the second wall, set back into the interior part of the house, there remains the floor of the balconies, as is shown below in the plan: and thus in the middle part, the wall must be made further out, as the middle balcony is wider than those on the sides. And even if one did not want to make the said middle wall of such thickness, one could however make an arch of good strength in the rear part of the house to support the middle part, which remains entirely hollow and of little weight. And this plan is understood to be above the* <term>Rustic</term> <note>The "Rustico" or Rustic style uses heavy, rough-hewn stones, which the author notes is suitable for the "decorum" or appropriateness of buildings sitting in water.</note> *band, which serves the decorum above the waters well. Having then made the first order in the manner demonstrated, the division of this facade above it will be done such that if the middle part by its width shall be three parts, those on the sides shall be three and a half—I mean inside the walls. The height of this second order should be the same as the first, for the reason stated in the previous page. First, one shall make the* <term>Podium</term> <note>A low pedestal or wall serving as a base; here, the balcony railing.</note> *called the* **parapet**, *of a convenient height for leaning. What remains shall be divided into five parts, and one of them shall be for the architrave, frieze, and cornice, divided in the manner stated from the beginning. The width of the middle part shall be divided so that the opening where the semi-circle is above* <note>The central arched window.</note> *shall be double those on the sides, and its height shall be two squares. Thus, having placed the architrave above the columns to support the arch, all the windows will go at that level: and for more convenience of light, and also for ornament of the facade, those* **eyes** <note>Oculi: circular windows.</note> *shall be made, as well as the square windows, as has been demonstrated. Regarding the ornaments of many things, if for the sake of expense one does not wish to make them of marble or other stones, one can certainly supplement this with painting, imitating the truth with artifice. The third order shall be diminished by a fourth part relative to the second, and thus all the members, each in its proportional part, observing the rule given in past matters: and although the* **Ionic** *is placed above this* **Doric**, *a thing which the ancients did in many buildings, its proportion and its measure will nonetheless be found in the* **Ionic** *order.*

<image-desc>A technical architectural floor plan showing the footprint and thickness of a building's wall. The diagram illustrates the varying depths and projections of the masonry required to support the 'poggiuoli' (balconies) discussed in the text. The wall sections are filled with parallel diagonal hatching to represent solid structure.</image-desc>

It also details the proportional relationships between the Doric and Ionic orders when stacking them in a multi-story building.</summary>

<header>DORIC</header>
<page-num>XXXIIII</page-num>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the Doric order, specifically focusing on its application to the design of fireplaces within domestic spaces.</meta>

<header>B O O K</header>

<warning>Two punch holes are visible in the left margin. The text block is followed by a large blank area.</warning>

->THE ORDER<-

<image-desc>A decorative drop-cap "A" featuring a background illustration of a landscape with classical ruins, including columns and a broken archway.</image-desc>

**Although** the judicious **Architect**, having seen so many inventions in the previous parts of this **Doric work**, will know how to adapt to various ornaments for **fireplaces** where the Doric order is required; nevertheless, I will form two versions of this order. One is for the needs of a **room** <note>original: "stantia," an archaic form of "stanza"</note> of good size, built out from the wall with its **modillions** <term>ornamental brackets or blocks under a cornice</term>; the other for a mediocre or small room, contained entirely within the thickness of the wall. This is because a mediocre or small room might sometimes be too crowded by a fireplace with modillions, and also because another fireplace could be placed above it, so that two fires can use the same chimney flue; in that case, it is necessary that the lower one be entirely within the thickness of the wall.

Therefore, if it is to be made in the Doric order, establish the height of the opening according to the height of the room, as the Architect sees fit. Let said height be divided into four and a half parts, and let one of these be the face of the **pilaster** <note>the vertical side-piers of the fireplace</note>, but the **architrave** <note>the horizontal beam spanning the opening</note> shall be half of that. The **fillet** <note>original: "quadretto"</note> or **listel** <note>original: "regolo"</note> that surrounds it should be one-seventh part, and let all the other listels be of the same width. The face of the modillions and of the **triglyphs** <term>blocks with three vertical grooves, a primary feature of the Doric order</term> shall be half the width of the architrave. As for its height: once the width of the fireplace is determined by the needs of the room and the modillions are placed above the pilasters as seen in the illustration, let the middle space be divided so that the spaces between one triglyph and another are equal to the height of the architrave, and let their height be the same. In this way, the spaces will be a perfect square, which looks well, and the triglyphs will have a double proportion—that is, two squares high. However, the angular spaces between the modillions cannot be made into perfect squares. 

The height of the capitals of the triglyphs and of the modillions (which are themselves called modillions) shall be half of the modillion itself. The **corona** <note>the flat, projecting part of the cornice</note> with the **Sima** <note>the uppermost molding, often an S-curve</note> and the **Cymatium** <note>the crowning molding of the cornice</note> shall have a height equal to the architrave. This height is divided into two equal parts: one shall be for the corona, and the remainder divided into three parts; one part shall be given to the cymatium with its fillet, and the rest shall be for the sima and its fillet. The projection of the corona shall be such that its underside between one triglyph and another forms a perfect square, so that if one wishes to carve a **rose** <note>a rosette ornament</note> there, the space is large enough. The projection of the sima and the cymatium shall be equal to their height. The ornaments above the **cornice** may be made at the Architect’s pleasure, and it can also be made without them. These given measurements will work well whether the work is of medium or large scale; but if it is of a small form for a small room, the face of the pilasters must be made as one-seventh of the height of the opening, and all other members proportioned to those following the rule given above.

<vocab>Architect, Doric work, fireplaces, modillions, room, pilaster, architrave, fillet, listel, triglyphs, Sima, Cymatium, corona, cornice</vocab>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Doric order from the previous page, transitioning from general principles to a specific practical application: the design and proportioning of a wall-mounted fireplace. The previous page’s translation was fragmented, but the context remains the architectural standards of the Fourth Book.</meta>

# BOOK OF THE ORDER

This fireplace, projecting from the wall, is to be made in this way: according to the capacity of the <term>room|stantia</term>, once the appropriate height and width are set, the height from the floor to the underside of the <term>architrave|the lowest part of the entablature or mantelpiece</term> should be measured into four parts. One of these parts shall be given to the architrave, <term>frieze|the middle section of the mantelpiece, often used for carving or decoration</term>, and <term>cornice|the uppermost projecting molding of the mantelpiece</term>, dividing these members according to the rules given at the beginning. And although this figure shows these members at a greater height, this is caused by the lower viewpoint; because in viewing such a work from below upward, the eye takes in more than the given measurements. The face of the <term>modillions|decorative brackets or consoles that support the mantel</term> shall be one-seventh of their height, and the capital half of that face, and it should be divided as was said of the <term>Doric capital|the topper of a column or bracket in the Doric style, known for its sturdy and simple design</term>. Some diminish the thickness of this modillion at the bottom by a fourth part, because the foot spreads outward by that fourth part in such a way that the <term>plinth|zocco; the square block at the base</term> under the foot comes to be as wide as the upper part. But if you wish to make such a modillion of a single thickness throughout, I would praise this in a large work; because that part which retreats further toward the wall is further from the sight, and of itself it appears to diminish. And because that part which receives the smoke, which rises in a <term>pyramidal|tapering upward like a pyramid</term> shape, would not be pleasing to the eye at a great height, one could place another <term>order|a specific style or level of architectural decoration</term> above the first cornice, of more or less height as it seems fit to the <term>Architect</term>, and also according to the height of the room. And these proportions can be maintained in a large form; but if this is to be made in a medium or small form, let its height from the architrave to the floor be divided into five parts, and let one be given to the architrave, the frieze, and the cornice according to the same rule mentioned above. Thus, the height of the face of the modillion shall be one-ninth part, and also the capital half of it; in this way, in a medium or small form, it will turn out more graceful. And I say this from experience, for having had some made in a small form for chambers and having observed the first rule, those works turned out too <note>original: "sode"; meaning bulky, heavy, or overly massive</note> solid; but with this second rule, the small forms turn out more pleasing and more elegant.

<summary>This page provides technical instructions for designing a Doric-style fireplace, focusing on the proportional relationships between the mantelpiece elements and the room size. The author offers two sets of rules: one for large installations (1/4 ratio) and a modified one for smaller rooms (1/5 ratio) to ensure visual elegance.</summary>

--- Page 76 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the classical orders, moving from the Doric (found on previous pages) to the Ionic order. It establishes the symbolic associations of the order before detailing the mathematical proportions of the column base.</meta>

# BOOK ON THE IONIC ORDER AND ITS ORNAMENTS, CHAPTER VII

*Vitruvius* <term>Vitruvius<gloss>Marcus Vitruvius Pollio, a Roman architect and engineer of the 1st century BC whose treatise "De architectura" was the foundational text for Renaissance architecture.</gloss></term> discusses this *Ionic* work in his fourth book, first chapter. The ancients derived this style from the matronly form <note>In the hierarchy of the classical orders, the Ionic is considered "feminine," representing the proportions of a mature woman (the matron), whereas the Doric represents a man and the Corinthian a young maiden.</note> and dedicated it (as was said at the beginning of this book) to *Apollo*, *Diana*, and *Bacchus*. However, we *Christians*, if we have to build any sacred temple in this order, shall dedicate it to those saints whose lives were between the robust and the tender; likewise to those female saints who were of a matronly life. And if any building, whether public or private, is to be made for men of letters and of a quiet life—neither robust nor indeed tender—this *Ionic* order will be suitable for them. Also, if anything is to be made for matrons, this manner will be appropriate.

Now we come to the measurements and proportions of this species. The *Ionic* column, as a general rule, shall be made of eight parts, including its base and capital—although *Vitruvius* describes it as being eight and a half parts high. Sometimes it will be made of nine parts or more, depending on the site and the composition of the buildings. But this one (as I have said) should be made of eight parts, one of which will be its thickness at the bottom. Thus, its base will be made half as high as that thickness. *Vitruvius* describes this base diligently in his third book, second chapter, in this manner: that the height of the said base should be half the diameter of the column, but the **Plinth** <term>Plinth<gloss>original: "Plintho"; the square block that serves as the lowest part of the column base.</gloss></term> should be one-third of that height. Once the **Plinth** is removed, the remainder is divided into seven parts: three of those shall be given to the **Torus** <term>Torus<gloss>original: "Toro"; a large, convex, semi-circular molding.</gloss></term>, and the other four shall be for the two **Scotias** <term>Scotia<gloss>A concave, "hollow" molding between the fillets of the base.</gloss></term> and their **astragals** and fillets <note>original: "quadretti"</note> in this way: those four parts are divided equally, and each of those parts will have one astragal with its fillets. The astragal shall be one-eighth part, and the fillet half of that astragal. Although each **Scotia** will be of the same height, nonetheless the lower one will appear larger because of its projection <note>original: "proiettura"</note>, which will spread further out than the other. The said projection, called the **Sporto**, will be one-eighth and one-sixteenth part on each side. Thus, the **Plinth** will be one-fourth and one-eighth part wider on each side than the thickness of the column. And because the fillet under the **Torus** is occupied by so much of the bulk of that **Torus**, it seems to me that it should be made twice as large as the others, observing in all members that discretion which was mentioned regarding the **Doric base**.

->Upper **Torus**, called a staff or roundlet,<-

->**Fillet**, called a listel or ruler,<-

->**Scotia**, or **trochilus**, called a hollow,<-

->**Astragal**, called a roundlet,<-

->**Scotia**,<-

->**Plinth**, called a block<-

<summary>This page introduces the Ionic order, defining its "matronly" character and providing the specific mathematical proportions for constructing an Ionic column base according to Vitruvian principles.</summary>

<meta>This page follows the introduction to the Ionic order. The previous page ended while discussing Vitruvius's descriptions of column height; this page transitions into specific critiques and measurements for the Ionic base and capital.</meta>

->## FOURTH BOOK. IONIC<-

Because the *Ionic* base described by *Vitruvius* <term>Vitruvius<gloss>Marcus Vitruvius Pollio, the Roman author of "De architectura," whose rules were the standard for Renaissance architects.</gloss></term> does not satisfy most men—both because the <term>torus<gloss>a large, rounded, convex molding located at the base of a column</gloss></term> is very large, and because the <term>astragals<gloss>small, bead-like circular moldings</gloss></term> are too small beneath such a large member in the judgment of many experts who have often debated this matter—I will, with great reverence and respect for such a great author, form one according to my own opinion. Once the <term>plinth<gloss>the flat, square block that serves as the very bottom of the base</gloss></term> is made, as was said of the other base, the remainder should be divided into thirds. One part shall be given to the *torus*. The part below the *torus* shall be divided into six parts: one of these shall be for the *astragal*, and its square fillet shall be half the size of that *astragal*. The fillet below the *torus* should be the same size as the *astragal*. The remainder shall be for the <term>scotia<gloss>a deep concave molding between two fillets, which casts a strong shadow</gloss></term>, also called a <term>trochilus<gloss>the Greek term for a scotia</gloss></term> or <term>cavetto<gloss>a simple concave molding with a curve that is a quarter-circle</gloss></term>. The third remaining part shall be divided into six parts: one shall be the *astragal*, and its fillet shall be half that *astragal*, and the fillet below it, above the *plinth*, shall be the same size. The remainder shall be for the lower *scotia*. The projection shall be as described for the other base, and with those lines shown below.

<note>The author is proposing a modification to the traditional Vitruvian "Attic" or "Ionic" base, seeking better visual balance by adjusting the proportions of the moldings.</note>

**[Architectural diagram of an Ionic base with geometric construction lines and circular arcs indicating the proportions of the torus, scotias, and astragals.]**

The *Ionic* capital shall be made in this way. Its height should be one-third the thickness of the column, and the face of the <term>abacus<gloss>the flat slab at the top of a capital</gloss></term> should be as wide as the <term>imoscapo<gloss>the diameter of the bottom of the column shaft</gloss></term>. However, it should be divided into 18 parts. Then, add one part to the two sides—that is, half a part per side—making 19 parts in total. But, moving inward one and a half parts per side, a line called the <term>cathetus<gloss>a vertical line used as an axis for drawing the spiral of the volute</gloss></term> should be drawn, which will be at 9.5 parts, representing half the width of the capital. This width is divided into those 9.5 parts: of these, one and a half shall be for the *abacus* made in whichever style the architect prefers, whether on the right or left side, as both are ancient. The eight parts below the *abacus* shall be for the <term>volute<gloss>the iconic spiral scroll of the Ionic order</gloss></term>, called a *Viticcio* <note>literally "tendril"</note> by the Tuscans, and others call it a *Cartozzo* <note>meaning "scroll" or "rolled paper"</note>. And because in such a small figure, and especially in the "eye" <note>the center of the spiral</note>, it would be difficult to place the numbers and the method of making it, I will demonstrate it more clearly in writing and drawing on the following page. I will also show the way to make the *striae* of the column—that is, the <term>fluting<gloss>the vertical grooves carved into the column shaft</gloss></term>—and the side view of the capital will be shown. But as for the column, if it is 15 feet or less, the upper part should be diminished by one-sixth, according to the rule given for all columns in the *Tuscan* section. And if it is from 15 feet up to 40 feet, read *Vitruvius* in the third book, second chapter, who demonstrates it diligently.

**[Diagram of an Ionic capital in front elevation, showing the abacus, the spiral volutes, and the decorated echinus. Labels point to the 'abacus,' the 'Volute,' the 'Top of the column shaft,' the 'Vertical axis' (cathetus), and the 'Bottom of the column shaft.']**

<summary>This page details the author's preferred proportions for the Ionic base and capital, offering a specific critique of Vitruvius's measurements to achieve better visual harmony. It introduces technical terminology for the moldings and provides initial geometric instructions for constructing the volutes.</summary>

<keywords>Ionic order, column base, Ionic capital, Vitruvius, architectural proportions, volute, torus, scotia, abacus, cathetus, fluting</keywords>

--- Page 78 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical demonstration of the Ionic order. While the previous page discussed the base of the column, this text moves to the most recognizable feature of the Ionic style: the volute (the scroll-like ornament) of the capital. Although the header says "Book I," the content remains consistent with the Fourth Book of Serlio's treatise on the Five Orders.</meta>

# BOOK OF THE FIRST ORDER

Having thus formed part of the <term>Ionic capital<note>The decorative top of a column characterized by scroll-like spirals.</note></term>, as I have demonstrated, there remains the <term>volute<note>The spiral, scroll-like ornament on an Ionic capital.</note></term>. It shall be made in this way: from the line called the <term>cathetus<note>A vertical line used as a reference point in architectural drawing; here, it passes through the center of the spiral.</note></term> beneath the <term>abacus<note>The flat slab forming the uppermost member of a capital.</note></term>, which is divided into eight parts from the abacus downward, four parts shall be left. Below these, one part shall form the eye, and below that three parts remain, making eight in total. The eye shall be divided into six parts, and the numbers placed as seen in the figure. Place one point of the compass on number 1 and the other point beneath the abacus, circling downward to the cathetus. Then, keeping the compass point there and placing the other on number 2, circle upward to the cathetus. Then fix one point of the compass and place the other on number 3, circling downward to the cathetus. Then fix a <term>point<note>original: "pũta"</note></term> and place the other on number 4, circling upward to the cathetus. Fix the compass there and place the other point on number 5, circling downward to the cathetus. Finally, fix the compass there and place the other point on number 6; by circling upward, it will intersect the circular line of the eye. Within this formed volute, on both the right and left sides, a <term>rosette<note>A rose-shaped decorative carving often found in the center of the volute's eye.</note></term> shall be made. The other particular measurements can be clearly <term>understood<note>original: "cõprender"</note></term>, and with a compass in hand, one may measure everything. The channels of the column, called <term>fluting<note>original: "canellature"; the vertical grooves carved into a column shaft.</note></term>, shall be twenty-four. One of these parts shall be divided into five: four shall be given to the channel, and one shall be its flat edge. Thus, from one edge to the other, a straight line shall be drawn, the middle of which will be the center of that fluting. However, if sometimes for the sake of elegance a column is desired to appear thicker, the fluting shall be twenty-eight; because the line of sight, expanding through a greater number of channels, comes to lengthen and makes that thing appear larger than it is through artifice. The abacus of this capital (as I have said) is the same on the side as it is on the front, but its side is the one marked "A" here nearby. It is a companion in measurement and proportion to the one on the previous page. Discreet reader, I have brought this volute to that end which my weak genius has been able to achieve, for the text of <term>Vitruvius<note>Marcus Vitruvius Pollio, the ancient Roman architect whose writings are the foundation of Renaissance architecture.</note></term> is difficult to grasp—especially as the author promised us the figure of this in the final book, along with other beautiful things, which book is now lost.

---

->Revision with the addition of the above-written part.<-

Once the part of the Ionic capital is formed as I have shown, the volute remains, which shall be made thus: let the line called the <term>cathetus</term> beneath the abacus be divided into eight parts from the abacus downward. One of these shall be for the eye, four parts shall remain above the eye, and three parts below the eye, totaling eight. Let the eye be divided into six parts, and the numbers placed as seen in the figure. Place one point of the compass on number 1 and the other beneath the abacus, circling downward to the cathetus. Fix the compass point there and place the other on number 2, circling upward to the cathetus. Fix one point of the compass and place the other on number 3, circling downward to the cathetus. Fix one point of the compass and place the other on number 4, circling upward to the cathetus. Fix one point and place the other on number 5, circling downward to the cathetus. Fix one point and place the other on number 6; circling upward, it will join the circular line of the eye. Within the eye of the formed volute, on the right and left sides, a rosette in low relief shall be made for ornament. But note here, reader, that there are many things which are poorly demonstrated in theory if the prudent <term>Architect</term> does not help himself with practice, though always taking his principles from theory. Having shown you the theoretical way to make the volute, it now remains to make its <term>ribbon<note>original: "cinta"; the raised band or molding that defines the spiral shape.</note></term> marked "B," and to make it diminish proportionally like the volute. The method for making this ribbon is that said ribbon should be as wide as one-third of the eye—that is, part "B" beneath the abacus. In the eye, between number 1 and number 3, place one point of the compass, and the other point beneath the ribbon, circling downward to the cathetus. Fix the point there and place the other between number 2 and number 4, circling upward to the cathetus. Fix the point there and place the other on number 1, circling downward to the cathetus. Fix the point and place the other on number 4, circling upward to the cathetus. Fix the point and place the other on number 5, circling downward to the cathetus. Fix the point and place the other on number 6; circling upward, the lines will join at the top of the eye. This matter (as I have said) consists more in practice than in art, because making it diminish more or less lies in the discretion of the Architect in placing the point of the compass a little higher or a little lower.

<column-break/>

The measurement of this ribbon will not always be the same: if the capital is of a large size, the ribbon will look well at a quarter of the eye's width; if it is of medium size, the ribbon will look well at a third of the eye; if it is of a small form, the ribbon can be half the width of the eye. This will always be at the will of the judicious Architect, because in the antiquities I have seen many volutes, and they are all varied in measurement and workmanship. The other particular measurements can be clearly understood, and with compass in hand, one may measure everything. The channels of the column, called fluting, shall be twenty-four. One of these parts shall be divided into five: four shall be given to the channel, and one shall be its flat edge. Thus, from one edge to the other, a straight line shall be drawn, the middle of which will be the center of that fluting. But if sometimes, for the sake of elegance, one wishes to make a column appear thicker, the channels shall be twenty-eight; because the line of sight, expanding through a greater number of channels, is lengthened and makes the thing appear larger than it is through artifice. The abacus of this capital, as I have said, is as wide in the side as in the front, but its side is marked "A" here. It is a companion in measurement and proportion to the one on the previous page. Discreet reader, I have brought this volute to that end which my weak genius has been able to achieve, for the text of Vitruvius is difficult to grasp, especially since the author promised the figure of this, along with other beautiful things, in the last book, which cannot be found. Regarding this, there are various opinions. Many say that in the time of Vitruvius there were some ignorant Architects who were more lucky than skilled, as there still are in our own times; for presumption, the sister of ignorance, has such force among the multitude of those who do not understand that the wise are suppressed by such people and held in little esteem, and for this reason Vitruvius did not want to give these things to the public, so as not to teach his rivals. Others say that these things were so beautiful and satisfying that they were kept hidden by those who possessed his writings. Some others have suggested that the figures were so difficult to describe and to put into drawings that the author refrained from including them in his writings. But this last reason I do not affirm, for Vitruvius was so knowledgeable, as his writings bear witness, that he would not have written anything he did not understand, both for himself and to teach others.

<summary>This page provides a detailed technical guide for drawing the Ionic volute using a compass and a system of eight parts, while discussing the visual effects of column fluting. The author also reflects on the mystery of Vitruvius's lost architectural illustrations, blaming either the envy of rivals or the disappearance of ancient manuscripts.</summary>
<keywords>Ionic volute, Vitruvius, architectural drawing, compass technique, column fluting, architectural theory vs practice, cathetus, abacus, rosette</keywords>

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Ionic Order, moving from Vitruvius's theoretical rules to practical Roman examples and the specific problem of arranging Ionic capitals at the corners of a building.</meta>

# BOOK OF THE ORDERS

I have demonstrated previously how to make the Ionic capital according to the text of **Vitruvius** <note>Marcus Vitruvius Pollio was a 1st-century BC Roman architect whose treatise "De architectura" became the foundation for Renaissance architectural theory.</note> as far as I understand it; now I will show how some were made by the ancient **Romans**. Of **Capital M**, which is still in place at the **Theater of Marcellus** <note>An ancient open-air theater in Rome; its well-preserved exterior provided Renaissance architects with a standard model for the Doric and Ionic orders.</note>, I will give some general measurements. The front of the abacus <term>abacus: the flat slab forming the top of a column's capital</term> is equal to the width of the column at its base. The volutes <term>volute: the decorative spiral scroll characteristic of the Ionic order</term> project outward by a sixth part of this abacus, and hang down by half of the abacus. The height of the capital is one-third the width of the column at its base. Because such capitals seemed to some architects to be poor in ornament, they added this frieze <note>original: "fregio"; here referring to a decorative band or necking added below the volutes</note>, which is shown in **Capital P**, making the height of the capital two-thirds of the column's base width; this capital can be seen at present in **Rome**, along with many others of this type.

[image-desc: Three detailed architectural drawings showing the Ionic order. On the left is the front elevation of an Ionic capital labeled "M". In the center is a side profile of a similar capital, showing the scroll of the volute and the profile of the abacus. On the right is a more ornate version of an Ionic capital labeled "P", which includes a decorative horizontal band or frieze between the volutes, embellished with leaf and floral motifs.]

Because it might sometimes happen that an architect has to make a square cloister with Ionic columns, or a palace courtyard, if he is not careful with the corner columns, some of those columns will have the front of the volutes facing the courtyard, and some will have the sides of the volutes facing the courtyard; this has happened to some modern architects. But to avoid falling into such an error, it will be necessary to make the corner capitals as shown below in **Plan A**. One of these capitals was found in **Rome**, which gave many people much to think about, nor could it be understood for what purpose it was made, so that they called it the "capital of confusion." Nevertheless, after much dispute, it was concluded that it had been used at an interior corner of a colonnade as I have said. And if one has to make columns on the exterior corners, so that the fronts of the volutes are seen from every side of the building, one can do as shown below in **Plan B**.

[image-desc: Two architectural floor plans demonstrating column placement. The diagram on the left (A) illustrates an interior corner of a colonnade, showing how specific angular capitals are used to maintain symmetry. The diagram on the right (B) shows an exterior corner of a structure, illustrating the orientation of the volutes to ensure they face outward on both sides of the corner.]

<summary>This page compares the proportions of Vitruvian Ionic capitals with Roman archaeological examples from the Theater of Marcellus and offers a technical solution for orienting Ionic volutes at the corners of a building.</summary>
<keywords>Ionic Order, Vitruvius, Theater of Marcellus, volute, abacus, corner capital, Roman architecture, architectural proportion, Renaissance architecture</keywords>

<meta>This page follows the discussion of the Ionic capital, transitioning from the column head to the details of the entablature—the horizontal structures that rest upon the columns.</meta>

# XXXIX
### FOURTH BOOK: IONIC

*The **epistyle** <term>epistyle: the Greek term for the architrave, the main beam resting on the columns</term>, called the **architrave**, is to be made in this manner: if the column is from twelve to fifteen feet in height, the architrave should be half the thickness of the column at its base. If the column is from fifteen to twenty feet in height, let it be measured in thirteen parts, and one shall be the height of the architrave. Again, if it is from twenty to twenty-five feet, let its height be divided into twelve and a half parts, and one shall be given to the architrave. Furthermore, if its height is from twenty-five to thirty feet, the architrave shall be made the twelfth part of such height. And as the columns become greater in height, the architrave shall be made larger by a proportional part, because those things that are farther from the view lose that much more of their magnitude, being surrounded by the spacious air. Once the architrave is made to its proper height, let it be divided into seven parts, and one of these shall be the **cymatium** <term>cymatium: a molding that serves as a cap or crowning feature</term>, called a **reverse cyma** <note>original: "gola rouerſa"; an S-shaped molding that curves outward at the bottom and inward at the top</note>, and its projection should be the same. The remainder shall be divided into twelve parts: three shall be given to the first **fascia** <term>fascia: a long, flat horizontal band</term>, four shall be for the second, and five shall be given to the third. The thickness of this architrave at the bottom part shall be like the column at its top shaft, but the thickness of the architrave at the top part shall be like the column at its lowest shaft. The **zophoros** <note>original: "zophoro"; Greek for "bearing life," referring to the frieze which often contained relief sculptures of figures or animals</note>, called the **frieze**, if it is to be carved with anything, shall be made one-fourth higher than the architrave; but if it is made without sculpture and plain, it must be one-fourth part smaller than the architrave. Above the frieze, let its cymatium be placed, the height of which shall be a seventh part of it, and its projection shall be as much as the height. Above the cymatium, let the **dentil** <note>original: "denticolo"; a decorative block molding resembling a row of teeth</note> be placed, and let its height be as much as the middle fascia. The projection of these shall be as much as their height. Its face should be twice as high as its width, and the hollow gap between one and the other should be a third part less than its width. The cymatium of this should have a sixth part of it. The **corona** <term>corona: the projecting part of a cornice that serves as a dripstone</term> with its cymatium, except for the **cyma** <note>original: "sima"; the uppermost molding of the cornice</note>, shall be as high as the middle fascia. The projection of the corona with the dentil shall be as much as the height of the frieze with its cymatium. The cyma, called the **upright cyma** <note>original: "gola dritta"; an S-shaped molding that curves inward at the bottom and outward at the top</note>, shall be as much as the corona and an eighth part more. Its square <note>original: "quadretto"; referring to a small flat molding or fillet</note> shall be a sixth part of it, and its projection shall be as much as the height. And thus every member of the cornice, except the coronas, will always turn out well if its projection is equal to its height.*

# BOOK
## THE ORDER

<image-desc>On the left side of the page is a detailed technical engraving of a complete Ionic column. The drawing shows a square pedestal with decorative moldings, an Attic-style base, a fluted shaft, and an Ionic capital with volutes. Above the capital is a full entablature comprising an architrave, a pulvinated (convex) frieze, and a cornice featuring modillions and other classical moldings.</image-desc>

*Because the antiquities of Rome are very different from the writings of **Vitruvius** <term>Vitruvius: a Roman architect and engineer whose 1st-century BC treatise became the essential textbook for Renaissance builders</term>; I shall form another column, upon which the architrave, the frieze, and the cornice will be placed. The height of the entire entablature should be one-fourth of the height of the column. If this height is divided into ten parts: three shall be for the **architrave** (divided in the manner previously described <note>The author refers to the division into three horizontal bands, or fasciae, discussed on the previous page</note>), three shall be given to the **pulvinated** frieze—that is, one that is rounded or convex—and four to the **cornice**. This cornice shall be divided into six parts: one shall be given to the **dentil** <term>dentil: a series of small, tooth-like rectangular blocks</term>, one to the **cymatium** <term>cymatium: a molding that serves as a cap or crowning feature</term> that supports the **modillions**, two shall be given to the modillions <term>modillions: decorative brackets or blocks placed under the projecting part of the cornice</term>, one to the **corona** <term>corona: the flat, vertical face of the cornice that acts as a drip edge to shed water</term>, and the last to the **sima** <term>sima: the uppermost molding, often serving as a gutter</term>. The projection of the whole should be at least as great as its height; a similar cornice was found at **Santa Sabina** <note>Santa Sabina: a 5th-century basilica in Rome famous for its reuse of ancient Roman columns and architectural details</note> in Rome, belonging to an Ionic order.*

<image-desc>On the right side of the page are two separate architectural engravings providing detailed profiles and front views of the column's components. The top engraving details the capital and entablature, while the bottom engraving focuses on the pedestal and the column base.</image-desc>

*If it should sometimes be necessary to raise the columns higher, and if you are not restricted by the need to match other surrounding structures, the proportions of the **Pedestal** shall be as follows: its front face should align vertically with the **Plinth** <term>plinth: the square, lowest part of the column's base</term>, and the height of the **die** <note>original: "netto"; the plain, central block of a pedestal between the base and the cap</note> shall be one and a half squares. If this height is divided into six parts, one part shall be given to its base and another to the cornice on top, making eight parts in total. Thus, this Pedestal will be eight parts in proportion to the column, which itself is also eight parts high. This should always be understood as a general rule, always leaving many matters to the discretion of the prudent Architect.*

<summary>This page defines an alternative Ionic entablature and pedestal based on Roman archaeological examples rather than Vitruvian theory, establishing a 1:4 ratio for the entablature and an 8-part proportion for the pedestal.</summary>

# FOURTH BOOK: IONIC

Because of the great differences I find between the buildings of **Rome** and the things described by **Vitruvius** <term>Vitruvius: a Roman architect and engineer whose 1st-century BC treatise became the essential textbook for Renaissance builders</term>; I have chosen to demonstrate some of the most notable examples, some of which can still be seen in **Rome** today, standing in place. The cornice, the frieze, and the architrave marked **T** are from the Theater of Marcello, specifically the **Ionic** work positioned above the **Doric** order. The small pilaster <term>pilaster: a rectangular column projecting slightly from a wall</term> with the base shown above, also marked **T**, belongs to the same order beneath the **Ionic** columns. The cornice acting as an impost <term>impost: the block or molding upon which an arch rests</term> for an arch, marked **T**, is also at the aforementioned Theater of Marcello; it supports the arch of the **Ionic** order. The cornice with the modillions <term>modillions: decorative brackets or blocks placed under the projecting part of a cornice</term>, marked **A**, was found between the churches of Saint **Adriano** and Saint **Lorenzo** in **Rome**. The architrave marked **F** was found at **Oderzo** in the **Friuli** region. Since this architrave has three bands <note>original: "fascie"</note> without any astragals <term>astragal: a small convex molding, often shaped like a string of beads</term>, I judged it to be **Ionic**. I have not provided written measurements for these elements because I have scaled them down from the full-sized originals into this form with the utmost care; these measurements can always be retrieved by using a compass.

<image-desc>

# BOOK OF THE ORDERS

Although in my opinion the **Ionic** door described by **Vitruvius** <term>Vitruvius: the Roman authority on architecture whose proportions were often debated by Renaissance masters</term> does not result in that corresponding proportion required by the building; I will not refrain from discussing what I understand of it. I say that **Vitruvius's** text refers, regarding the height of the opening <term>opening: original "lume," literally "light," referring to the clear space of a doorway</term>, to the **Doric** door: that is, from the floor to the coffered ceilings <term>coffered ceilings: original "lacunari," the recessed decorative panels in a ceiling or soffit</term> there should be three and a half parts. Where the cross is marked <note>The author refers to a small cross visible in the accompanying woodcut illustration</note>, the coffers are understood—that is, the ceiling—and two parts should be given to the height of the opening. Because of this, the corona <term>corona: the flat, vertical face of a cornice that projects out to shed water</term> remains very large, just like that of the **Doric** order. 

But another error follows: if the door is made with three parts at the bottom and its height is five parts, as the text says, and it is tapered <note>original: "minuita," referring to the classical practice of making the top of a door narrower than the base</note> at the top like the **Doric**; I find that its width becomes wider than the central intercolumniation <term>intercolumniation: the clear space between two adjacent columns</term>. This occurs when making a temple with those measurements that **Vitruvius** describes in his third book for a four-column structure, of which I have formed a figure below, so that the correspondence of this door to its temple may be seen.

->[Illustration: A woodcut of a classical Ionic temple facade. Four fluted columns with voluted capitals support an entablature and a triangular pediment. In the center of the pediment is a small cross. Between the central columns is a rectangular door framed by an ornamental border.]<-

In my opinion, this does not correspond. For if in the **Doric** order—whose columns are lower than the **Ionic** ones—the door has a height of two squares and a bit more; I say that the **Ionic** door, whose columns are of greater height, should have its opening taller than the **Doric** one. Nevertheless, it has less according to the text, which specifies five parts in height and three parts in width. All this is said, however, with great reverence for such a great author. Nonetheless, taking those parts that are relevant in **Vitruvius’s** text, I will form one here alongside it, without tapering it at the top; but whoever wishes to have it tapered for their own satisfaction should follow the rule of the **Doric** door. But again, let all this be said with great reverence.

<summary>The author critiques Vitruvius's proportions for the Ionic door, arguing that they result in a doorway that is too wide for its temple and shorter than those of the Doric order, despite the Ionic's taller columns.</summary>
<keywords>Vitruvius, Ionic door, proportions, doorway opening, intercolumniation, tapering, Doric comparison, architectural theory</keywords>

--- Page 85 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the Ionic order, moving from the critique of Vitruvius's proportions to the author's own specific prescriptions for the dimensions of an Ionic doorway and its decorative elements, such as the frieze and the consoles.</meta>

X L I

->### BOOK FOUR. IONIC<-

I say that the **opening** <term>opening: original "luce," literally "light," referring to the clear internal space of a doorway</term> of this door shall be at least two squares high. The **pilaster-frame** <term>pilaster-frame: original "pilastrata," referring here to the vertical side-molding or architrave of the door</term> should be one-twelfth the height of the opening, crafted in the manner previously described for the **Ionic architrave**, and **astragals** <term>astragal: a small, convex molding, often shaped like a string of beads</term> should be added to the **fascias** <term>fascias: the flat, horizontal bands that make up an architrave</term>, as shown in figure **F**. 

The **frieze** above it, if one wishes to carve it with any decoration, should be a quarter part higher than the said pilaster-frame; however, if it is to be left **plain** <note>original: "schietto," meaning smooth or unadorned</note>, it should be a quarter part smaller. The height of the **corona** <term>corona: the projecting vertical member of a cornice that serves to shed water</term> and the other members should be equal to the pilaster-frame, divided in the manner seen in figure **F**. 

The **Ancones** or **Prothyrides** <note>original: "Anconi ouero Prothiridi." These are technical Greek terms for the decorative brackets or consoles supporting the cornice of a door</note>, which some call **consoles** <term>consoles: original "mensule," ornamental brackets with a scroll-like profile</term> and others call **brackets** <note>original: "Cartelle"</note>, should have a front width equal to the pilaster-frame. However, the lower part, at the level of the opening above the door, should be reduced <note>original: "minuita," meaning tapered or narrowed</note> by a quarter part; from these brackets the leaves hang, as can be seen in the figure. 

The curved part of the circle above this door, which is called the **Remenato** <term>Remenato: a segmental pediment or curved molding placed over a lintel</term>, shall have its height determined as follows: let the points of the compass be placed at the two sides of the **Cyma** <term>Cyma: a wave-like molding (S-shaped) at the top of a cornice</term> at its highest point; lower one point of the compass down to the **cross point** <note>original: "punto croce," referring to the geometric intersection marked with a small "+" in the woodcut</note>, and with the other point, draw an arc from one side of the Cyma to the other. This will be its height. Whether to include this **Remenato** or not will always remain at the discretion of the **Architect**, and this design will also serve for windows and other ornaments.

# BOOK FOUR
## THE IONIC ORDER

<page-num>71</page-num>

The **opening** <term>opening: original "luce," literally "light," the clear space of the doorway</term> of the following door shall be of double proportion—that is, two squares high. The face of the **pilaster-frame** <term>pilaster-frame: original "pilastrata," referring to the architrave or jamb framing the door</term> shall be one-eighth the width of the opening. The column will be twice as thick at its base <note>This means the diameter of the column base is twice the width of the door's side-frame</note>, and its upper part shall be **diminished** <note>original: "minuita," referring to the tapering of the column shaft</note> by one-sixth. Its height shall be nine parts, including the base and the **capital**, observing those measurements stated at the beginning of this section. 

And although these columns are one part taller than the standard rules dictate, it is not a **flaw** <note>original: "vitiosa," meaning a defect or violation of architectural principle</note>, because only two-thirds of the column projects from the wall, and they carry no weight other than the **pediment** <term>pediment: original "frontespicio," the triangular decorative gable above the door</term>. Indeed, if by some accident these columns were to exceed nine parts in height, they would not be blameworthy, as they are placed for ornament alone, and also because they are bound into the wall. 

The height of the **architrave** shall be the same as the width of the pilaster-frame. The **frieze**, whether **carved** or **plain** <note>original: "schietto," unadorned</note>, should be made as described for the others. The height of the **cornice** should be equal to that of the architrave. For the other members, proceed as stated at the beginning. The **pediment** shall be at the **discretion** <note>original: "arbitrio"</note> of the Architect to make it higher or lower using one of the rules given in the **Doric order**. From this invention, the prudent Architect may adapt to many situations; sometimes, according to necessity or the surrounding features, the opening of such a door may be made one and a half squares high, or sometimes one square and two-thirds. However, if not forced by any necessity, I would most highly praise this double-square proportion.

# BOOK FOUR
## THE IONIC ORDER

<page-num>XLII</page-num>

->**FOURTH IONIC**<-

To provide **light** <term>light: original "lume," referring to the clear openings of windows or doors that allow illumination into a building</term> to these temples, Vitruvius <note>Marcus Vitruvius Pollio, the Roman architect whose 1st-century BCE treatise "De Architectura" was the foundational text for Renaissance architects</note> in the sixth chapter of his fourth book gives these rules: firstly, that the front of the temple should be divided into four parts, and two of these should be assigned to the width of the door between its frame members. And this shall be the rule for Vitruvian temples.

But because in the **Ionic order** <term>Ionic order: one of the classical orders of architecture, known for its elegant proportions and capitals with spiral scrolls called volutes</term>, such a height is not typically given to the door opening without its **pediment** <term>pediment: original "frontespicio," the triangular decorative gable above the doorway</term> occupying half of the ornament of the **corona** <term>corona: the flat, projecting part of the cornice that serves to shed water; here used to refer to the crowning part of the building's entablature</term> of the entire structure, one may therefore increase or decrease the proportions to accommodate the specific location. Nevertheless, the design that follows is for a door of the Ionic order, in which I have observed those rules that seemed most appropriate to me, keeping in mind the style of our own time. For if the ornament above the door did not occupy a great part of the...

The image depicts a doorway flanked by fluted Ionic columns resting on pedestals, supporting a full entablature and a prominent triangular pediment, illustrating the author's modern adaptation of Vitruvian principles.</note>

# BOOK FOUR: THE ORDERS
## IONIC

<page-num>I</page-num>

<warning>Faint ghosting of an Ionic capital and architectural details visible in the center of the page, likely bleed-through from another folio.</warning>

Having paired this **Rusticated** style <term>rusticated: original "Rustico," a style of masonry featuring rough-hewn stones and deep-set joints, often used to suggest strength, nature, and antiquity</term> with **Tuscan** work—not only within the **Tuscan** order in many places, applying this **Rusticated** style to the **Tuscan**; but also mixing it with **Doric** work in a certain portal—I have now decided to introduce it into the **Ionic** order as well. However, this style is not to be applied to every **Ionic** building without careful consideration. While it is highly suitable for a country villa, such an order is also not to be criticized in the city if used for the residence of a man of letters or a merchant of robust character; it can be tolerated there <note>The author suggests that "rusticated" architecture, though rugged, can reflect the "robust" or sturdy intellectual and professional life of the inhabitant</note>. But in whatever location one chooses to build it, if one wishes to place another order above it to serve as a balcony <term>balcony: original "poggiuolo," a projecting platform or ledge</term>, it will be necessary to project the masonry out far enough that the thickness of the wall forms the floor of the balcony, as is shown in the floor plan below.

The proportions of that work shall be such that the opening <term>opening: original "luce," literally "light," referring to the clear space of a doorway or window</term> is two squares high, measured to the underside of the arch. The pilastered sections at the sides of the column should be one-eighth of the width of the opening, and the column itself should be one-fourth the width of that opening. Its height, however, shall be nine parts, including the base and the capital. The semicircular arch is to be divided into thirteen and a quarter parts: the central wedge <term>keystone: original "cuneo di mezo," the central stone at the summit of an arch</term> shall be one and a quarter parts, while the other twelve shall be equal. 

For the wedges <term>voussoirs: original "cunei," the wedge-shaped stones that form the arch</term>, the architrave, the frieze, and the cornice, these should be one-fifth of the column's height. This fifth shall be divided into eleven parts: four for the architrave, three for the frieze, and four for the cornice. The height of the balcony's parapet should be half the width of the door. The measurements for its specific components can be derived from the pedestal of this order. Regarding the individual members of the bases, capitals, architraves, friezes, and cornices, they shall be executed as described at the beginning of this book. But for the wedges that radiate toward the center and those that encircle the columns, one should follow what is shown in the following drawing.

<image-desc>At the bottom of the page is a technical architectural floor plan (ichnography) of a portal entrance. The masonry is indicated with parallel hatching lines, and the drawing shows the projection of the wall that forms the "poggiuolo" (balcony or ledge) mentioned in the text.</image-desc>

<header>FOURTH
IONIC</header>
<page-num>XLIII</page-num>

--- Page 90 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of architectural orders, specifically focusing on the transition from the Ionic to the Corinthian order in a multi-story facade, following the pagination of Serlio’s Fourth Book.</meta>

# BOOK OF THE ORDERS

Although the height of these arches is not in double proportion <note>A 2:1 ratio, where the height is twice the width</note>, as in the majority of the others I have demonstrated, this is not a flaw; rather, it is done with artifice. Sometimes it may happen that in the division of a facade, one must obey a specific required height, or perhaps aim for an odd number of arches—as they should always be—to place the principal door in the center. In such cases, the arches might not reach that standard height. However, if we are not forced by any necessity, I will always praise a height that is double the width more than any other proportion.

The width between one pillar and the other shall be three parts, and the height five parts. Once the width is divided into five parts, the face of the pillars <term>pilastro: a rectangular column projecting from a wall</term> shall be two parts, and the thickness of the column shall be one part. Thus, on the sides of the column, there will be the *parastatæ* <term>parastatæ: an ancient term for square pillars or pilasters flanking an opening</term> called <term>pilastrate: pilaster strips or rectangular uprights</term>, which are half the thickness of a column; the arch will follow this same measure. But let the *impost* <term>impost: the molding or block on which the foot of an arch rests</term> that supports the arch be of the same height and style as that of the Theatre of Marcellus <note>A famous ancient open-air theatre in Rome, used as a model for Renaissance architects</note> marked "T" on page 40.

The columns shall be nine parts in height, including the bases and the capitals, made according to the rule given at the start of this chapter. The central door shall be half the width of the space between the pillars. Its height is determined as follows: once the *pilastrata* is made one-sixth of the opening, and the cornice above the door is level with the arch imposts, add the *cyma* <term>cyma: a decorative molding with a wave-like profile</term> on top; then, making the frieze one-quarter smaller than said *pilastrata*, the resulting height will be slightly less than two squares <note>A ratio of roughly 2:1</note>. The pediment <term>frontespicio: the triangular or curved gable above a door or window</term> should be made according to one of the rules provided for the Doric order. The architrave, frieze, and cornice should be one-quarter of the column's height, following the aforementioned rules.

The upper order, which is the second, should be one-quarter shorter than the first. Consequently, its architrave, frieze, and cornice should be one-fifth of the total height, which will amount to one-quarter of the column's height. The division of the specific members <note>The smaller decorative components of the moldings</note> will be found more fully in the discussion of the Composite order.

The arched windows shall be as wide as the door, and their pilasters and arches shall match as well, but their height shall be two and a half squares; this is to provide more light to the rooms. These Corinthian columns shall be smooth and reduced <note>tapered</note>—as I have stated—by one-quarter compared to those on the level below. The width of the niches between the columns and windows shall be one and a half column-widths, and their height shall be four times the thickness of a column. If any other parts remain to be sized, one can always refer back to the primary rule of that order; for the specific measurements of this Corinthian column will be found in the section on the Corinthian order.

Above this order, if one wishes, a walkway could be constructed across the facade, provided it is well-protected from rainwater with stone slabs carefully fitted together. The height of the parapet should be at a comfortable height for leaning; such a feature would add great ornament to the facade and provide much convenience for the inhabitants.

<summary>This page describes the proportional rules for designing a two-story facade, transitioning from a lower order to a Corinthian upper order. It details the specific measurements for arches, doors, and windows, emphasizing that while ideal proportions exist, practical necessity often dictates adjustments.</summary>

<keywords>architectural proportions, Ionic order, Corinthian order, Theatre of Marcellus, pilasters, imposts, pediments, Renaissance architecture, Sebastiano Serlio</keywords>

XLIIII
FOURTH.
IONIC.

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Ionic order from the previous page, specifically addressing practical solutions for when available columns are shorter than the desired height of a portico.</meta>

-># BOOK OF THE FIRST ORDER<-

Sometimes (as I have mentioned before) the **Architect** will have a good number of columns, but they will be of such a low height that they will not suffice for his needs unless he knows how to adapt and apply these members to the requirements of the building he wishes to construct. Therefore, if the height of the **portico** <note>A porch or walkway with a roof supported by columns</note> is greater than the height of the columns, one may create an arch in the center of a facade, supported by the **architrave** <term>the horizontal beam resting directly on the columns</term> that sits above the columns. This architrave shall serve as the **impost** <note>the structural point where an arch rests</note> for a **barrel vault** <term>a masonry ceiling shaped like a continuous semicircular tunnel</term>; but where the arch is located, a **cross vault** <term>also known as a groin vault, formed by the intersection of two barrel vaults</term> should be made. For the strength of this vault, an **iron or bronze key** <term>original: "chiaue di ferro"; here referring to a structural tie-rod or tension bar</term> should be placed above every column, in the manner I described regarding a similar subject in the **Doric order**. 

The division of this facade shall be as follows: the **intercolumniation** <term>the clear space between two columns</term> in the center shall be six column thicknesses wide, and the column's height, including its base and capital, shall be eight parts <note>eight times its diameter</note>. The architrave should be as thick as the column is at its top, as should the arch above which a **cornice** <term>the projecting decorative molding at the top of a wall or entablature</term> will be made. The height of this cornice shall be one-quarter larger than the architrave, excluding its **bead and fillet** <note>original: "tondino & il quadretto"; small decorative moldings</note>. This cornice will serve as a **capital** for the **small pilasters** <term>rectangular columns attached to a wall</term>, which shall be the same thickness as the columns at their upper part. 

The spaces at the sides between the columns shall be three column thicknesses wide. The height of the **door** shall be such that the architrave supporting the vault serves as a cornice for said door, though some of its members may be changed as seen in the drawing. Beneath the cornice, a **frieze** <term>the middle section of the entablature</term> shall be placed, which should be one-quarter smaller than the architrave. Once the **pilastrade** <note>a row of pilasters or the structure forming the door frame</note> is made, its height should match the distance from the pilastrade to the door step; its width should be half its height, so that the opening of this door is a **double square** <note>original: "due quadri"; a 1:2 height-to-width ratio</note>. The **windows** shall be set level with the door; their width shall be two columns wide, and their height shall be of **diagonal proportion** <term>original: "diagonea proportione"; a ratio where the height is equal to the diagonal of a square formed by the width, roughly 1:1.41</term>.

The **second order** shall be one-quarter smaller than the first, and the **parapet** <term>a low protective wall</term> shall be made at a convenient height. The remainder is to be divided into five parts: four shall be for the height of the columns, and the remaining part shall be for the architrave, frieze, and cornice, observing the given measurements for this order. The width of the central window, including its pilasters, should be as wide as the door opening below; its height shall be double its width. Regarding its ornamentation above, the rule given for similar doors should be followed. The windows on the sides shall be the same width as those below, and their height should be level with the central one.

The central elevation above this order shall be one-quarter smaller than the second, reduced proportionally member by member; for its openings, the same order used in the first part below should be kept, as it is the same subject. However, whether to build or not to build this **third order** is left to the discretion of the **Architect**.

<summary>This page provides technical instructions on how to adapt short columns to a tall portico using arches, vaults, and tie-rods, while detailing the specific proportions for doors, windows, and stacked architectural orders.</summary>
