<meta>Continuing from the discussion of the Ionic order, where its proportions were likened to the matronly form. This page applies these principles to the design of a fireplace mantel.</meta>

LXIX

# BOOK ON THE ORDERS

Since (as I said at the beginning of this book) the **Ionic** work is derived from the matronly form <note>Architectural theorists of the Renaissance, such as Serlio, characterized the Ionic order as having the proportions of a dignified, mature woman, contrasting it with the "manly" Doric and "girlish" Corinthian.</note>; it is also a fitting thing, when having to make any fireplace of such an order, to imitate this type as much as possible, in order to stay within the boundaries of the order while maintaining decorum <note>In architecture, 'decorum' (decoro) refers to the suitability of a design to its function and social standing.</note>. The proportions of the following fireplace will be these: once the appropriate height of the fireplace opening is established, the height from the floor to the architrave <term>architrave: the main beam resting across the tops of columns or supports</term> should be divided into eight parts, which will be in imitation of the **Ionic** column. From these parts shall be drawn this "monstrous" <note>original: "monstruosa"; in this period, the term often referred to a hybrid or imaginative decorative form rather than something frightening.</note> or mixed form, as we might call it, conforming to such a style, which will serve as a modillion <term>modillion: an ornamental bracket or block used in a series under a cornice</term>. Let the architrave, frieze, and cornice be a fourth part of the height of the modillion, divided in the manner stated at the beginning: although these members may appear to have greater height, this happens (as has been said other times) from the low viewpoint, which encompasses two parts of the members—namely the front and part of the projection. That tablet above the capitals, which occupies the architrave and the frieze, was used by some ancients; I believe to have more space for writing letters, and also because they delighted much in novelty. This tablet will always be at the Architect's discretion to include or omit. The second tier where the Dolphins are is made for two reasons: one is to make the mouth that receives the smoke wider, and the other is to remove that pyramidal shape created by the chimney flue in a room of good height: which things will always be at the liberty of the Architect to make larger or smaller, and sometimes not to make them at all.

->which is **Ionic** belongs in this place, and this was an error by the printers.<-

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Ionic order from the previous page, transitioning from the general proportions of columns and capitals to a practical application in interior architecture: the design of an Ionic fireplace or portal.</meta>

# BOOK OF THE ORDERS

This sort of <term>fireplace<note>original: "camini"</note></term> proves very convenient for small spaces, and is used lower than a person's face, so that the fire—which is very harmful to the eyes because of its glare—can warm the rest of the body without offending the sight, especially when standing. Let the opening of this fireplace be a perfect square. The <term>pilaster<note>The vertical side-post of the fireplace; original: "pilastrata"</note></term> shall be one-sixth part of that opening. The <term>cymatium<note>A molding, typically with an S-shaped profile, crowning a larger member; original: "cimatio"</note></term> will be made a seventh part of the pilaster. From the remainder, twelve parts shall be made: three shall be given to the first <term>fascia<note>A horizontal band or broad molding; original: "fascia"</note></term>, four shall be for the second, and the five that remain will be given to the third fascia; and for further ornament, <term>astragals<note>Small, semi-circular moldings, often resembling a string of beads; original: "astragali"</note></term> may be added as can be seen there to the side. The height of the <term>volute<note>The characteristic spiral scroll of the Ionic capital; original: "voluta"</note></term> should be as much as the three fasciae without the cymatium, and from this, three parts are made: one will be for the <term>frieze<note>original: "fregio"</note></term> where the <term>fluting<note>original: "canellatura"</note></term> is, another will be for the <term>ovolo<note>A rounded, convex molding, often decorated with an egg-and-dart pattern; original: "vuouolo"</note></term> with its bead-molding and fillet, and the third shall be given to the volute. This volute will hang from the sides level with the cymatium, but the leaves will hang down to the level of the <term>architrave<note>The main beam resting on the columns or pilasters; original: "architraue"</note></term>. The height of the <term>corona<note>The projecting part of the cornice that sheds water; original: "corona"</note></term> with the two cymatia and the <term>cyma<note>The uppermost molding of the cornice; original: "sima"</note></term> should be as much as the second and third fascia with the cymatium; but the projection of the corona should be as much as its total height, and the projection of the cyma and the cymatia should always be as much as their height. I have had this form put into practice, which proves very pleasing to all and has a grand appearance. But because this proportion spreads out significantly in every direction, occupying much space, the pilaster can be made an eighth part of the opening, and with those same proportions all the smaller members can be made; thus the whole work will remain proportioned, but with greater slenderness. The upper part made for ornament can be done as the Architect pleases, because this fireplace is understood to be contained entirely within the thickness of the wall; and this ornament would also be suitable for a door or window of this order.

->Here ends the Ionic, the Corinthian follows.<-

<meta>This page follows the transition from the Ionic order to the Corinthian order. The previous page concluded the discussion on Ionic proportions and ornamentation, specifically focusing on fireplaces and windows, before introducing the Corinthian order.</meta>

# BOOK OF THE CORINTHIAN ORDER, AND ITS ORNAMENTS. CHAPTER VIII.

In the Corinthian work, Vitruvius treats only the capital in the first chapter of the fourth book, almost as if he wishes to say that once that is placed atop the Ionic column, it becomes a Corinthian work; although in the second chapter he notes the derivation of the modillions <term>modillions: decorative brackets placed under a cornice</term> under the coronas <term>corona: the flat, vertical face of a cornice</term>, yet for all this he gives no rule or measure for the other members. But the ancient Romans, taking great delight in this Corinthian style as they did in the others as well, made the bases of this column very ornate and rich with members. In order to provide some rules for these bases, I shall choose one from the most beautiful building in Rome, which is the Pantheon, called the Rotunda, setting all its measurements into a rule.

The Corinthian column, as a general rule, shall be made nine parts in height, including the base and the capital. This capital shall be as high as the column is thick at its lowest part; but its base should be half [the thickness] of the column <note>In classical architecture, the base height is typically half the diameter of the column shaft</note>. Having divided that height into four parts, one shall be given to the plinth, called a socle <note>original: "zocco"</note>. The remaining two parts <note>The text likely means the remaining three-quarters of the total height</note> shall be made into five parts: one shall be for the upper torus, and the lower torus shall be a fourth part larger. The remainder is to be divided into two equal parts, one of which shall be given to the lower scotia <term>scotia: a concave molding between two tori</term> with its astragal <term>astragal: a small convex molding, often bead-like</term> and with the two fillets <note>original: "quadretti"</note>. The astragal shall be the sixth part of that scotia, and each fillet shall be half the size of the astragal, and the fillet above the lower torus shall be two-thirds the size of the astragal. The other part is divided such that the astragal is a sixth part of the whole, its fillet is half of said astragal, and the fillet under the upper torus shall be a third part larger than the other. The projection, called the "sporto," if it is placed above another order of columns, shall be made like that of the Ionic; but if its placement is upon the ground floor, its projection shall be half of the base, like the Doric. However, according to the places where the bases are set, it is necessary that the Architect be very shrewd; for when the bases are below the eye of the beholders, these measurements will turn out well. But if they are placed higher than human sight, all those members which, because of the distance, would be hidden by other members, must necessarily be made larger than the given measurements. When the bases are placed at a greater height, they should be made with a smaller number of members and more shapely. In this, the Architect of the Rotunda was shrewd, for on the flat columns <note>Referring to pilasters</note> above the first order on the inside, he made the bases with two scotias, yes, but with only one astragal in place of these two.

-> **[Image Description]** <-

The origin <note>original: "deriuation"</note> of the Corinthian capital was from a Corinthian maiden; I shall not weary myself further in narrating its origin, because Vitruvius describes it in the first chapter of the fourth book. I will say, however, that if a sacred temple is to be made in this order, it should be dedicated to the Virgin Mary, mother of Jesus Christ our Redeemer, who was not only a virgin before, but was a virgin during the birth, and even after the birth. And thus for all those holy men and women who have kept a virginal life, this order is also suitable. Monasteries and cloisters that enclose virgins devoted to divine worship shall be made in this manner. But if public or private houses, or tombs are to be made...

# BOOK FOUR.
# THE CORINTHIAN ORDER.

-> For people of honest and chaste life, <- one may use this style of ornamentation to preserve the decorum of the Corinthian capital <note>Vitruvius, the Roman architectural authority, associated the Corinthian order with the slender proportions and decorative grace of a young maiden, making it suitable for temples dedicated to goddesses or for settings reflecting "chaste" virtues</note>. The height shall be equal to the thickness of the column at its base, and the abacus <term>abacus: the flat, crowning slab of a capital</term> shall be the seventh part of the total height. Of the remaining height, let three parts be made: one for the bottom leaves, another given to the middle leaves, and the third established for the cauliculi <term>cauliculi: the stalks or stems from which the volutes of a Corinthian capital emerge</term>, or volutes <term>volutes: the spiral, scroll-like ornaments</term>, as we may wish to call them. But between these volutes and the middle leaves, a space should be left for the minor leaves, from which the cauliculi sprout.

Form the naked capital, marked **B**, which shall be as thick at its lower part as the column is at its top. Under the abacus, a belt or strap should be made, the height of which shall be half that of the abacus. This abacus is then divided into three parts: one shall be the cymatium <term>cymatium: a molding with a wave-like profile</term> with its small square fillet, and the other two shall be for the face of the abacus. Under the four horns <note>The "horns" are the four projecting corners of the Corinthian abacus</note> of the abacus, the larger cauliculi are to be made, and in the middle of the abacus there shall be a flower as large as the height of the abacus itself, under which the smaller cauliculi are made. Under the larger cauliculi, and also under the smaller ones, the middle leaves are placed; between these the minor leaves sprout, and from those the stalks arise. There will be eight middle leaves, and just as many on the bottom, placed in the manner shown in figure **C**.

<margin>
D. diameter of the column at the base, or thickness.
</margin>

<margin>
G. flower
a. Cymatium
b. Abacus
c. Cauliculus
d. Minor leaves
e. Middle leaves
f. Bottom leaves
</margin>

<margin>
C. thickness of the column at the top.
</margin>

The width of the abacus from corner to corner along a diagonal line shall be equal to two diameters of the column at its base. When this is placed within a square, and a larger circle is drawn outside of it touching the four corners, and outside that larger circle another square is made, divided by diagonal lines, it will show that these lines are in length equal to two column thicknesses, as the text of Vitruvius <note>Marcus Vitruvius Pollio, the author of the only surviving major ancient Roman treatise on architecture</note> states. But from the line **B.C.** a perfect triangle shall be made, and at angle **X** will be the point from which to curve the abacus—that is, to hollow it out. From that part which is between the larger circle and the smaller circle, let four parts be made: one will remain above the point **A**, and three are taken away in this manner: placing one point of the compass at point **X** and the other point above **A**, and drawing a curve from **B** through **a** to **C**; where the curved line intersects the two sides of the triangle, there will be the ends of the horns of the capital. An example of this is in figure **D**. In this way, the abacus will fall perpendicularly over the plinth <term>plinth: the square block at the very base of a column</term> of the base, so that no line is made by chance; rather, all will be guided by geometric and probable reason.

A large construction triangle, with its vertex at point X and its base at the top of the column shaft (point C), defines the capital's expansion. The capital's elements are keyed with lowercase letters a through f. At the bottom, the column shaft stands on a base (labeled B) with tiered moldings.]

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical exposition of the Corinthian order, moving from the proportions of the capital discussed on the previous page to the design and measurements of the entablature (the architrave, frieze, and cornice) that sits atop the column.</meta>

# BOOK ON THE ORDERS

As for the Corinthian architrave, frieze, and cornice (as I mentioned at the beginning of this chapter), Vitruvius gives no specific measurements, although he mentions the derivation of the modillions <term>modillions: ornamental brackets or blocks found under the corona of a cornice</term>. These modillions can be used in any style of cornice, as is seen in ancient ruins. However, to proceed modestly and without deviating too far from the text of Vitruvius, I will place Ionic ornaments above the Corinthian capital, adding astragals <term>astragals: small, semi-circular moldings, often decorated like a string of beads</term> to the architrave and an ovolo <term>ovolo: a rounded, convex molding, often carved with an egg-and-dart pattern</term> beneath the corona <term>corona: the flat, vertical "face" of the cornice that projects out to shed rain</term>, just as some modest Roman architects did. I say that once the architrave is made, as described in the Ionic order, a small bead molding should be made beneath the middle fascia <term>fascia: a long, flat horizontal band</term> at one-eighth the height of that fascia. Beneath the upper fascia, another bead molding should be made at one-eighth the height of that upper fascia, carved as shown below. Then, once the frieze is made with its cymatium <term>cymatium: a wave-like crowning molding</term> and the dentils <term>dentils: small, rectangular blocks spaced like teeth</term> with their own cymatium, the ovolo shall be placed above that, with a height equal to the first fascia. Because of its projection and its carving, this ovolo will appear larger than the middle fascia. Above the ovolo, the corona and the cyma <term>cyma: the uppermost S-shaped molding of a cornice</term> with its cymatium should be placed, as was described for the Ionic order.

*(The image described here shows the complex stacking of these moldings: the stepped architrave at the bottom, the plain frieze, and the elaborate cornice at the top featuring both the "teeth" of the dentils and the "brackets" of the modillions.)*

Some Roman architects, proceeding a bit more licentiously <note>In this context, "licentiously" refers to taking artistic liberties or breaking established classical rules rather than moral conduct</note>, did not just place the ovolo above the dentils; they even placed both modillions and dentils within the very same cornice. This practice is strongly condemned by Vitruvius in the second chapter of his fourth book. He argues that dentils represent the ends of small rafters—called *asseri* <note>original: "asseri"</note> by Vitruvius—while modillions are made in imitation of the ends of larger primary rafters, which he calls *canteri* <note>original: "Canteri"</note>. These two types of rafters cannot exist one on top of the other in the same place in a real roof. For my part, I will never tolerate both modillions and dentils in a single cornice, even though Rome is full of examples, as are various other places in Italy. 

But proceeding modestly within this order, I find a general rule: the total height of the column (including the base and the capital) should be divided into four parts, and one of these parts is given to the architrave, frieze, and cornice. This height corresponds and agrees with the Doric work, where these members are also a fourth part of the column's height. This fourth part should then be divided into ten units: three shall be for the architrave (divided as mentioned above), three shall be given to the frieze, and the remaining four units shall form the cornice in the following way: divide those four units into nine smaller parts. One part will be for the cymatium above the frieze; two parts shall be given to the ovolo with its small square fillet; another two parts shall be for the modillion with its cymatium; two more parts shall be for the corona; and the final two parts will form the cyma with its cymatium (the cymatium being one-fourth of the cyma's height). The projection of all these members should be as stated above. One could also make this architrave, frieze, and cornice a fifth part of the column's height, as Vitruvius mentions regarding theaters in the seventh chapter of his fourth book.

<summary>This page explains the proportions and components of the Corinthian entablature, emphasizing a "modest" approach that avoids mixing dentils and modillions, a practice Serlio notes is common in Rome but technically incorrect according to Vitruvius.</summary>
<keywords>Corinthian order, entablature, architrave, frieze, cornice, Vitruvius, modillions, dentils, architectural proportions, Roman architecture</keywords>

### XLIX
-> **FOURTH BOOK: CORINTHIAN** <-
N

<image-desc>To the left of the main text block is a large, detailed architectural woodcut illustrating a Corinthian column. The column is fluted, with the lower third of the flutings cabled (filled in). The capital is ornately decorated with acanthus leaves. Above the capital, the entablature consists of a three-tiered architrave, a frieze featuring a relief of a winged griffin, and a cornice with various moldings. On the far right edge of the page, a partial view of another column of a similar order is visible.</image-desc>

The **tapering** <term>diminution: the gradual decrease in the diameter of a column shaft from the bottom to the top</term> of the **Corinthian column** shall be done as has been said of the others according to its height; however, from sixteen feet and below, let the top be reduced by a sixth part, following the rule given at the beginning of the **Tuscan order**. And if the column is to be **striated** <note>original: "striata", meaning fluted</note>—that is, **channeled**—it shall be done like the **Ionic**, but the flutings shall be filled <term>cabled: flutes that are filled with a convex molding, usually in the lower third of the shaft</term> from the bottom third down, as is shown here to the side. 

The **architrave**, **frieze**, and **cornice** shown here are without **modillions** <term>modillions: ornamental brackets under the cornice</term>, in order to demonstrate a variety of measurements. Its **architrave** is half the thickness of the column. The **frieze**, because it is sculpted, is one-quarter larger than the architrave; and the **cornice**, excluding the **cymatium** <term>cymatium: the crowning molding of a frieze or cornice</term> of the frieze, is as high as the architrave. The height of the entire unit [the entablature] is less than a fifth of the height of the column; nevertheless, if the decoration of the **corona** <term>corona: the flat, projecting vertical face of the cornice</term> is bold, it will appear to have a greater height than it actually does, and it will place less weight upon the building. 

Therefore, the prudent Architect can always make a choice of those parts which best suit his convenience, provided that he does not stray far from the writings of **Vitruvius** <note>Marcus Vitruvius Pollio: a Roman architect whose 1st-century BC treatise became the "bible" of Renaissance architecture</note> and from the "good antiquity" which is known through the writings of that author. 

And if by some circumstance the Corinthian column should require its own proportioned **pedestal**—not being bound by other requirements—its proportions shall be such that its width is equal to the **Plinth** <term>plinth: the square block at the very bottom of a column base</term> of the base. Let this width be divided into three parts; then add two more parts to those three, and these [five parts] shall be for its height—that is, the **die** <term>netto: the square, central block of a pedestal between the base and the cap</term> without its moldings. These moldings shall be made so that the height of the die of the pedestal is divided into seven parts: one of these shall be given to its base, and another shall be given to its **cap** <note>original: "cima", the crowning molding of the pedestal</note>, which results in a total of nine parts. Thus, this pedestal will be proportioned to the column, which itself is also nine [diameters] high. However, regarding the particular members of the base and the cornice, I will demonstrate further on various ancient examples which the judicious Architect may use. These will be shown even more abundantly in the Third Book on Antiquities, where there will be many different pedestals, all measured from ancient ruins.

<summary>This page details the tapering and fluting of the Corinthian column, specifies the mathematical proportions for an entablature without modillions, and provides a formula for a pedestal based on a nine-part ratio to match the column's height.</summary>
<keywords>Corinthian order, tapering, fluting, entablature proportions, Vitruvius, pedestal design, die, plinth, Renaissance architecture</keywords>

# THE BOOK ON THE ORDERS

-> **CORINTHIAN ORDER** <-

Among the other antiquities of the **Corinthian order** <note>original: "opera Corinthia"</note> seen in Italy, it seems to me that the **Pantheon** in Rome and the triumphal arch located at the port of **Ancona** <note>The Arch of Trajan in Ancona, built c. 115 AD</note> are among the most beautiful and the best understood. Of that arch, the capital shown below at **A** is proportioned to the original, scaled down from large to small with great diligence. Its height departs from the writings of **Vitruvius** <note>Marcus Vitruvius Pollio, the 1st-century BC Roman architect whose treatise "De Architectura" was the primary authority for Renaissance builders</note>; nevertheless, it has a very good correspondence. Perhaps Vitruvius intended for the height of the Corinthian capital to be equal to the thickness of the column excluding the **abacus** <term>abacus: the flat, topmost slab of a capital that supports the architrave</term>, but the text in this section might be flawed. For I find not only this present capital in these proportions, but I have found many others where the height is equal to one column-thickness without the abacus. 

The columns of this arch are **channeled** <note>original: "canellate", meaning fluted</note> in the manner shown here, and they stand out slightly more than halfway from the wall <note>original: "fuori del vivo"; this refers to engaged columns that are attached to a wall rather than free-standing</note>. The **pedestal** and the base above it are part of the same arch, crafted with its proportions in minute detail. 

The cornices shown here to the side were found at the **Forum Transitorium** <note>Also known as the Forum of Nerva in Rome</note>. The one marked **A** is very modest for a Corinthian cornice without **modillions** <term>modillions: decorative brackets placed under the corona of a cornice</term>. The one marked **B** is somewhat more **licentious** <note>In classical architecture, "licentious" refers to designs that deviate from established rules or "decorum"</note>, because two members are of the same nature. But the one marked **C** is most licentious, both because of the duplicated members—which below the **corona** <term>corona: the flat, vertical projecting face of the cornice</term> appear very awkward—and also because the corona has very little projection for such a large cornice. 

The base of a pedestal marked **D** is, in my opinion, very beautiful; as is the **basement** <note>the lower continuous molding of a wall or pedestal</note> marked **E**. Although it was a base that continued along an entire building, it could nevertheless serve as the base for a pedestal. All of these things can be applied to the Corinthian order, and I have seen similar ones in the **Ionic** order as well. 

The **architrave** marked **V** is found on a triumphal arch in **Verona** <note>Likely the Arco dei Gavi</note>; its **fasciae** <term>fasciae: the three horizontal bands that make up a classical architrave</term> create the opposite effect of what Vitruvius describes. Nonetheless, I wanted to place it here to demonstrate such a difference. And although I do not state all the specific measurements of these antiquities here, they are nevertheless proportioned according to the ancient originals, though reduced to this small format.

# BOOK ON THE ORDERS

Regarding the *Corinthian* door, *Vitruvius* <note>Marcus Vitruvius Pollio, the 1st-century BCE Roman architect whose treatise was the foundation for Renaissance architecture</note> does not treat the subject at all; however, I shall turn to the antiquities that can still be seen. The door marked here below as **S** and **Y** is in *Tivoli* above the *Aniene* river, at a round temple <note>Referring to the Temple of Vesta in Tivoli, famous for its preservation</note> of *Corinthian* workmanship. This door is tapered in its upper part by one-eighteenth of its width. Its height exceeds the measure of two squares. The rest of the moldings are all proportioned to their own parts.
The window marked **T** and **X** is at the same temple, tapered in the same manner as the door, and all the other members are proportioned to this window: in which, with diligence and a compass, one may find the whole arrangement.

The following door marked **P** and **Z** is that of the *Pantheon*, called the *Rotunda* in *Rome*, a *Corinthian* work. Its width is twenty ancient palms <term>palmo: an ancient Roman unit of measurement, approximately 22.3 cm</term>, and its height is forty palms. It is said that the entire frame of the doorposts <term>pilastrate: the vertical side-posts or jambs of a door frame</term> is made of a single piece; for my part, I have seen no seam in it. The doorpost of this door is one-eighth the width of the opening, and this is because the doorpost is of a very substantial thickness on its side. The front of the doorpost cannot be viewed without also seeing part of the side, which makes it appear to the viewer to have a greater thickness in the front than it actually has. Because this door is of such great height, it is perfectly vertical and not tapered like the others mentioned previously. All the other members are proportioned to the door and accurately reduced in size. The base above the door is like those of the flat columns above the first order, which I cited in the *Corinthian* base at the beginning of this order.

->### ORDER<-

The door shown below is in **Palestina**, which is now called **Pelestina** <note>The author refers to the city of Palestrina, ancient Praeneste, home to a massive Roman temple complex that served as a major inspiration for Renaissance architects.</note>, and it is a **Corinthian** work: the opening of which is two squares high <note>meaning the height is exactly twice its width, a 2:1 ratio</note>. Its door-jamb <gloss>for the</gloss> <note>original: "pilastrata"</note> is one-sixth part of that width, divided in the manner that has been stated. The frieze is one-fourth part larger than the jamb. The corona <note>The flat, projecting vertical face of the cornice.</note> and the other members should be the same size as the jamb, divided in the way seen here. The consoles <note>The decorative S-curved brackets (also called modillions) on the sides.</note> hang down as shown. The pediment <note>The triangular structure at the top.</note> should be constructed as was stated in the **Doric** order on page 22, on the second side.

This door, which is different from all the others that I have ever seen from antiquity, is nonetheless very pleasing to the eye and represents grandeur. This door is located outside of **Spoleto**, about half a mile off the road, at an ancient temple of **Corinthian** work. Regarding its proportions and its particular members <note>Individual architectural components such as moldings and carvings.</note>, I will not elaborate further, because anyone who searches diligently will be able to find everything using a compass <note>The author encourages the reader to use a proportional divider or compass on the printed diagrams to derive the exact measurements, a common practice in Renaissance architectural manuals.</note>.

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical exposition of the Corinthian order, transitioning from the study of ancient ruins in Spoleto to providing a systematic method for designing modern buildings using Corinthian proportions.</meta>

-># BOOK<-
-># ORDER<-

Concerning this Corinthian work <note>original: "opera Corinthia"; referring to the architectural style characterized by ornate capitals with acanthus leaves</note>, which is very universally pleasing to everyone, even though it consists of many parts, I shall provide some general rules for it. To further satisfy those who take delight in this labor of mine, I will design several types of buildings. Because those ancient architects, who wished for their structures to last forever, made the pillars <term>pilastri: rectangular vertical supports rather than rounded columns</term> that support the arches with a very substantial thickness. 

The following facade—which can serve various purposes, as the discerning architect will know how to employ—has pillars with a width across the front equal to the opening of the arches; however, its thickness <note>The depth of the pillar as it extends into the wall</note> is one-fourth of that opening. The thickness of the columns shall be one-sixth of the width of the pillar's front. The niches between one column and the next should be made two column-thicknesses wide. Their height shall be slightly more than two squares <note>A "square" refers to a proportional unit where the height equals the width; "two squares" indicates a 2:1 height-to-width ratio</note>. The height of the pedestal should be three column-thicknesses. The height of the arch will be made of two squares. 

The height of the columns, including the bases and capitals, will be nine and a half parts <note>In architectural manuals, "parts" or "modules" are units derived from the diameter of the column</note>. The pilasters of the arches shall be made half a column wide, as shall the arch itself. The impost <term>imposta: the structural molding from which an arch springs</term> that supports the arch should be of the same height, made in the same manner as that of the **Theater of Marcello** <note>A massive ancient Roman theater that served as a primary model for Renaissance architects studying the classical orders</note> in the **Ionic** order on page forty. This impost will serve as a cornice for the door. 

The height of the door is to be made such that, beneath the said cornice, the architrave is made of the same height; from there down, two parts should be made reaching to the steps, one of which will be its width. In this way, the cornice of the door will be level with that of the windows. The cap <term>cimatia: the crowning molding of a pedestal</term> of the pedestal shall be level with the sills of the said windows. The opening of these windows should have a diagonal proportion <term>proportion diagonea: a rectangle where the height is equal to the diagonal of a square based on the width</term>, and the pilaster should be one-sixth of the light <note>The "light" refers to the clear open space of the window</note>. 

The pedestals, bases, and capitals—that is, the individual members—should be made as was described at the beginning of this order. Above the columns, the architrave, the frieze, and the cornice shall be placed, divided in the manner shown at the beginning. The height of the second order shall be one-fourth smaller than the first; all the members are to be reduced by their proportional share, as can be seen and even measured in the figure. 

The middle elevation <note>Likely referring to an attic or mezzanine level</note>, which I do not consider a full order, but much lower, shall have a height equal to the opening of the arches below. Its cornice, which serves the function of both architrave and frieze, shall be one-fifth of the total height. These measurements can be derived from the **Doric** capital. Furthermore, for greater ornament, one could add a pediment <term>fastigio or frontespicio: the decorative triangular or curved gable over an opening</term>. If placed in the center, having two others above the niches would be a defect <note>original: "vitiosi"; meaning architecturally incorrect or visually cluttered</note>, but one could make two broken pediments <term>remenati: a "broken" pediment where the top center is omitted, often used for decorative variety</term>, because in this way the work will be varied and more pleasing to the eye.

Geometric construction lines, forming a series of squares and intersecting diamonds, are drawn across the plan to demonstrate the proportional relationships between the width of the openings (gliarchi), the thickness of the supports, and the placement of the decorative elements.</image-desc>

**FOURTH, CORINTHIAN**

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>Continuity with the previous page: This page continues the discussion of the Fourth Order (Corinthian), specifically applying its proportions to the design of a sacred building or temple facade.</meta>

->**BOOK**<-
->**ORDER**<-

Whenever the Architect wishes to build a sacred temple, the more its floor level is raised from the ground, the more majesty the building will have; for so did the good ancients. However, the ancients used other forms of temples very different from our own, because they built them as a single body <note>referring to the *cella*, the inner chamber of an ancient temple</note>, but we Christians make the majority of our temples in three parts: a central part, and two parts on the sides. Sometimes the chapels are contained within these two side sections, and sometimes the chapels are built outside the two sides, as shown in the floor plan below. 

The width of this facade shall be 32 parts, one of which will be the thickness of a column. To the central <term>intercolumniation</term> <note>the clear space between two adjacent columns</note> seven parts shall be given. To the larger intercolumniations on the sides, four and a half parts shall be given. Between one column and the other where the niches are located, there will be two parts, and thus the 32 parts shall be distributed. The <term>pilasters</term> <note>rectangular columns attached to the wall</note> that support the arch will be half the width of the column. The opening of the door shall be three and a half parts in width, and its height shall be seven parts. The <term>impost</term> <note>the projecting block from which an arch springs</note> that supports the arch will be as high as the pilaster, and it will serve as a cornice not only for the door but for the windows as well. 

The height of the <term>pedestal</term> <note>the base supporting the column</note> shall be three parts. Let the height of the column be nine and a half parts, including the base and the <term>capital</term> <note>the decorative head of the column</note>. The architrave, frieze, and cornice should be made to a height of one-quarter the height of the column; and for the individual members that remain, the first rule should be observed. Regarding the windows, the niches, and the other ornaments, one can understand them from the figure and also measure them. 

The second order will be one-quarter smaller in height than the first, and all its members should be diminished by their proportional share. However, the architrave, frieze, and cornice will be divided into three equal parts: one shall be for the architrave, the second for the frieze where the <term>modillions</term> <note>ornamental brackets found under the corona of a cornice</note> are placed, and the third shall be given to the <term>corona</term> and the <term>cyma</term> <note>the uppermost moldings of the cornice</note>. The <term>pediment</term> <note>the triangular gable crowning the facade</note> shall be made in the manner demonstrated by <term>Vitruvius</term> <note>the Roman author whose works served as the foundation for Renaissance architecture</note> for the Doric order. The two wings at the sides, which serve as ornament for the facade and also for support, shall be the quarter of a circle, and their center will be the points **A +** and **B**. Regarding these wings, one can be placed above every arch that divides the chapels, which will provide great support to the central section; also, the rainwater from the roof above can descend through them into the lower parts.

--- Page 112 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the Corinthian order from page 54, transitioning into the practical application of these proportions in building design, specifically for structures featuring shops and multi-story arcades.</meta>

-># BOOK [ON]<-
->## THE ORDERS<-

THE division of the following work shall be such that the void between one pillar and the next is divided into three parts: one of these parts shall be for the width of the pillar, and its thickness shall be half of that. The thickness of the column shall be half the width of the pillar's front. Its height shall be ten and a half parts, including the base and the capital, and likewise the <term>pilasters<note>Rectangular supports projecting from a wall, mimicking the appearance of a column.</note></term> that support the arch shall be half a column. The <term>impost<note>The structural member or block that sits on top of a pillar and supports the base of an arch.</note></term> of the arch shall be the same: its measurements can be taken from the Doric capital by changing the members, and this will serve as the cornice for the doorway and also as a support for the windows above the shops. The height of the arch—because sometimes certain arrangements will require it to be as low as is shown—shall be three parts in width and five in height, and the doorway shall have the same proportion. The pilaster for it shall be a sixth part of that width; and if the Architect wishes to make the height of the arches double their width, the doorway will also take on that proportion. However, the columns will then require a square <term>plinth<note>A heavy square base or block (zoccolo) placed under the column to give it extra height and stability.</note></term> beneath their bases, as the ancients accommodated such things. The height of the <term>entablature<note>The entire horizontal structure supported by columns, consisting of the architrave, frieze, and cornice.</note></term> (comprised of the architrave, frieze, and cornice) shall be equal to the thickness of two columns, divided as stated in the first rule, or in the manner of some of the ancient examples found in the preceding pages.

And because the floor of the second <term>order<note>A level or story of a building designed according to a specific architectural style (e.g., Corinthian or Doric).</note></term>—at the level of the first's cornice, from beneath the arch to beneath the floor—would be too large a space for creating <term>cross-vaults<note>An architectural feature produced by the intersection at right angles of two barrel vaults.</note></term>; I intend in such cases to build an arch in line with every column, and to vault every space with a <term>basin vault<note>A shallow dome or "catino," resembling an inverted bowl.</note></term> or a cupola, whichever we wish to call it. 

The height of the second order shall be made a fourth part smaller than the first, divided in this manner: the Podium, called the Parapet, shall be two column-thicknesses high (measured from the base), and from there upwards, five parts shall be made. One part shall be given to the architrave, frieze, and cornice, and the remaining four shall be for the column. The pilasters that hold up the arches shall be half a column, and the arches likewise. For the remaining members, the general rule should be observed. If this facade is built facing a square, as is shown for the shops, a podium above the final cornice will be very convenient and serve as an ornament. However, to protect against rain and ice, one must construct a floor with great care using stone slabs, with their joints well-sealed with the best <term>stuccos<note>A durable plaster or cement used for sealing and decoration.</note></term>, and above all, it must have a good slope so that water does not collect. But it will be more secure if such floors are covered with good sheets of lead. 

And although all good Architects condemn and avoid placing a column or pillar over a void <note>This refers to the structural taboo of placing a heavy support directly over an opening, like a doorway or window, which can cause structural failure.</note>—which I also do not praise—nonetheless, because I have seen a similar design at the **Portico of Pompey** in **Rome** (though of the Doric order), I have taken the liberty [of including it] should anyone wish to make use of such a thing.

<summary>This page provides the geometric rules for constructing multi-story arcades, focusing on the proportions of pillars, arches, and vaults for commercial use. It also offers practical advice on waterproofing roofs with lead and justifies the unconventional placement of columns over voids by citing the ancient Portico of Pompey.</summary>

<keywords>architectural proportions, Corinthian order, Doric capital, shop design, vaulting, Portico of Pompey, waterproofing, plinth, parapet, structural rules</keywords>

<header>FOURTH.
CORINTHIAN</header>
<page-num>LV</page-num>

--- Page 114 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Corinthian order from page 55, now specifically applying it to the Venetian architectural style and multi-story loggias.</meta>

# BOOK
# ORDER

Because the Venetians delight greatly in buildings of the **Corinthian** <term>Corinthian: The most ornate of the five classical orders of architecture, featuring slender columns and capitals decorated with acanthus leaves.</term> order, featuring many windows and numerous balconies, I have designed one very rich in light and balconies. I have also chosen to place loggia upon loggia; these will provide more convenience than balconies alone, and the building will have a grander presence, for all things in which the sight can expand are always more satisfying.

The division of the following facade shall be such that its width is divided into 30 parts. One of these will be the thickness of a column. The central intercolumniation <term>intercolumniation: The open space between two adjacent columns.</term> shall be four columns wide, while all the others shall be three; in this manner, the 30 parts shall be distributed. The height of the columns shall be 10 and a half parts, including the bases and the capitals. The architrave, the frieze, and the cornice <note>These three elements together form the "entablature," the horizontal structure supported by the columns.</note> should be one-fifth the height of the column. Having divided the members as stated previously, the width of the windows should be one and a half columns, all aligned vertically from top to bottom. However, the height of the first windows at the bottom should be three parts in width and four in height. Those above, which will serve the mezzanine rooms, shall be made in diagonal proportion <note>original: "proportion diagonea"; likely a ratio of 1 to the square root of 2, derived from the diagonal of a square.</note>. The width of the door shall be two column thicknesses, and its height four. The pilaster, the frieze, and the cornice should be as described for the others; thus, the cornice of this door will be level with the bottom windows. 

The second order should be one-fourth lower than the first; however, once the podium is made with balusters as high as a window opening is wide, the remaining height should be divided into 5 parts. One part shall be given to the architrave, the frieze, and the cornice; the remaining 4 shall be for the column with its base and capital. The height of the windows shall be two squares high. For the rest of the ornaments, one should proceed as with previous examples of similar subjects. Thus, the door of the loggia shall be like the one below, and the windows likewise. 

The third order shall be reduced from the second by one-fourth, each member reduced by its proportionate part, except for the height of the windows, which shall be made two squares high—or rather more than less—because their height naturally appears smaller from a distance. The central elevation should be reduced in height by a quarter, as said of the others. The architrave, frieze, and cornice should be one-fourth of that height, and the pediment shall be made in the manner described for the **Doric** <term>Doric: The earliest and simplest of the classical orders, characterized by heavy columns and plain, saucer-shaped capitals.</term> temple. If any other measurements remain, one must always refer back to the first rule. 

This building would serve not only as a residence according to the custom of Venice, but would also be very suitable for a villa and of great appearance. And if it is to be built as a villa, the higher it is raised from the ground, the more presence it will have, and the subterranean rooms will be healthier. Below, I will not show any floor plan for this facade, because the perspective of the loggias clearly demonstrates everything.

<summary>The author describes a multi-story Venetian-style facade using the Corinthian order, providing specific mathematical proportions for columns, windows, and loggias. He emphasizes that this airy, window-rich design is suited for both city residences and grand country villas.</summary>

<keywords>Venice, Corinthian order, loggia, architectural proportions, windows, balconies, Doric temple, villa design, classical orders</keywords>

...they of such a height as is the width of a window's light; the remainder of the height shall be divided into five parts. One shall be given to the architrave, the frieze, & the cornice. Three shall be for the column with the base, and the capital. The height of the windows shall be of two squares. The remainder of the ornaments shall be made as the things previously described for similar subjects: and thus the door of the loggia shall be like the one below, and similarly the windows. The third order shall be diminished from the second by one fourth, member by member its proportionate part, except for the height of the windows, which shall be made of two squares, and rather more, than less, for their height, which diminishes itself considerably. The middle elevation shall be of height diminished by one fourth, as has been said of the others. The architrave, the frieze, and the cornice shall be of that height one fourth, and the entasis shall be made in the manner that was said for that of the Doric temple: and if other measurements remain to us, one must always refer back to the first rule. And this building would not only serve for one according to the custom of Venice; but for a villa it would be very appropriate, and of grand effect. And if it is to be made for a villa, the more it is elevated from the ground, the greater presence it will have, and the subterranean rooms will be healthier. Below I will not show any plan of this following facade, because the perspective of the loggias clearly shows everything.

--- Page 116 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of architectural proportions and facade design, following the previous page's focus on Venetian villa layouts and the diminishing heights of successive building orders.</meta>

-># BOOK<-
-># ORDER<-

It may happen, as I have mentioned at other times, that an Architect will have a great number of columns of such thinness that, when he wishes to design a building for the convenience and needs of a patron who intends to spend money to decorate said building, these columns will not be suitable for the structure unless the industry and art of the Architect are such that he knows how to make use of them. The composition of this facade shall be such that the opening of an arch is twice as high as it is wide, and the pillar that supports the arches should have a front width equal to half of that opening's width. But if this width is divided into three and a half parts, one part shall represent the thickness of a column. The space between the two columns shall be equal to half a column, and the same for the pilasters. The height of the Pedestal without the lower Plinth, called the <term>zocco</term><note>The 'zocco' or socle is the plain block forming the lowest part of a base.</note>, shall be equal to the front of the pillar, with its parts divided as previously described for the Corinthian Pedestal.

The height of the columns, including the bases and the capitals, shall be eleven parts. This height will not be considered a defect, despite the columns being "geminate" <note>original: "geminate"; meaning paired or doubled columns placed closely together.</note> and almost joined as one, because they are placed in such a position more for ornament than to support any great weight. The height of the architrave, frieze, and cornice shall be one-fourth the height of the columns. All these members should project forward <note>The text uses the term 'risaltati', referring to an entablature that breaks forward to follow the line of the columns rather than running in a straight line.</note> directly above the columns, except for the corona and the <term>Sima</term><note>The uppermost molding of a cornice, often acting as a gutter.</note>, which should run continuously without being interrupted, as the good ancients used to do. Also, Bramante <note>Donato Bramante (1444–1514), a key architect of the High Renaissance.</note>, the light of good Architecture in this century, did a similar thing at the Belvedere in Rome.

The width of the door shall be four column-thicknesses, and its height twice that. The pilaster and the frieze should be designed so that the cornice supporting the arch also serves for the door, and similarly for the windows. The width of these windows shall be three column-thicknesses, and their height shall be five.

The second order should be reduced from the first by one-fourth, but the total height is to be divided into six parts. One part shall be given to the <term>podio</term><note>A platform or continuous pedestal.</note> called a <term>parapetto</term> [parapet]. Four parts shall be for the window openings, and the remaining part shall be for the architrave, frieze, and cornice, divided in the manner found in the Composite order. The width of the windows should align vertically with those below, and their height should be twice their width. As for the rest of the ornaments, both for the windows and the niches, let them be made as shown for the Ionic door, which is similar to these. When these are worked with greater delicacy and more ornaments, it will result in a Corinthian work.

The width of the niche including its pilasters shall align vertically with the columns of the upper part; if this width is divided into seven parts, five shall be for the niche itself and the remaining two for the pilasters. Its height shall be three times its width because, being at a great height, it will appear shorter due to the distance. The small pilasters <note>original: "pilastrelli"</note> above the cornice are made for ornament, but also for utility; for wherever chimneys are required, these can serve that need.

<header>CORINTHIAN</header>

--- Page 118 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Corinthian order from the previous leaf. While the prior page defined the specific measurements of the Corinthian capital and column, this page applies those principles to the design of a monumental sacred temple elevated on a high, rustic base.</meta>

### BOOK / ORDER

In those things made according to common usage, even if they are crafted with all the correct proportions and measures, they are certainly praised, but never admired. However, those things that are unusual—if they are made with reason and are well-proportioned—will not only be praised by the majority but also admired. For this reason, the present building, which represents a **sacred temple**, shall first be made of a **rustic base** <note>original: "sodo rustico," referring to solid, heavy masonry with a rough-hewn finish, often used for foundations to suggest strength</note> in the manner shown, of whatever height the location and site require. But let it be no less than the height of two men. One ascends to this level beginning at step **A**, which will be at the entrance, and climbing up to **B**. There shall be the platform where the temple will have a wide **ambulatory** <note>original: "ambulation," a covered walkway or terrace for walking around a building</note> around it, with its **platforms** <term>podio: a continuous projecting base or pedestal supporting a building or a series of columns</term> called **parapets**. 

This temple will be raised from said platform three steps above the height of the podium; and to climb to it, one will begin at step **C**, ascending to the level **D**, which will be the height of the podium, with another podium which will be higher than the one below. From this level to that of the temple, there shall be three steps. The width of this facade will be divided into 24 parts. Let one of these be for the **thickness of a column**. To the middle **intercolumniation** <term>intercolunnio: the clear space between two adjacent columns</term>, four parts shall be given. To those on the sides, where the windows are placed, two shall be given to each. To those where the **niches** are placed, one and a half shall be given to each; and thus the 24 parts shall be distributed. 

The same **pedestals** that are on the outer podium shall also be made under the columns of the facade. Their height, without the **plinth** <term>zocco: the square block at the very bottom of a column's base or a pedestal</term> of its base, will be three parts. The height of the columns, with their **bases** and with their **capitals**, will be ten and a half parts. The **architrave**, the **frieze**, and the **cornice** <note>Together, these three horizontal elements form the entablature</note> will be a fourth part of the height, as was said in the first order; and all the members shall be partitioned in that manner. 

The width of the **door** will be three parts. Its height will be seven and a half parts, which is two and a half squares. Because of its height, it will appear shorter to the sight of someone looking from below <note>The author is describing an "optical correction," where the architect intentionally elongates a feature so it looks "correct" when viewed from a low angle</note>. The width of the **windows** will be one and a half parts, but their height will be more than two squares, for the aforementioned loss of height in perspective. The width of the **niches** shall be one part, and their height will be tripled for the same reasons. 

The **order** that supports the **pediment** <term>fastigio: the triangular gable forming the roof-end of a Greek or Roman-style building</term> should be as high as the pedestal below. The cornice should be a fourth part of that height, and the cornice at the base of the **dome** <term>cuppola: a rounded vault or roof</term> should be of the same height. The dome will be as much larger than a semi-circle as is taken away by the projections of the cornices. At the four corners of the temple, for great ornament, four **obelisks** may be made. Their height, without the tip, should be level with the start of the pediment, and their tip level with that of the pediment. This pediment will be made according to the rule stated for the **Doric temple**. The lower parts beneath the temple shall be for certain places of prayer called **confessionals** <note>original: "confessionali," here referring to a subterranean chapel or crypt, often containing a martyr's tomb, located directly beneath the main altar</note>, of which I have seen many in many places in Italy beneath the high altars.

<summary>This page describes the design and specific proportions for a Corinthian temple elevated on a monumental rustic podium, emphasizing how to adjust measurements for perspective when viewed from below. It also notes the inclusion of "confessionals" (crypts) beneath the main structure, citing Italian architectural tradition.</summary>

<meta>Continuing from page LVIII, which introduced the principles of the Corinthian order. This page focuses on the practical application of the order in the design of triumphal arches for ceremonial entrances.</meta>

# BOOK ON THE ORDERS

Even though in our times triumphal arches are no longer made of marble or other stones, nevertheless, when some great personage makes an entrance into a city—either in passing through or to take possession of it—triumphal arches of various styles, decorated with painting, are constructed for them in the most beautiful locations of that city. Therefore, if one wishes to create a Corinthian arch of a certain appearance, its proportion and form shall be as follows: the opening <note>the central passageway</note> should be two squares high plus one-sixth more. The thickness of the column should be one-fifth of the width of the opening. The height of the pedestal should be three column thicknesses. The height of the column will be ten and a half parts. The architrave, frieze, and cornice together should be one-quarter of the height of the column. 

Thus, from the underside of the arch <note>the soffit</note> to the underside of the architrave, there will be a console <note>a decorative bracket or corbel</note> with a height of two column thicknesses, and its lines shall be drawn toward the center of the arch. Regarding the particular members—namely the pedestal, base, capital, architrave, frieze, and cornice—one should observe the rule given at the beginning of this order. However, the pilaster of the arch will be half a column [thick], and the space between one column and the next will be one and a half columns. The niche should be as wide as one column, and its height shall be three times its width, so that it is capable of holding a standing figure.

The height of the second order <note>the attic or upper level</note> shall be determined by dividing the column (excluding the pedestal) into four parts up to the top of the cornice; one of these parts shall be said height. If this height is then divided into four parts, one shall be for the upper cornice; the partition of which can be adapted from the Doric capital, varying the members. The height of the bases should be raised above the cornice by the same amount as the thickness of the column at its base, because the projection <note>proiettura</note> of the column obscures everything that remains beneath those bases. 

The cornices will project <note>original: risaltarãno—referring to the way the molding "breaks" or steps forward over the columns</note> as shown in the drawing. The height of the pediment <term>fastigio</term> <note>the triangular gable crowning the top of the structure</note> will be made according to one of the rules given in the Doric section. This present arch is in part similar to the one at Ancona <note>The Arch of Trajan in Ancona, a famous Roman landmark</note>, but with great reverence for such a great Architect, I have reduced the measurements to a general rule, so that everyone may easily [use] such mea- <note>The text cuts off mid-word: "misure" (measurements)</note>

<column-break/>

--- Page 122 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Corinthian order from page LIX, moving from the mathematical proportions of columns and pedestals to their practical application in interior architectural elements like fireplaces.</meta>

# BOOK ON THE ORDERS

I have treated as much as seemed necessary regarding the Corinthian manner, although many other ornaments could be discussed; however, the ornamentation of fireplaces is very necessary because of the constant need for them, as one cannot do without them. Indeed, even in every small room it is customary to build a fire; in such narrow places, these fireplaces built entirely into the depth of the wall are used, called French fireplaces <note>original: "camini Franceschi"; these were fireplaces flush with the wall, a style Serlio identified with French architecture compared to the projecting Italian style</note>, which could be decorated with various ornaments of Corinthian work. 

But if it is to be made in this form, its opening shall be made of such a size that is appropriate for the room where it will be placed. The pilaster <term>pilastrata: a rectangular vertical column integrated into a wall</term> shall be one-sixth of the width of the opening, and the whole work will be even more elegant if it is one-eighth. This pilaster shall be divided in the same manner as was described for the Corinthian architrave. The frieze above it—because it is to be sculpted—shall be made one-fourth larger than the said pilaster. The entire cornice, including the part that projects over the brackets <note>original: "mensule"</note>, shall be as large as the pilaster, divided into three parts as was described for the Corinthian cornice, although from the perspective of looking at it from below it will appear to have greater height. 

The front of the brackets—or scrolls <note>original: "cartelle"</note> if we wish to call them that—shall be at the top as wide as the pilaster; but the bottom part, which will be level with the lower opening, should be one-fourth smaller. From these brackets hang two leaves, as is shown in the drawing. Its painting <note>original: "piettura," likely a typo for "pittura" (painting) or "sportura" (projection)</note> shall be at the discretion of the Architect. Regarding the ornament above the cornice, whether to make it or not does not matter much. And this design will serve not only to decorate a fireplace, but could also be used for a door or other ornament; furthermore, a pediment <term>frontespicio: a decorative gable, often triangular or arched, placed above an entrance</term> above it will suit it well when it is used for a door.

<summary>This page explains how to apply Corinthian architectural proportions to the design of "French-style" fireplaces that are recessed into the wall. It provides specific ratios for the pilasters, friezes, and decorative brackets, noting that the design can also be adapted for doorways.</summary>

in such narrow places, fireplaces are used, all within the wall, called French fireplaces, to which various ornaments of Corinthian work could be made. # BOOK / ORDER

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial letter 'P' depicting a person seated at a desk, likely an architect or scholar at work.</image-desc>

*Because in a hall, or in a great chamber, one also seeks a fireplace proportioned to that room,*
*to which a large opening is necessary, for which, if one wishes to make the modillions* <note>modiglioni: decorative brackets or corbels that support a projecting weight</note> *sufficient for such a*
*projection, they would occupy two spaces on the sides; but in such a subject, I intend a flat column of*
*low relief, and separate from that, a round column in such a manner that between one column and the other*
*there remains a space, and in this way, it will provide convenience and ornament. And because (as I said at the*
*beginning of this chapter) the Corinthian style had its origin from a Corinthian virgin; I wanted to imitate her,*
*placing her as a column.* <note>The author refers to the legend recorded by the Roman architect Vitruvius, where the Corinthian capital was inspired by a basket of toys left on a young girl's grave, overgrown with acanthus leaves.</note> *Once the height and width of the fireplace are established, according to the place where it*
*will be made, let the height be measured in 9 parts, and one of these will be for the head of the young woman; and thus the whole*
*figure is formed and draped, as is shown; the flat column* <note>pilaster</note> *shall be made of the same proportion, observing the given*
*measurements from the beginning. Above the columns let the architrave, the frieze, and the cornice be placed. The height of the whole shall be the*
*fourth part of the column, divided in the manner said from the beginning. From the cornice upwards, according to the room and*
*its height, it can be decorated in the manner shown here before. And who can doubt that sometimes this invention might*
*not be suitable for decorating a door: by leaning these similar columns against the wall, and especially for the*
*gate of a garden, or for places of triumphs, and even for other ornaments, the judicious Architect*
*will always know how to adapt them.*

The Corinthian Work. The Composite begins.

--- Page 126 ---
<meta>This page continues from the discussion of the Corinthian order, introducing the final of the five orders: the Composite.</meta>

# BOOK OF THE COMPOSITE ORDER. CH. IX.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'V' containing a portrait of a bearded man's head in a circular frame surrounded by ornate scrollwork and foliage.</image-desc>

Although **V**itruvius has taught us that there are four manners of columns—that is, **D**oric, **I**onic, **C**orinthian, and **T**uscan—giving us, as it were, the first and simple elements of **A**rchitecture; nevertheless, I have wished to add to the aforementioned a sort of fifth manner, mixed from these simple ones. I am moved to do so by the authority of the works of the **R**omans, which can be seen with one's own eyes. Indeed, the prudence of the craftsman should be such that, according to the need, he must often make a mixture of the aforementioned simple styles, regarding the nature of the subject. Furthermore, in this area, the **A**rchitect (to whose judgment various subjects will come) will sometimes be abandoned by the counsel of **V**itruvius, who was not able to embrace everything. Because of this, he will be compelled to rely on his own opinion. For **V**itruvius does not speak in any place, in my view, of this **C**omposite work, called by some the **L**atin work, while others call it **I**talic. The ancient **R**omans—perhaps being unable to surpass the invention of the **G**reeks, who were the discoverers of the **D**oric column (modeled after the man), the **I**onic (modeled after the matron), and the **C**orinthian (taking its form from **V**irgins)—created a composition of the **I**onic and **C**orinthian. They placed the **I**onic <term>volute</term> <note>the spiral, scroll-like ornament</note> along with the <term>ovolo</term> <note>original: "vuouolo"; an egg-shaped molding</note> into the **C**orinthian capital, and they used this more for triumphal arches than for anything else. And this they did with very good counsel; for, triumphing over all those lands from which these works had originated, they could, at their pleasure as the masters of those lands, put them together. This they did at the great building of the **C**olosseum in **R**ome: having placed three orders one above the other—that is, **D**oric, **I**onic, and **C**orinthian—they placed above all of them this **C**omposite work, which is so called by everyone, although, as far as can be seen, the capitals are **C**orinthian. But it was a fine judgment, in my opinion, that having placed such an order in the highest part of the **C**olosseum (which is very far from the eye of the viewers), it would have happened that if they had placed the architrave, frieze, and cornice of the **I**onic or **C**orinthian above the column, such a work would have appeared poor because of the great distance. But by placing <term>modillions</term> <note>decorative brackets used to support the upper part of the cornice</note> in the frieze, it made the work rich and helped the appearance of the <term>corona</term> <note>the flat, vertical face of the upper cornice</note>. It also achieved another effect: that the architrave, frieze, and cornice appeared as a single cornice because of the modillions that were placed in the frieze in such a way that it represented grandeur while observing its proportion.

The height of this **C**omposite column, including the base and the capital, shall be 10 parts. Let its base be half the thickness of the column, and it shall be made **C**orinthian, using the measurements given for the **C**orinthian; this can also be seen on the Arch of **T**itus **V**espasian in **R**ome. The column can be fluted like the **I**onic, and sometimes like the **C**orinthian, at the pleasure of the **A**rchitect. The capital can be made according to the rules given for the **C**orinthian, making the volutes somewhat larger than the **C**orinthian <term>cauliculi</term> <note>original: "caulicoli"; the stalks or stems supporting the volutes in a capital</note>; this capital is seen on the aforementioned arch and is shown here to the side. As for the architrave, frieze, and cornice, if they are far from the sight, the architrave shall be as high as the column is thick at the top. The frieze where the modillions are located should be of the same height. The <term>cymatium</term> <note>original: "cimatio"; a molding with a wave-like profile</note> of the modillions shall be a sixth part of them. The projection of the modillions should be equal to their height. The height of the corona with its cymatium shall be equal to the architrave and divided into two parts: one shall be the corona, the other the cymatium. Its projection shall be as much as its height, and this is a general rule. However, in the following figure marked **C**, one can see the members and measurements of the one at the aforementioned **C**olosseum. And because this column is more slender than all the others, it also requires a pedestal of greater delicacy than the others as a resulting general rule: the height of which shall be in double proportion to its width—that is, the <term>die</term> <note>original: "netto"; the main square block of a pedestal</note>—and its height shall be divided into eight parts. One of these shall be given to its base, and just as much to its cap. But for the particular members, one may take the example shown here to the side, which are proportioned and taken from the pedestals of the aforementioned arch. Thus, the column being 10 parts, the pedestal itself will be 10 parts, proportioned to the column. And although all pedestals are made vertically straight, nevertheless in **A**thens, a most ancient city, there are some that are slightly diminished <note>tapered</note> at the top, a thing which I do not find fault with.

<summary>This page introduces the Composite Order, a Roman architectural innovation that combines elements of the Ionic and Corinthian styles. It details the order's proportions and provides historical examples such as the Colosseum and the Arch of Titus to justify its use in grand or distant structures.</summary>

# BOOK
## ORDER

<margin>Advice</margin>

Because the ancient Romans created various mixtures, I will choose some of the most well-known and best understood, so that the Architect may, with his good judgment and according to circumstances, choose that which will be most useful to his purpose. The capital marked **T** below is composed of Doric, Ionic, and Corinthian elements. The <term>abacus</term> <note>the flat slab forming the topmost member of a capital</note> and the <term>cymatium</term> <note>a molding with a double-curve profile</note> are Doric; the <term>ovolo</term> <note>a rounded, egg-shaped molding</note> and the flutings are Ionic; the <term>astragals</term> <note>small, semi-circular moldings often resembling beads</note> and the leaves are Corinthian. Similarly, its base is Doric due to the two <term>tori</term> <note>large, convex semi-circular moldings at the base of a column</note>, but because of the two <term>scotiae</term> <note>concave moldings between the tori</note>, the astragal, and the delicate workmanship, it is denoted as Corinthian. These examples are found in <note>original: "Trastevere"</note> Trastevere in Rome. The capital marked **X**, and likewise the base, are of two species: Doric and Corinthian. The abacus of the capital and also the base are Doric; however, the base, due to the delicacy of the carvings, can be called Corinthian, and likewise the leaves of the capital are of the Corinthian style. Because the abacus is square while all the other members are circular, <term>rosettes</term> <note>rose-shaped decorative ornaments</note> shall be carved at the four corners under the abacus, as demonstrated below. The capital marked **A**, because of the horse-monster used in place of the <term>cauliculus</term> <note>the stalks from which the acanthus leaves of a Corinthian capital emerge</note>, can be called Composite; it is found at the <note>original: "Basilica del Foro transitorio"</note> Basilica of the Transitory Forum, also known as the Forum of Nerva. The flutings of the column are different from the others, as can be seen under the **A**. The base marked **X** is Composite and is in Rome. The capital marked **B** is pure Corinthian and is found at the <note>original: "tre colonne"</note> three columns—a most beautiful work—beside the Colosseum. The capital marked **C** is a composite of Ionic and Corinthian from a triumphal arch in Verona. The capital marked **D** is at the same arch, depicted in <term>bas-relief</term> <note>low-profile sculpture</note> on some flat columns or pilasters. The base marked **Y** is composite because of the astragal that sits above the upper torus, and it is an antique found in Rome.

->B O O K / O R D E R<-

LXXI

<note>Decorative woodcut initial 'D' containing a landscape with a small gabled building and trees.</note> *Of this* **Composite** *work, one does not see many buildings, except for triumphal arches, and even the majority of those are made from the spoils* <term>spolia: materials or decorative elements scavenged from older ruins</term> *of other buildings: nonetheless, having given a general rule for it, I will not make other inventions for buildings of this kind. This is because the prudent* **Architect**, *according to the circumstances, will be able to make use of the past inventions, transforming them into the* **Composite** *work. But because I wish to demonstrate two types of* **fireplaces** *for every order—one entirely within the wall, the other protruding from the wall—this present fireplace, which is to be entirely within the wall, if made in a small place, its height shall be at the shoulder of a man: so that the face and the eyes are not harmed by the fire, and the width shall be according to the needs of the space. Let the height beneath the architrave be divided into four parts, and one part shall be the front of the pilaster-strip, worked in the manner seen in the drawing. And because this* **Composite** *work is more licentious* <note>In this context, 'licentious' refers to a creative freedom or "license" to deviate from the strict rules of the more ancient Greek orders.</note> *than the others, I have made these pilaster-strips very different from the others, more by a whim* <note>original: "capriccio"</note> *than by strict reason, at the pleasure of whoever wishes to use them; I have, however, taken part of this invention from an ancient chair* <term>cathedra: an ancient formal seat or throne</term> *that is at* **Saint John Lateran** *in* **Rome**. *The architrave shall be half the width of the pilaster-strip. Its cymatium* <term>cymatium: a wave-shaped molding</term> *shall be one-sixth part of that. The remainder shall be divided into seven parts: three shall be given to the first face, and four shall be for the second. The astragal* <term>astragal: a small convex molding, often shaped like a string of beads</term> *shall be half a part, shared between one side and the other. The frieze, because it is carved, shall be made a fourth part larger than the architrave. Let the cornice be as large as the architrave, and it shall be made of seven parts: two shall be given to the cymatium below the corona, two shall be given to the corona* <term>corona: the flat, vertical "drip" part of a cornice</term>, *one shall be for the cymatium above it, and the two remaining shall be for the sima* <term>sima: the uppermost gutter-like molding of a cornice</term>, *and the projection of the whole shall be as much as its height. But if the pilaster-strip is made with its height at six parts, and the other smaller members are scaled proportionally, the work will turn out more graceful, especially if the work is small in size; the ornaments above the cornice may be included or omitted according to the will of the patron of the work.*
