--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Composite Order from the previous page, transitioning from the general description of the order to its specific application in domestic architecture—specifically, the design of ornamental fireplaces.</meta>

# BOOK ON THE ORDERS

**Other** fireplace ornaments could be made of this **Composite** work, and in various forms, because this manner is more licentious <note>In architectural theory, "licentious" (licenziosa) refers to a style that takes creative liberties and deviates from strict classical rules.</note> than the other ways of building. Thus, to vary it from other forms, the present one can be made according to this rule: the height of the architrave <term>architrave: the main beam resting across the top of the columns or opening</term> should be as tall as a man of average stature. This height is then divided into eight parts, one of which shall be for the face of the modillion <term>modiglione: a decorative bracket or console used to support a weight</term>, or "cartouche" <note>original: cartella</note> as we might wish to call it. The height of the pedestal should be at a comfortable height for sitting. 

The order above the modillions, which follows no specific rule, shall be two and a half parts of the face of the modillion. Because (as I have said) this feature is outside of the standard rules, the design of the leaves and the other members shall be at the discretion of the architect. One could also certainly place **Doric**, **Ionic**, or sometimes **Corinthian** work above these modillions, following the rules given at the beginning of those sections. To ensure that the throat <note>original: gola; refers to the internal flue of the chimney</note> which receives the smoke is more spacious, one can add that small order above, which gives it a more beautiful form than the usual style, which is shaped like a pyramid.

<meta>The previous page concluded the discussion on the Composite order. This page begins a general treatise on the "Book of Orders," specifically discussing the placement, proportions, and variations of columns when used in different architectural contexts, such as against walls or stacked in multiple tiers.</meta>

# BOOK ON THE ORDERS

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'G' at the start of the text, containing a detailed illustration of a man (likely a scholar or architect) seated and examining a book or scroll with a drafting tool, set against a background of classical architectural elements.</image-desc>

**G**reat judgment truly must the **Architect** possess, given the diversity of compositions and ornaments of buildings. There are certain parts of architecture where fixed rules can be given because they are not "accidents" <note>In this context, "accidents" refers to unpredictable variations or situational changes.</note> that occur outside our expectations. Indeed, every day we see columns which, by their various positions, demonstrate different measurements according to the places where they stand. These variations are given to buildings in one of four ways: 

First, by placing the column almost like an island, without any companion support at the side or back; these truly carry a great weight and do not exceed the height of the given rules. An example of this is shown in the first column, **A**. 

Second, by leaning them against the wall in full relief <note>The column is fully round but attached to the wall.</note>. Supported by this attachment, they can rise one thickness <note>The "thickness" (groſſezza) refers to the diameter of the column at its base, which architects use as a standard unit of measurement.</note> higher than the aforementioned ones; this is shown in the second column, **B**. 

Third, by pulling them only two-thirds out from the wall. These could go higher than the others by one thickness, and even more. In some buildings, such columns are seen reaching a height of around nine and a half thicknesses, especially at the **Colosseum** in **Rome** in the **Doric** work, as shown in the third column, **C**. These are aided even more when they have parastades or pilasters <note>Rectangular supports or decorative pillars standing against a wall.</note> at their sides; which, by carrying all the weight, allow the Architect the convenience of making the columns more slender—so slender, in fact, that they may be judged to occupy that place more for ornament than for support. 

One can also pull a column two-thirds out from the wall and place a half-flat column on each side. These half-columns will give such support to the middle one that its height can be raised one thickness more than the others. In this case, the **architrave**, the **frieze**, and the **cornice** <note>These three parts together make up the "entablature," the horizontal structure that rests on the columns.</note> can project outward over the round or flat column. Because the half-columns will support the architrave, frieze, and cornice from the sides; however, it is a defect to make such work project outward over a single column alone, because the other parts at the sides would be abandoned without being supported by anything. An example of this is shown in the fourth column, **D**.

But when columns must support some weight without help from others, and have appropriate intercolumniation <note>The spacing between columns.</note>, it will not be permitted to exceed the limits; rather, they must support order upon order. It will be reasonable to make them more robust so that the building achieves greater permanence. Even though the **pedestal** is a great support and help in raising columns, nonetheless, if the columns themselves are of such height that they satisfy the needs of the building, I would praise them being free of pedestals, especially those of the first orders <note>The ground floor or bottom-most level of columns.</note>. 

But for the second and third orders, because of the *podij* (called parapets) and also to exalt the columns to a greater height, they are seen to turn out better with pedestals. One sees that the ancient **Romans** observed this in theaters and amphitheaters. Regarding the positioning of columns above columns, there are various reasons and authorities. 

The first reasoning would be that the projection of the pedestal of the upper column should not go further out than the thickness of the column below. The projection of the column's base should rest upon the "live" part <note>The "live" part (viuo) refers to the solid vertical face or shaft of the pedestal, rather than its decorative moldings.</note> of its pedestal; this would truly be a very secure and well-founded method. However, because this second order would diminish greatly compared to the first, no other order should be placed above it due to the great reduction that would follow. 

The other reasoning, and more to the purpose, is this: the front of the pedestal should be at least plumb with the column below, and upon this pedestal, one should place the column diminished by a fourth part of the one below, both in thickness and in height. The projection of the base should be as wide as the front of the pedestal. This rule conforms with the one **Vitruvius** <note>Marcus Vitruvius Pollio, the most famous Roman author on architecture.</note> gives for the theater, which is demonstrated above column **A**. 

And if one wishes to make the columns less diminished, one can make the bottom part of the upper column the same thickness as the top part of the lower one. But in this case, the face of the pedestal will come out further than the face of the column; nonetheless, those of the **Theater of Marcellus** produce such an effect; the example of this is seen above column **B**. 

These three reasons are quite probable. But the ancient Romans, at the great structure of the **Colosseum**, made the **Ionic**, the **Corinthian**, and the **Composite** columns all three of the same thickness, and they made the **Doric** below them only thicker by about a twentieth part. In my view, they did this with very good counsel; for if all the columns were diminished by a fourth part one above the other, those last ones at the top would have appeared very small to the viewers due to the long distance in such a tall building. In that structure, they are seen to correspond very well for its height; the demonstration of this is seen above column **C**. 

And likewise, the column above column **D** is diminished from the one below by a fourth part. Therefore, if a building of modest size is to be made with three orders of columns, I will always praise that each order be diminished by a fourth part, as I have said in the past pages. But if the building is of great height, one can keep to the order of the Colosseum: where the Doric, the Ionic, and the Corinthian are roughly of one height, but the order above increases in height by about a fifth part. This is (as I have said) because of its distance; due to the great distance, that part comes to appear the same height as the other orders. And although the demonstration of these columns is Doric, nonetheless this reasoning is understood to apply to all manners of columns proportionately.

<summary>This page discusses the practical and aesthetic rules for placing columns in various architectural settings, including their attachment to walls and the principles for stacking different architectural orders (Doric, Ionic, etc.) in tall buildings like the Colosseum.</summary>
<keywords>architectural orders, column proportions, pedestals, Vitruvius, Colosseum, Roman architecture, entablature, intercolumniation</keywords>

--- Page 135 ---
And these three reasons are quite probable. But the ancient Romans, in the great building of the Colosseum, made the Ionic, Corinthian, and Composite columns all three of one thickness, and the Doric below them they made only thicker by about the twentieth part: and this, in my opinion, they did with very good counsel, because if all the columns were diminished by a quarter part one above the other; those uppermost would, due to the long distance, appear very small to observers in such a tall building, in which they are seen to correspond very well due to their height, the demonstration of this is seen above column C. And thus the column above column D is diminished by a quarter part from the one below: for which reason, if a mediocre building is to be made with three orders of columns; I will always praise that each order be diminished by a quarter part, as I have said in the past facades. But if the building will be of great height, one can follow the order of the Colosseum: that the Doric, Ionic, and Corinthian orders are approximately of one height, but the order above increases in height by about the fifth part, and this is (as I have said) because of its distance, which part, due to the great distance, comes to appear of the height of the other orders, and even though the demonstration of these columns is Doric; nevertheless, this reasoning applies proportionally to all types of columns.

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of architectural principles, moving from the proportions of column orders (discussed on the previous page) to the practical methods of construction and the structural integration of natural stone and brickwork.</meta>

->**BOOK**<-
->**ORDER**<-

Having treated so many and various ornaments of stone; it is a fitting thing that I should also treat how they ought to be put into work, and especially when one has to accompany <term>natural stones</term> <note>original: *pietre vive*, literally "living stones," referring to quarried ashlar or hard stone</note> with <term>bricks</term> <note>original: *pietre cotte*, literally "cooked stones"</note>; which things require great diligence and art: because the bricks are the flesh of the building, and the natural stones are the bones that support it. If these two things are not well connected together, in the process of time they will fail: and therefore, once the foundation is made in those proper ways required by the site, the wise Architect must have prepared and worked all the natural stones, and also the bricks, with the other materials needed for the building, and thus at one time come building and connecting the natural stones together with the bricks. The natural stones must enter so far into the wall that, even if there were no <term>mortar</term> <note>original: *calcina*, lime-based mortar</note> to hold them together, one could judge that they would stand firm in the wall by themselves; by doing this, the works will attain some degree of permanence. The example of this is seen here to the side in figure **A.** where it is demonstrated how one can make balconies outside of windows without any defect, making the first order of <term>rusticated work</term> <note>rough-hewn, textured masonry</note>; and if it is also made of delicate work, one may follow this method, provided the first wall is of such thickness that it creates the floor for the said balconies. And if bases or pedestals with columns above them are to be made where natural stones and bricks meet, as I said above, if the natural stones are not well bound and <term>inset</term> with the bricks, as seen in the figure marked **B.** the works will not last long. And if the columns are made of several pieces, it will be well that some of them—namely the smaller ones—enter further into the wall to more securely support the others. But if the columns are of a single piece, they should be at least a third part into the wall. However, the bases and the capitals should be made in such a manner that they enter much further into the wall: and above all, the <term>coronas</term> <note>the projecting part of a cornice</note> and other moldings that project out from the wall must enter so far into the wall that the unworked part is of greater weight than the worked part, so that these things can stay in the work by themselves without other support. But if, due to a scarcity of stones or the great expense of marble and other fine stones, one wishes to <term>veneer</term> <note>original: *vestire*, literally "to clothe"</note> a facade or wall, it will be necessary for the prudent Architect, before beginning to build above ground, to have prepared all the natural and worked stones along with the other materials for such a need, and thus come building and connecting the natural stones with the bricks. I say that it will be necessary for some pieces to enter so far into the wall that they support the other thin pieces by means of certain <term>dovetail joints</term> <note>original: *incastri a coda di Rondena, o di Gazza*, literally "swallow-tail" or "magpie-tail" joints</note>, so that over time they cannot fall out from the others: which things must be put into place while the brick wall is being made, because of the aforementioned joints. But so that the brick wall does not settle <note>sink or compress</note>—for if it settles, the natural stones would be crushed by the weight from above—it will be necessary that the wall be made of well-squared bricks and the very best strained mortar, and there should be little mortar between the stones, which should be well pressed one upon the other. Above all, such works should not be done with haste, nor by adding weight upon weight too quickly; but let them settle somewhat from course to course: because if one wishes to do it with speed, placing weight upon it, it is a certain thing that the wall will settle somewhat, and the stones, being unable to bear the weight, will break. But if, from time to time, such works are built gradually, things will remain in their proper places. Nevertheless, I will always praise more the works that are entirely bound into the walls than the <term>facings</term> <note>original: *investigioni, o incrostationi*</note>, as we might call them, and especially on exterior facades, which in my opinion should not be done this way; for those few buildings made by the ancients that were covered in marble and other fine stones are seen today without their "skin," where only the mass of bricks remains, and even those are consumed by the years. But those buildings where the natural stones are bound with the bricks are still seen today in existence. Nonetheless, if such works are still desired, this seems to me the most secure way, although some Architects in various places in Italy have made some buildings of simple walling, leaving spaces for the natural stones, and then after some time have placed their ornaments there; however, because such things are not well bound into the walls, but almost attached with <term>glue</term> <note>original: *colla*</note>, one sees in many places that pieces have fallen, and every day they threaten ruin.

<summary>This page explains the structural necessity of bonding natural stone ("pietre vive") deeply into brickwork ("pietre cotte") to ensure longevity. The author warns against thin marble veneers or "gluing" ornaments onto finished walls, citing the decay of ancient Roman ruins as evidence.</summary>

<keywords>masonry techniques, natural stone, brickwork, structural bonding, dovetail joints, architectural settlement, veneers, construction durability, Vitruvian principles</keywords>

<header>FOURTH.</header>
<header>COMPOSITE.</header>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of architectural ornamentation from the previous section on the Composite order, shifting the focus from exterior stone masonry to the design and mechanics of doors.</meta>

# BOOK OF DOORS MADE OF WOOD OR BRONZE. CHAPTER X.

*As much as I thought necessary, I have discussed the ornaments of every kind of building regarding stonework; now I shall treat the doors that close buildings, whether made of wood or bronze. I will show several illustrations of them, though I will not otherwise dwell on the treatment of the hinges called* <term>cancani: heavy iron strap hinges or hooks common in the author’s time</term> *which support them, because in every part of the world—not only in Italy—every blacksmith knows how to provide a good account of them. Nevertheless, those hinges used in antiquity, which held the doors suspended as shown below in Figure A, placed less weight upon the buildings and were easier to close and open than those used throughout Italy today in the manner shown in Figure B.* <note>The "ancient" method refers to pivot hinges (socket hinges) where the door rotates on vertical pins set into the stone sill and lintel, rather than hanging off the side of the frame.</note> *But whether these doors be of bronze or wood, their ornaments shall be such that, just as the stone ornament is more solid, so too should that of the door be of the same solidity, to remain within its proper limits.* <note>The author is advocating for "decorum," the principle that the door's visual weight must match the architectural style of the entryway.</note> *And likewise, if the stone ornament is delicate, that of wood or bronze shall also be made with similar delicacy, the choice of which shall be at the discretion of the prudent Architect. To provide some light on such ornaments, you will see five* <term>inuentioni: creative designs or architectural "inventions"</term> *nearby, for the most part adapted from the ancients.*

->[Illustration comparing two hinge systems: **A** shows the ancient vertical pivot system integrated into the masonry; **B** shows the contemporary Italian strap-hinge system with decorative ironwork.]<-

<summary>This page introduces Chapter X, transitioning from stone ornamentation to the design of wooden and bronze doors, with a specific focus on the mechanical superiority of ancient pivot hinges over contemporary strap hinges.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of architectural elements, specifically focusing on the construction and material properties of doors, following the previous discussion on general proportions.</meta>

->BOOK<-
->ORDER<-

*Bronze doors are not always made from a single piece, for in those made that way, neither wood nor iron is necessary; rather, the hinges themselves are fashioned from that same piece. As for those made of wood and then covered with bronze of a suitable thickness—which, if they are made of boards joined side by side, even if they were well-fitted and reinforced together, the nature of timber is nonetheless always to shrink and swell according to dry or humid weather. If one wishes to make such a door that is to be covered in bronze or another metal, the most secure method is demonstrated here to the side in figures A and B <note>Reference to the diagrams in the original manuscript</note>, because timber never expands along its length, but always remains within its limits. These works can be made with whatever thickness or thinness the Architect <term>Architetto: the master builder or designer</term> deems appropriate, according to the weight they will have to carry. Thus, the empty spaces can be filled with the same wood, but always crossed in every direction <note>This refers to cross-lamination or cross-bracing to stabilize the wood</note>, so that they do not expand more in one direction than the other. And because all ancient doors—both public and private—are square in shape, except for those of cities and triumphal arches which are arched; nevertheless, in our times, many make them arched, perhaps for greater strength, and also because they suit certain purposes of the building well. I have wanted to demonstrate at least one invention <note>original: "inuentione"; here meaning a specific design or architectural solution</note>, because in truth one cannot always cover every case, since many accidents <note>unforeseen circumstances or site-specific challenges</note> occur where it behooves the Architect to be clever in the arrangements according to the subjects <note>original: "suggetti"; the specific requirements or themes of a project</note> that arise from day to day.*

# BOOK

## On the ornamentation of painting, outside and inside buildings. Chapter XI.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut drop-cap initial 'P'. Inside the square frame, a figure is seated on the ground beneath a tree in a landscape setting, appearing to look toward a distant hill.</image-desc>

**P**o as not to leave out any kind of ornament for which I might provide some rule, in painting as in other things, I say that the Architect must not only take care of the ornaments concerning stone and marble, but also those of the brush used to decorate the walls. It is proper that he be the director, as he is the master of all the colors used in the building. For there have been some painters, skilled in their practice but otherwise possessed of so little judgment that, to show off the beauty of their colors and ignoring all else, they have distorted and sometimes ruined an architectural order <note>The "order" refers to the classical systems of columns and proportions like Doric or Ionic.</note> by failing to consider how to place their paintings in their proper locations. Therefore, if a building’s facade must be decorated with the brush, it is certain that no opening that simulates the open air or landscapes is appropriate. Such things serve to "break" the building, transforming it from a solid, physical form into a transparent one lacking firmness, like an imperfect or ruined structure. Neither are colored figures or animals appropriate, unless one simulates a window where people might be seen—though they should be in quiet poses rather than vigorous movements. Similarly, one can include animals suitable for such places, as I said, at a window or atop a cornice. And if the owner of the work or the painter wishes to enjoy the beauty of colors without breaking or spoiling the architectural work as I mentioned above, they could simulate some cloths <note>original: "panni"</note> hanging on the wall as if they were movable objects. In these, one can paint whatever one likes; by doing so, the architectural order is not broken, and the simulation remains truthful while maintaining decorum. One could also, for the sake of triumph and celebration, use beautiful fiction to attach festoons of leaves, fruits, and flowers, as well as shields and trophies and other similar colored things that represent movable items. The background <note>original: "campo"</note> should be the same color as the wall itself; in this way, paintings in such places can exist without any criticism. But if one wishes to decorate a facade with the brush using sound judgment, one could simulate marble or other stone, carving into it whatever is desired. One could also simulate bronze figures in full relief within niches, or perhaps some historical scenes <note>original: "historiette"</note> also simulated in bronze. Doing so maintains the solidity of the work, making it worthy of praise from all those who know truth from falsehood. And in this, *Baldassare Peruzzi of Siena* <note>original: "Baldessar Petrucci Senese"</note> had the most beautiful judgment (as he always did in all his works). Wishing to decorate the facades of some palaces in *Rome* with the brush during the time of *Julius II*, he painted with his own hand some things simulating marble—namely sacrifices, battles, histories, and architectures. These not only kept the buildings solid and decorated but greatly enriched their presence. What shall I say of the fine cleverness of many others who delighted in decorating the buildings of *Rome* with the brush, who never used any color in such subjects other than <term>chiaroscuro</term> <note>A technique using only light and dark tones to simulate three-dimensional volume.</note>? Nonetheless, their works are of such quality and beauty that they strike wonder into any ingenious person who looks upon them. Such was *Polidoro da Caravaggio* and *Maturino*, his companion, who, to the honor of all other painters, decorated *Rome* so well with such paintings that no one else in our times has reached such a standard. Great foresight was also shown by *Dosso* and his brother, who, wishing to adorn some facades of the *Ducal Palace of Ferrara* with the brush, decorated them only in <term>chiaroscuro</term>, simulating architectures supported by figures rendered with great intelligence and admirable art. I will not expand upon many other judicious Italian painters who never used any color other than light and dark in such places, so as not to break the architectural order. But if one wishes to decorate the inside of buildings with paintings of various colors, it can be done with good judgment moved by reason. In the walls of loggias around gardens and courtyards, one may simulate some openings, and in those, create landscapes both near and far, skies, buildings, figures, animals, and whatever else is desired, all in color. This is because it simulates the truth: when looking out of buildings, all the aforementioned things can indeed be seen. Similarly, when decorating halls, chambers, or other ground-floor rooms with painting, the painter is allowed to simulate openings of air and landscapes within the architectural orders of the walls, provided they respect the height of such openings. For if they are higher than a person’s line of sight, one should see nothing but the sky, or perhaps the tops of mountains or buildings. And if one wishes to make something in higher places that simulates being open within solid walls, nothing but the sky should be seen through them; but if figures are made in such places, their placement shall be upon a line—

<sig>M 2</sig>

<vocab>Architecture, painting, light and dark (chiaroscuro), facade, Baldassare Peruzzi, Rome, Polidoro da Caravaggio, Maturino, Dosso, Ducal Palace of Ferrara</vocab>

<summary>This page outlines the rules for architectural painting, emphasizing that exterior decorations should maintain the building's perceived solidity by avoiding "holes" like simulated landscapes, unless using techniques like chiaroscuro or simulated hangings. It cites masters like Baldassare Peruzzi and Polidoro da Caravaggio as examples of painters who respected architectural integrity.</summary>
<keywords>architectural painting, facade decoration, chiaroscuro, Baldassare Peruzzi, Polidoro da Caravaggio, Ferrara, Rome, simulated architecture, decorum</keywords>

# BOOK FOUR

[li]ne, because in such a case the floor cannot be seen reasonably; and in this, Master Andrea Mantegna <note>Andrea Mantegna (c. 1431–1506) was a North Italian painter renowned for his mastery of perspective and interest in Roman antiquity.</note> was very wise and full of judgment in the **Triumphs of Caesar**, which he made in Mantua for the most liberal Marquis Francesco Gonzaga. In that work, because the feet of the figures are higher than our viewpoint, no ground plane is visible at all; instead, the figures (as I have said) stand upon a single line, yet are so well arranged that they perform their function admirably. Certainly, this painting of which I speak is to be celebrated and held in great esteem, for in it one sees the depth of design, the artful perspective, the marvelous invention, the innate discretion in the composition of the figures, and the extreme diligence in the finishing. 

And if the painter should wish at times to use the art of perspective to make a hall or other room appear longer, he may—on the wall facing the entrance—use certain architectural orders drawn with such art as to make it appear much longer than it actually is. This was done by Baldassarre <note>Baldassarre Peruzzi (1481–1536), a Sienese architect and painter who worked extensively in Rome.</note>, who was as learned in this art as anyone else in this century. Wishing to decorate a hall for Agostino Chigi <note>A wealthy Sienese banker and patron of the arts in Rome; the building mentioned is the Villa Farnesina.</note>, a noble merchant in Rome, he simulated with art some columns and other architecture for this purpose. The great Pietro Aretino <note>A famous and influential Italian author, playwright, and satirist known for his sharp art criticism.</note>, who was as judicious in painting as in poetry, had to say that there was no more perfect painting of its kind in that house, even though there are also works by the hand of the divine Raphael of Urbino. But what shall I say on this occasion of the stupendous and artful stage scenes made in Rome by the said Baldassarre? These were all the more worthy of praise because the expense to make them was less than others made before or even after them, thanks to his knowledge and intelligence.

Once the walls are decorated, if one wishes to decorate the **vaulted ceilings** <note>original: "Cieli voltati", literally "turned skies," a poetic term for ceilings.</note> in various ways, one should follow the tracks of the ancient Romans. They were accustomed to making different compartments according to the subjects and the shape of the vaults. Within these, they created various whimsicalities called **grotesques** <note>original: "grottesche". This style was inspired by ancient Roman wall paintings discovered in the "grottoes" or ruins of Nero's Golden House.</note>. These things turn out very well and are convenient because of the license one has to do whatever one wishes—such as foliage, branches, flowers, animals, birds, and figures of every sort mixed together. Sometimes, with animals and foliage separated in different poses, they made certain cloths held by these figures, or sometimes attached to other things, and in these, they painted whatever they pleased. Occasionally one can make a little figure feigning a cameo or other similar material; small temples and other architectures can also be mixed in. All of these can be made on the ceilings, either colored with paint, or in stucco, or in **chiaroscuro** <note>original: "chiaro e scuro", a technique using light and dark shading to create the illusion of three-dimensional volume.</note>, at the will of the painter. These will be without any reproach, for the good ancients used them so, as evidenced by the antiquities found in Rome, Pozzuoli, and Baiae—where even today some traces are seen. Far more would be seen if the malignant and envious nature of some had not spoiled and destroyed them, so that others might not enjoy that which they themselves possessed in abundance; I wish to remain silent regarding their homeland and names, for they are all too well known among those who have delighted in such things in our times. 

Now, among those who know how to paint in this manner is **Giovanni da Udine**, who has been and still is such an imitator of antiquity in these things, and an inventor in his own right, that he has brought them back to total perfection. Indeed, I dare to say that in some parts he has surpassed antiquity, as is well testified by the loggias above the Pope's secret garden at the Belvedere in Rome, the vineyard of Clement VII at Monte Mario <note>This refers to the Villa Madama, designed by Raphael and decorated by his workshop.</note>, and the beautiful house of the Medici in Florence. These were decorated by him in various places such that, to the honor of all others, he can be called unique rather than merely rare in this profession; furthermore, he is an intelligent architect of excellent judgment, as an ingenious pupil of the divine Raphael.

But if the painter wishes to take pleasure in making some figure at the top of the vaults that represents a living being, it will be necessary for him to be very judicious and highly practiced in perspective. He must be judicious in choosing things appropriate to the location and suitable to the subject—which should be celestial, airy, or winged things rather than earthly ones. He must be practiced in knowing how to **foreshorten** <term>scortiar: the technique of depicting an object or figure in a picture in depth to make it appear as if it is receding or protruding into space.</term> the figures so that, although they appear very short and monstrous in the place where they are painted, nonetheless at their proper distance they are seen to lengthen and represent a proportionate living being. This can be seen observed by Melozzo da Forlì <note>Melozzo da Forlì (c. 1438–1494) was a pioneer of "di sotto in sù" (seen from below) perspective.</note>, a worthy painter of past times, in several places in Italy; among others, in some angels on the vault of the sacristy of Santa Maria at Loreto. Master Andrea Mantegna also, in the castle of Mantua, made some figures and other things seen from below with the art of perspective, accompanied by the discretion of judgment, which certainly represent the truth. Nonetheless, in such subjects, it is difficult to accommodate histories with confused and joined figures; those who make them discreetly separated would perform their duty...

He emphasizes the use of "grotesques" and the technical challenge of foreshortening figures painted on vaults to be viewed from below.</summary>

<meta>catchword: rump</meta>

them; nevertheless, the intelligent painters of our time have avoided such methods, because in truth (as I have said) most of what I am describing proves to be unpleasant to the eyes of the onlookers. And therefore **Raphael of Urbino**, who was among the rarest of the rare in this part of composition and possessed such marvelous judgment that he is considered to have had no equal, let alone a superior—a painter who was always called "divine"—wishing to decorate the vault of a loggia for the aforementioned Agostino Chigi <note>This refers to the Loggia of Cupid and Psyche at the Villa Farnesina in Rome, painted by Raphael and his workshop around 1517–1518.</note>, created pleasing figures at the base of the lunettes, avoiding extreme <term>foreshortenings</term> <note>Original: "scorci" – the technique of depicting an object or figure in a picture in depth to create the illusion of three-dimensional space.</note>, even though he knew and understood them as well as any other. But when he reached the summit of the vault, even though he wished to depict the banquet of the **Gods** (a celestial subject suited to the location), he nevertheless desired to give beauty to the viewer by removing the harshness of so many foreshortenings. He simulated a sky-blue cloth attached to several garlands, like a movable thing, upon which he depicted the aforementioned banquet. He did this with such arrangement and judgment, with such diverse movements and varied coloring, that they represent the truth. The entire work is so well accommodated together that one might judge that loggia to be a temporary decoration for some triumph <note>The author suggests the painting looks like a festive tapestry hung for a specific celebration rather than a permanent wall painting.</note> rather than a permanent painting made on the wall. If this work had not been done with such judgment, but simply painted on the vault in the usual way, one might perceive that all those figures were threatening to fall. Therefore, the Architect—who cannot and must not be without a knowledge of <term>perspective</term>—must not tolerate, as the overseer of all craftsmen, that anything be done in the building where they work without his judgment and counsel.

### CHAPTER XII: ON FLAT WOODEN CEILINGS AND THEIR ORNAMENTS.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'P' featuring a central mask or face surrounded by classical foliate scrollwork.</image-desc>

*Because in many places within buildings it is necessary to make flat ceilings of wood, which have various names: the ancients called them <term>lacunaria</term> <note>Original: "lacunarij" – sunken panels in a ceiling, often referred to today as a coffered ceiling.</note>; now the Romans call them <term>palchi</term>, as they do in Florence; but in Bologna and throughout Romagna they are called <term>tasselli</term>; in Venice and the surrounding areas they call them <term>trauamenti</term> [beam-work] or <term>soffitadi</term> [soffits]. Thus, the diversity of regions creates different names, which it is reasonable to discuss, both for the sake of the woodwork and for the painting. I say, then, that if the ceiling of a hall or other room is of great height, its partitioning should be of solid work, with spacious forms, deep recesses, and high relief, so that due to its distance, it may diminish somewhat and correspond well to the eye from below. Similarly, if one wishes to decorate it with painting, it should consist of solid subjects conforming to such size and distance. This painting should be done in <term>chiaroscuro</term> <note>Original: "chiaro e scuro" – a technique using light and dark tones of a single color to create the illusion of three-dimensional volume.</note> rather than in many colors, because such works have greater strength. All judicious painters who have done such things in noble places in Rome, Florence, Bologna, and especially in this most noble city <note>Likely referring to Venice, where Serlio was living when writing this.</note>—where wooden works are more common than in the rest of Italy—have followed this custom. For this reason, most noble soffits are made in chiaroscuro, and in the center of the forms (whether square or whatever shape you wish), one places a rose or a gilded bowl. If one wishes to add some color to give more beauty to the work, it is tolerable for the backgrounds of the recessed forms to be blue, like something transparent representing the air; however, the roses should be encircled by some foliage or <term>grotesques</term> <note>Original: "grottesche" – a style of decorative art characterized by fanciful human and animal forms interwoven with foliage.</note> so that they do not appear to be suspended in mid-air. Whenever the cornices and other things that encircle the panels or other forms are rich with gold, they will turn out well, or they may truly be made in the same color as the rest of the work. But if, by some accident, the ceiling of a room is of a lower height than reason and art would allow, in this case, the Architect must be clever, using the art of perspective with both judgment and discretion. I say that there is nothing else that gives distance to things made in perspective than the diminishing of one thing after another; therefore, if one wishes to make something close to the eye appear further away, it will be necessary to use art, making the forms further from the center of moderate size, and as they approach the center, making the work finer and smaller. In this way, the object will appear further from our sight than if the works were solid. Similarly, the paintings should be conformable to the woodwork: where there are moderate forms, paintings of moderate size should be made, and as they approach the center, the paintings should also be diminished. Thus, they shall be made such that they can be understood from all directions without straining the sight too much, and the entire work can be grasped at a single glance, consisting of diverse foliage, various types of scrolls, and varied*

<summary>This page discusses Raphael's masterful use of perspective in the Villa Farnesina to avoid visual discomfort and introduces technical guidelines for designing and decorating flat wooden ceilings (coffers) based on their height and regional terminology.</summary>
<keywords>Raphael, Agostino Chigi, Villa Farnesina, perspective, foreshortening, wooden ceilings, lacunaria, coffering, chiaroscuro, architectural decoration</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 70 regarding the decoration of flat wooden ceilings. The previous page ended with the word "comparti-", which is completed here as "compartments." The author, Sebastiano Serlio, is transitioning from the general theory of ceiling design to his specific practical experience in Venice.</meta>

<page-num>LXXI</page-num>
<header>FOURTH BOOK</header>

...ments: of small figures, foliage, and animals mixed together. Above all, in such <term>compartments</term> <note>Original: "compartimenti" – the divided sections or panels of a ceiling or wall.</note> one needs great discretion and fine judgment so as not to place two friezes of the same kind next to each other. Instead, next to a foliage design, one should place a <term>scroll</term> <note>Original: "chiocciola" – literally "snail shell," referring to a volute or spiral ornament.</note> or a <term>knotting</term> <note>Original: "groppamento" – an intricate decorative knot or interlaced pattern.</note>, then a <term>grotesque</term> <note>Original: "grottesca" – a style of decorative painting consisting of fanciful human and animal forms interwoven with foliage, inspired by ancient Roman wall paintings found in "grottoes" (ruins).</note> of figures and animals alongside foliage. In this way, the variety of items will prevent the viewer's sight from becoming confused.

These things of which I speak—if they are done in <term>chiaroscuro</term> <note>Original: "chiaro e scuro" – a method of painting using only light and shadow, often to imitate the look of stone or plaster relief.</note> within their own panels, well-shaded and illuminated—will be more praised by those who understand art than if they were colored. This is because colored works are better suited for vaulted ceilings decorated with grotesques, as we have already stated.

I followed this order in the ceiling of the great and well-stocked library <note>This refers to the Marciana Library (Libreria Marciana) in Venice, designed by Jacopo Sansovino. Serlio is discussing his specific decorative contributions or design principles applied there.</note> in the palace of this illustrious city of **Venice**, during the time of the most serene Prince Messer **Andrea Gritti** <note>Andrea Gritti was the Doge of Venice from 1523 to 1538, a great patron of the arts and architecture.</note>. Because this ceiling was much lower than the width and length of that hall required, I had the work done in a much more delicate and minute style than if the ceiling had been at its proper height, for the reasons mentioned above. Whenever ceilings are lower, the decorative works must be more detailed and small-scale.

An example of this will be seen on the following page, along with many other inventions, various compartments, varied friezes, and other things, mostly taken from antiquity, to enrich with ideas those who might be lacking in them. ✥

# BOOK OF THE ARMS OF NOBLE AND IGNOBLE HOUSES. CHAPTER XIII.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut drop-cap initial 'L' featuring an ornate square frame with scrolling foliate and architectural motifs.</image-desc>

It is also fitting for the **Architect** to have a general knowledge of <term>arms</term> <note>Specifically, heraldic coats of arms</note>, which are part of the ornament of a building. This is so that they are not made incorrectly, and that he knows how to give them their proper place. For if, through this defect, he were to err in the arms of Princes—making it later necessary to remove them or change their location—it would not be without prejudice to the already established architecture and to the honor of the Architect. In ancient times, statues were used and given as a reward for virtue, which denoted the nobility of that house. He who had no statues, being not noble, was called a <note>original: "figliuolo de la terra"</note> **son of the earth**, or born of himself. In place of these statues, **Arms** were later used, which were similarly granted by Captains of armies and by Princes in the same manner as the statues had been. This beautiful institution was later lost, and everyone took it upon themselves to take whatever Arms most pleased them. Over time, this custom has been so approved that one might say it can be done by right—but not so indiscriminately as many do. For it is not lawful for a subject to take the Arms of his Lord as his own, and doing so would merit punishment. A low-born or unknown man cannot and should not use the Arms of a noble house, just as a merchant or artisan could not use the legal and approved mark of another merchant or artisan. The conclusion is this: anyone may take a coat of arms for himself, provided that (as has been said) he does not take that of his Lord—unless it was granted by privilege—nor to the prejudice of the person to whom the Arms belong.

Artisans, peasants, and other low-born people cannot use Arms such as shields and other similar things. Such people must have signs and marks that are different from noble Arms. In every case, one who is not noble must not use the <term>crest</term> <note>An ornament placed above the helmet in a coat of arms, traditionally a sign of knightly rank</note> above his Arms, as it is a sign fitting only for the nobility. The Architect must also know this: if a new coat of arms is to be made, he must not allow it to be "false." When making bars, stripes, or other designs, it is not proper to place <term>metal upon metal</term> or <term>color upon color</term> <note>This is known as the "Rule of Tincture," intended to ensure that the design is clearly visible from a distance</note>. For example, one should not place gold upon silver, nor silver upon gold; nor red upon blue, nor green upon black. If the shield is of metal, that which goes inside must be of color, and vice-versa. 

Birds, fish, and land animals that appear in Arms must be placed according to their nature. A non-aquatic bird is not fitting in water, nor a fish upon a tree or in the air. It is not lawful to place an eagle upon a flower, for this is not its proper place; but it is certainly fitting atop its prey, or with open wings. A land animal in fire would make the Arms "false," because we must always imitate nature as much as we can. Animals in Arms are always placed in their most noble postures and in those that show their vigor. We see the ancients depict Princes in majesty, Popes in pontificals, and Captains armed—each in the habit most fitting to his rank. Fierce animals—such as the Lion, the Bear, the Tiger, the Leopard, and others—should be in a biting or ferocious act suitable to their nature. The Horse should be leaping or running. In every movement of animals, the right foot (as the more noble part) should always go forward. The lamb should be on the ground or walking slowly. By thus conforming to Nature, the Arms will not be "false."

To give colors their proper places in Arms, one must have knowledge of their nobility, for that which precedes must be placed in a more noble spot than the other. The color that most represents light is the most excellent. By **gold**, we represent the Sun, which is the body more luminous than all others; therefore, it must be placed in the most noble spot. By **red**, we represent Fire, the most noble element of all and the most luminous after the Sun. By **blue**, the element of Air; by **white**, that of Water. And so the others, by which the Earth is represented, come after these: such as **green** to represent fertile fields, meadows, spring, and the youth of man. **Black**, which represents darkness, is the lowest and least noble of all, even if it is more powerful than the others for being able to obscure and cancel all colors indiscriminately.

The most noble places in buildings for locating Arms are three: that which is highest toward Heaven, that which is toward the right hand, and that which is in the middle. On a wall, the "right" part is that which faces our left as we look at it; those inside buildings are arranged differently. The Prince's arms must be placed above those of the master of the house toward Heaven as a sign of superiority. Others are honored more if they have the middle place or the right side. Besides providing ornament to the building, Arms provide this utility: they prove that the part where they are placed belongs to the owner of the Arms. With this general knowledge, the Architect will not err in this matter.
